## Part 0 · The Big Picture ◆ **A · THE WORLD**


![A university campus with a half-empty lecture theatre, a library, a coffee queue, bicycles and a poster showing a pass rate.](figures/fig_b13_u01_p00_v01.png){width=6.6in}

*The arts building, Tuesday, 11.05 — Twelve things to find. Five of them are competing for one person's attention.*


# What is attention worth?

**By the end of this unit you can analyse how one interface is designed to hold you, and show
your evidence. One hundred and eighty words.**

| ◆ **A · THE WORLD** | A lecture theatre that is half empty, and a recording that is not |
|---|---|
| ◆ **B · THE SYSTEM** | non-defining relative clauses · *of which* and *of whom* · 23 words for attention and design · 22 connectors and relatives · the pitch reset at the clause break |
| ◆ **C · THE PERFORMANCE** | A hundred and eighty words with the evidence in them |

---

### 0A · Find it

**Find twelve things in this picture.** Five of them are competing for the same person's
attention.

> a lecture theatre half full · a laptop with eleven tabs · a phone face up ·
> a recording light on the ceiling · a screen showing a playlist · a notice about attendance ·
> a library window · a queue for coffee · a bicycle against a rail · a pass rate on a poster ·
> a clock · a lift with a sign on it

### 0B · Guess it

**Two of the twelve exist because of the other ten.** Which, and what is the causal chain?

### 0C · Say it ⏺

**Record forty-five seconds.** *Name one interface you use every day. What is it measuring, and
what does it want you to do next?* You will record it again at the end of this unit.

## Part 1 · Vocabulary Lab 1 ◆ **B · THE SYSTEM**


![A network from a prompt and a notification through autoplay and the feed to dwell time and time on site, with the opt-out shown as a long dashed path.](figures/fig_b13_u01_p01_v08.png){width=6.6in}

*What an interface is actually optimising — Six nodes. Everything on the surface serves the one at the far right.*


### 1A · Meet — the words of a designed surface

Match each word to one place on the map.

> **interface** · **notification** · **engagement** · **retention** · **feed** · **metric** ·
> **dwell** · **prompt** · **autoplay** · **scroll**

### 1B · Sort — what you do, what it does, or what it counts?

Three columns.

> autoplay · engagement · notification · retention · feed · scroll · prompt · metric ·
> dwell

**Two of them are the same event described from opposite sides.** Which pair?

### 1C · Meet — the word that hides a decision

> **by default**

Everything an interface does without being asked is a decision somebody made and did not
announce. **Name three defaults on a device you own**, and say which of the three you have ever
changed.

### 1D · Collocation Lab

Complete each one with one word.

1. The whole thing is engineered to **hold your ______** for eleven more seconds.
2. Autoplay is on **by ______** , and it is the single most effective thing on the page.
3. You can **______ out** , in a submenu, in two taps that nobody finds.
4. **The next ______** starts before you have decided whether you wanted it.
5. **Time on ______** is the metric everything else is built to raise.
6. He has learned to **______ off** at about eleven and it took him two years.

### 1E · The fixed expressions

> **f** **I only came on to check one thing.**
> **f** **It is designed to do that.**

The first sentence is said by a user. The second is said by somebody who has read the
documentation.

> **Write the three sentences between them.**

### 1F · Own it

Think of one application you use. Tell a partner **four sentences**: what it measures, what it
does without asking, what you have turned off, and how long it took you to find the setting.

## Part 2 · Listening ◆ **A · THE WORLD**


![A lecturer and a student union officer in a corridor with a screen of figures, speech bubbles using defining and non-defining relative clauses.](figures/fig_b13_u01_p02_v07.png){width=6.6in}

*Attendance and the pass rate — One of them records the lectures. One of them has the survey nobody commissioned.*


### 2A · Attendance and the pass rate 🔊 Track 1.1

**Before you listen.** Dr Ahmadi records his lectures. Attendance has halved. The pass rate for
students who work has risen. **Guess: what is he going to be asked to do?**

**Listen once. One question.** What is the committee's concern?

**Listen again. Complete the table.**

| | What they have measured | What they conclude | What they have not measured |
|---|---|---|---|
| Dr Ahmadi | | | |
| the committee | | | |
| Bec | | | |

**Listen again. Choose the best answer.**

1. Attendance has fallen from **80 to 40 / 60 to 30 / 45 to 22** per cent.
2. The pass rate for students in paid work has risen by **4 / 11 / 19** points.
3. The recordings are watched **at two in the morning / live / within a week**.
4. The committee's objection is about **learning / the look of the room / money**.

**Noticing.** Four sentences from the recording.

1. *The students who work have done better.*
2. *The students, who work, have done better.*
3. *Forty-one students, most of whom work, have done better.*
4. *Attendance has halved, which nobody disputes.*

**Sentences 1 and 2 differ by two commas and describe different groups.**
**How many students are there in each?**

### 2B · Decoding clinic 🔊 Track 1.2

Thirty seconds of Dr Ahmadi. Four jobs.

1. How many non-defining clauses can you hear?
2. After each pause, does his pitch start higher or lower?
3. Catch the three percentages.
4. He uses one clause that refers back to a whole sentence rather than a noun. Which?

> Transcripts, page 150.

## Part 3 · Grammar Lab 1 ◆ **B · THE SYSTEM**

### Adding a clause without narrowing the noun

### 3A · Notice

Look at the four sentences in Part 2.

1. In sentence 1, how many students are being described? In sentence 2?
2. In sentence 3, what does *most of whom* refer to?
3. In sentence 4, what is the antecedent of *which*? **It is not a noun.**
4. **Try putting *that* into sentence 2.** What happens?
5. Try *"Forty-one students, most of them work, have done better."* **What is wrong?**

### 3B · See


![One sentence shown twice with brackets marking the relative clause, once without commas and once with.](figures/fig_b13_u01_p03_v09.png){width=6.6in}

*Two commas, two populations — The same eight words. One of them is about some students and the other about all of them.*


### 3C · Know

| | Shape | |
|---|---|---|
| **defining** | no commas, *that* allowed | *The students **who work** have done better.* — only those ones |
| **non-defining** | commas, never *that* | *The students, **who work**, have done better.* — all of them, and by the way they work |
| **quantifier + *of which / of whom*** | *most of whom*, *all of which* | *41 students, **most of whom** work* |
| **sentential *which*** | refers to the whole clause | *Attendance has halved, **which** nobody disputes.* |

> **Of whom and of which take a verb of their own.**
> ✗ *41 students, most of them work* — that is two sentences joined by a comma.
> ✓ *41 students, most of whom work* · ✓ *41 students, and most of them work*

> **Sentential *which* is the only relative that can point at a whole clause.**
> It cannot be *what*. ✗ *Attendance has halved, what nobody disputes.*

> **In writing, a non-defining clause needs both commas.** One is an error that changes the
> meaning of everything after it.

> **Why it matters.** An audit sentence with the commas in the wrong place reports a different
> population from the one that was studied, and nobody will notice.

### 3D · Use — controlled

> Forty-one students, most (1) __________ whom are in paid work, completed the unit.
> The recordings, (2) __________ are watched mainly between eleven at night and two in the
> morning, have been available since March. Attendance has fallen by half,
> (3) __________ nobody disputes. The students (4) __________ attend in person are slightly
> younger, (5) __________ is the only demographic difference anybody has found. There are
> eleven lectures, four (6) __________ which were not recorded, and the pass rate for those four
> is the figure (7) __________ the committee has not seen.

### 3E · Use — guided

Join each pair. **Use a non-defining clause, *of whom / of which*, or sentential *which*.**

1. Forty-one students completed the unit. Thirty-one of them work.
2. The recordings have been available since March. They are watched at night.
3. Attendance has halved. Nobody disputes it.
4. There are eleven lectures. Four were not recorded.
5. The pass rate rose eleven points. That is the figure in dispute.

### 3F · Use — open

**In pairs.** Describe a group you belong to in five sentences, each with a non-defining clause.
**Your partner's job** is to catch any sentence where the commas have changed who you mean.

### 3G · Error autopsy


![Two panels comparing most of them work joined by a comma and most of whom work as a relative clause.](figures/fig_b13_u01_p03_v11.png){width=6.6in}

*The comma splice that looks like a relative clause — The comma splice that looks like a relative clause*


**Read both versions. Then say how many students each one is about.**

### 3H · Check — eight items

> 1. Forty-one students, most ______ ______ work, passed.
> 2. The recordings, ______ are watched at night, went up in March.
> 3. Attendance has halved, ______ nobody disputes.
> 4. The students ______ attend in person are younger. *(only those ones)*
> 5. Eleven lectures, four ______ ______ were not recorded.
> 6. Thirty-one students, ______ ______ ______ are in paid work. *(all)*
> 7. ✗ *The students, that work, passed.* → correct it.
> 8. ✗ *41 students, most of them work, passed.* → correct it two ways.

**0–4 →** Workbook pp. 4–7. **5–6 →** Part 6. **7–8 →** page 19.

## Part 4 · Speaking ◆ **C · THE PERFORMANCE**

### 4A · Fluency starter — the added clause

One learner makes a statement. The next adds a non-defining clause. The next adds a
*which* referring to the whole thing. **The chain breaks on a *that*.**

### 4B · The functional core — showing your evidence


![Two panels compared: a half-empty lecture theatre at eleven in the morning and a screen of viewing figures peaking after midnight.](figures/fig_b13_u01_p04_v04.png){width=6.6in}

*The room, and the recording — Learner A has the attendance figures. Learner B has the viewing figures.*


**Language Bank**

| | |
|---|---|
| **the claim** | *The interface is built to…* · *What it optimises for is…* |
| **the evidence** | *You can see this in…* · *The default is…, which means…* |
| **the measurement** | *I counted…* · *Over two weeks, …* |
| **the honest limit** | *I cannot tell whether…* · *That is an inference, not an observation.* |

**Information gap.** Learner A has the attendance figures. Learner B has the viewing figures.
**Find the four lectures that were not recorded, and what happened to them.**

### 4C · The performance — three minutes ⏺

Analyse **one interface**. **Three minutes.** You must include:

> what it measures · one default that you can demonstrate · what you counted, with a number ·
> one thing you are inferring rather than observing

**Record it.**

### 4D · Observer

One person listens for **one thing**: was any inference presented as an observation?

### 4E · Three criteria

> ☐ Did I use three non-defining clauses, none with *that*?
> ☐ Is there a number I collected myself?
> ☐ Did I mark my one inference as an inference?

## Part 5 · Reading ◆ **A · THE WORLD**

### 5A · Predict

An archive document: the minutes of the committee that approved lecture recording, in 2019.
The title is **"Item 9: lecture capture."**
**What do you expect the 2019 committee to have worried about?** Two sentences.

### 5B · Read — two minutes

---

**MINUTES OF THE TEACHING COMMITTEE — 4 MARCH 2019**
**Item 9: lecture capture**

**9.1** The committee considered a proposal to record all lectures and make the recordings
available to enrolled students.

**9.2** Dr Ahmadi, who had piloted recording in two units, reported that attendance in those
units had fallen by about a third and that assessment outcomes had been unchanged.

**9.3** Professor Lindqvist said that she would not support a measure which reduced attendance,
on the grounds that attendance was itself a form of engagement. Asked whether there was evidence
that attendance caused attainment rather than accompanying it, she said that she did not think
the question could be answered.

**9.4** Ms Oyelaran, the student representative, said that of the eleven students she had
consulted, nine were in paid employment, most of whom had at some point missed a lecture for a
shift. She asked the committee to record that none of them had chosen between a lecture and
leisure.

**9.5** The committee approved recording for a two-year trial, subject to review, and agreed
that attendance would be monitored.

**9.6** **ACTION:** Review in March 2021. Attendance data to be collected. Ms Oyelaran asked
whether employment data would also be collected. The chair said this would be difficult.

---

*Archivist's note, added 2025: the review did not take place in 2021. Attendance data has been
collected continuously since 2019. Employment data has never been collected. Recording was made
permanent in 2022 by a decision of the Dean, without reference to the committee.*

---

### 5C · Comprehension — eight questions

1. What did Dr Ahmadi report from his pilot?
2. **What was Professor Lindqvist's objection, and what did she concede when questioned?**
3. What did Ms Oyelaran report, and what did she ask to have recorded?
4. **Why does her last sentence matter?**
5. What was approved, and on what conditions?
6. What two kinds of data were discussed in the action?
7. **What does the archivist's note say happened to each?**
8. What does the note say about the 2022 decision?

### 5D · Vocabulary in context

Guess from the text. Then check.

> piloted · accompanying it · subject to review · would be difficult ·
> without reference to the committee

### 5E · The counter-text


![A university data pack showing attendance, pass rates, viewing figures and the four lectures that were not recorded.](figures/fig_b13_u01_p05_v05.png){width=6.6in}

*Attendance and attainment since 2019 — Six years of one figure, and a footnote on the other one.*


**Read the current attendance and attainment data, and answer.**

1. What has happened to attendance since 2019, and over what period?
2. **What has happened to the pass rate, and what has happened to it for students in paid work?**
3. How many lectures were not recorded, and what happened to attendance at those?
4. What does the data not record?
5. One figure has a footnote. Which, and what does the footnote say?

### 5F · Two texts

The 2019 minutes record a request for **employment data**.
The current pack shows a pass-rate rise **concentrated in one group that cannot be identified**.

**Put those two facts together. What has six years of data collection produced?**
Three sentences.

## Part 6 · Vocabulary Lab 2 ◆ **B · THE SYSTEM**

### Connecting without a comma splice

### 6A · Sort — adds, contrasts, names, or points back?

Four columns.

> namely · likewise · moreover · at which point · conversely · additionally ·
> the latter of which · of which · incidentally · respectively

### 6B · Chunk completion

1. Eleven lectures, four **______ which** were not recorded.
2. Forty-one students, most **______ whom** are in work.
3. The figure rose in March, **at which ______** the trial was extended.
4. Two cohorts, **the latter ______ which** had recordings from week one.
5. The report **refers ______** to a 2019 decision it does not quote.
6. The two tables **cross-______** and neither of them says so.

### 6C · Three that are not interchangeable

**namely · respectively · incidentally**

> *Namely* introduces the list you have just promised. *Respectively* maps two lists onto each
> other in order. *Incidentally* announces that what follows is beside the point — and it
> usually is not.

**Which fits each sentence?**

1. Two groups were compared, ______ those in work and those not.
2. The two groups scored 61 and 72 ______ .
3. ______ , nobody has looked at the four unrecorded lectures.

### 6D · The fixed expression

> **f** **None of which was mentioned.**

A sentence that is only ever written after a list.

> **Write the list that precedes it**, for a meeting you have been in.

### 6E · Contrast Clinic

Eight items. Defining, non-defining, *of which / of whom*, or sentential *which*.

1. Two cohorts. The second had recordings from week one.
2. The pass rate rose. That is the disputed figure.
3. The recordings are watched at night. There are eleven of them.
4. Only the students in work did better.
5. Attendance halved. Nobody disputes this.
6. All the students did better, and all of them are in work.
7. 41 students. 31 of them work.
8. Eleven lectures. Four were not recorded.

## Part 7 · Pronunciation & Fluency Lab ◆ **B · THE SYSTEM**

### The reset after the comma


![Three pitch contours comparing a sentence with a non-defining clause between pauses and the same sentence run together.](figures/fig_b13_u01_p07_v10.png){width=6.6in}

*The reset after the comma — Three units, two pauses, and the pitch starts lower after each.*


### 7A · Hear 🔊 Track 1.3

> *The students,* | *who work,* | *have done better.*
> **Three units. After each pause the pitch resets lower, and the last one carries the verb.**

Now the defining version, run together:

> *The students who work have done better.*

### 7B · Say

Say both. Then say this one with the pauses and without:

> *Forty-one students, most of whom are in paid work, completed the unit.*

### 7C · Make it mean something

Say these two to a partner who is holding up fingers:

> *My lecturers, who record their lectures, are popular.* *(how many lecturers?)*
> *My lecturers who record their lectures are popular.* *(how many?)*

**Did they get it from the pause alone?**

### 7D · Fluency 30 ⏺

Thirty seconds: **three sentences, each with a clause between two pauses.**
Three times, faster, keeping the reset.

## Part 8 · Writing ◆ **C · THE PERFORMANCE**

### How an interface holds you · 180 words


![An interface drawn in four layers from the visible player through the countdown and the recommendation engine to the settings menu.](figures/fig_b13_u01_p08_v02.png){width=6.6in}

*The autoplay stack, in section — Four layers, and each one is a decision somebody made and did not announce.*


### 8A · Read the model

> The autoplay countdown is five seconds, which is shorter than the time it takes most people to
> decide anything. `[the design feature, with its number, and a non-defining clause doing the
> argument]`
>
> I counted my own sessions for two weeks. Of nineteen sessions, fourteen of which I began with a
> specific thing to look for, eleven ended more than twenty minutes after I had found it.
> `[evidence the writer collected, with *of which* holding the qualification inside the sentence]`
>
> The setting that turns it off exists. It is four levels down and it is not in the first screen
> of the search, which I take to be deliberate, though I cannot demonstrate that.
> `[one inference, and it is marked as one]`
>
> None of this is a complaint about users. It is a description of a surface which is optimised
> for time on site and does that extremely well.
> `[the conclusion, which refuses the moral framing the reader expects]`

**Questions about the writer's choices:**

1. The evidence is nineteen sessions the writer counted. **Why is that stronger than a study?**
2. One sentence says *I cannot demonstrate that*. **What does admitting it buy?**
3. The last paragraph refuses to blame users. **Is that an evasion?**

### 8B · The anti-model

> Social media is designed to be addictive. Companies use psychological tricks to keep us hooked
> and we are all spending far too much time staring at screens. We need to take back control of
> our attention before it is too late.

Correct English, and no evidence of any kind. **Name three things that would make it checkable**,
and say what *psychological tricks* is standing in for.

### 8C · Plan

> the feature, with its number → what you counted, with *of which* → one inference, marked →
> the conclusion, without the moral framing

### 8D · Write — 180 words
### 8E · Peer review — three criteria

> ☐ Is there a number the writer collected themselves?
> ☐ Is every inference marked as an inference?
> ☐ Are the non-defining clauses punctuated on both sides?

### 8F · Write it again · 8G · Publish

On the wall, with the 2019 minutes from Part 5B beside it.

## Part 9 · The File ◆ **A · THE WORLD + C · THE PERFORMANCE**

# WORK FILE · The Remote Problem


![A summary of a support call about four failed lecture recordings, marked with each move and what was and was not agreed.](figures/fig_b13_u01_p09_v05.png){width=6.6in}

*The call about the four lectures — Twenty-two minutes, three time zones, and one name obtained at minute nineteen.*


### 9A · A problem nobody is in the room for 🔊 Track 1.4

The recording system has failed on four lectures. The supplier is in another country.
**Listen to the call.**

1. How long before anybody states the problem with a date?
2. **What does the supplier need that nobody on the call has, and who eventually gets it?**
3. What is agreed, and what is not?

### 9B · The four moves on a call you cannot escalate

> stating the problem with dates · establishing what you can and cannot see ·
> asking for a named person · agreeing what happens by when

Match each to one phrase.

> *"Four lectures, the 3rd, the 10th, the 17th and the 24th."* ·
> *"I can see the schedule. I cannot see whether the hardware responded."* ·
> *"Who should I be emailing about this, by name?"* ·
> *"So you will tell me by Thursday whether the files exist."*

### 9C · The sentence that ends a circular call

> *"Let me say back to you what I think we have agreed."*

**Why does it work?** And **what has to follow it** for it to be more than a stalling move?

### 9D · Your turn

In pairs, back to back. **Twelve minutes.** One has a problem; the other works for a supplier
and cannot fix it. **Both must leave with one named person and one date.**

### 9E · Transfer

**Find one unresolved problem you have with an organisation.** Write the four sentences from 9B
for it. Send one of them.

## Part 10 · Mediation & Interaction ◆ **C · THE PERFORMANCE**


![An infographic from 2019 to the present showing attendance, attainment, the data collected and the question that was never asked.](figures/fig_b13_u01_p10_v12.png){width=6.6in}

*Six years of lecture capture — Half the class sees the 2019 minutes. Half sees the data. Rebuild it by speaking.*


### 10A · Relay

Half the class sees the 2019 minutes. Half sees the current data.
**Rebuild six years by speaking.** Twelve minutes.

### 10B · Simplify

Explain the attendance and attainment data from Part 5E to a student who is deciding whether to
come to lectures. **Five sentences only.**

> **Plan:** what has happened to attendance → what has happened to results →
> what the data cannot tell you → which group you might be in → what you should actually decide on

### 10C · Compress

The 2019 minutes into **forty words**, keeping Ms Oyelaran's last sentence.

### 10D · Online

Write **one message** to the teaching committee: you have the attendance data, you have the 2019
action, and you are asking for one specific thing that was agreed in 2019 and never done.

### 10E · Your language

How does your language add a clause to a noun without narrowing it? **Is there a comma, a
pause, a different word, or a different position?** Say *the students, who work, did better* in
both languages.

## Part 11 · The Decision ◆ **C · THE PERFORMANCE**

# Keep recording, or stop

Attendance has halved since 2019. The pass rate overall is unchanged. The pass rate for students
in paid employment has risen eleven points — but the university has never collected employment
data, so that figure comes from a student survey with 41 responses.

- **Keep recording.** The evidence that it helps working students is weak and is the only
  evidence anybody has. The evidence that it harms anybody is weaker.
- **Stop recording.** Attendance returns, the room looks like a university, and the committee
  can stop having this argument. Students in work lose the only accommodation they have.
- **Keep recording and finally collect the employment data.** Two years and a question on
  enrolment. It would settle it, and it would mean admitting that six years of data were the
  wrong data.

### 11A · The evidence


![A line chart of attendance and pass rate over six years, with four weeks of hardware failure showing a sharp attendance spike.](figures/fig_b13_u01_p11_v06.png){width=6.6in}

*Attendance, attainment and the four unrecorded weeks — Six years of one unit, with the four hardware failures marked.*


1. **The 2019 minutes and the current pack** from Part 5 — and the footnote.
2. **The chart** — attendance, attainment and the four unrecorded lectures.
3. **The voice.** Thanh, thirty seconds: *"I work Tuesday and Thursday nights, which are the two
   nights this unit is taught, and which I did not choose. I have never been to a lecture in this
   subject. I got a seventy-eight. I do not know what that proves and I would like somebody to
   stop telling me it proves I should have come."*

### 11B · Talk about it

In fours. Everybody speaks twice. Use the frame.

> *The students, most of whom ______ , have ______ , which ______ .*

**Words you can use:** *engagement · retention · metric · by default · design · attention ·
opt out · dwell*

### 11C · Decide

Write **three sentences**. Choose, say why, and name what it costs.

> I think the university should ______________________ ,
> because ______________________ .
> What this costs is ______________________ .

### 11D · Model decision

> Keep recording and collect the employment data, and say in the minutes that six years of
> attendance figures were collected because they were easy and the figure that mattered was not
> collected because it was difficult. The committee's 2019 chair was right that it would be
> difficult. It was also the only question. An institution that can count a room every week for
> six years can ask one question on an enrolment form.

### 11E · The Reveal

> **What happened.** The question went on the enrolment form in the following year: *do you
> undertake paid work during term? If so, how many hours?* It is optional and about seventy per
> cent answer it.

> In the first full year the data showed that students working more than fifteen hours a week had
> a pass rate four points **below** the cohort, not eleven points above. The eleven-point figure
> had come from a survey answered mostly by students who were doing well, which is what surveys
> do.
>
> Recording was kept. The argument for it changed completely: it is now defended as a reasonable
> adjustment for a group that is doing worse, rather than as something that was working.

**They got the number. It said the opposite of what everybody had been arguing. And the
recordings stayed.**

> **Was it the right decision?** Say why — and say what the first eleven-point figure was
> actually measuring.

## Part 12 · Landing ◆ **all three lines**

### 12A · Recycle — twelve items

> 1. Forty-one students, most ______ ______ work, passed.
> 2. The recordings, ______ are watched at night, went up in March.
> 3. Attendance has halved, ______ nobody disputes.
> 4. Autoplay is on **by ______** .
> 5. You can **______ out** , in a submenu.
> 6. **Time on ______** is the metric everything else serves.
> 7. He has learned to **______ off** at about eleven.
> 8. Eleven lectures, four ______ ______ were not recorded.
> 9. *(B1.2 U8)* ______ patients waited 41 days. *(this group)*
> 10. *(B1.2 U9)* The majority of patients ______ seen in six weeks. *(verb)*
> 11. *(A2.2 U8)* The hall, ______ was built in 1884, is the oldest.
> 12. *(A2.1 U4)* The person ______ has the key is the manager.

### 12B · Consolidate

**One hundred and eighty words.** One interface, analysed. A number you collected, three
non-defining clauses, one *of which*, and one inference marked as an inference.

### 12C · Can-do, with evidence

| I can… | My evidence | Sure? |
|---|---|---|
| add a clause to a noun without narrowing it | Part 3H score | ◯ ◑ ● |
| analyse an interface in three minutes, with evidence | Part 4C recording | ◯ ◑ ● |
| write 180 words in which inferences are marked | Part 8 draft 2 | ◯ ◑ ● |
| get a name and a date out of a remote supplier | Part 9D role play | ◯ ◑ ● |

### 12D · Replay ⏺

Find your forty-five seconds from Part 0C. Listen. **Record it again.**
**Two sentences:** what is different, and what you now know that interface is counting.

### 12E · Glossary — forty-five items, as a map

> **THE SURFACE** · interface · feed · scroll · prompt · autoplay · notification · design · a design choice
> **WHAT IT COUNTS** · metric · engagement · retention · dwell · time on site · attention · hold your attention
> **WHAT IT DOES WITHOUT ASKING** · by default · the next video · opt out
> **WHAT YOU DO** · log off · switch off · zone out · put down · I only came on to check one thing · it is designed to do that
> **ADDING A CLAUSE** · which · whom · whose · where · of which · of whom · most of which · all of whom · the former of which · the latter of which · at which point
> **CONNECTING** · likewise · moreover · namely · incidentally · respectively · additionally · conversely · refer back · cross-refer · none of which was mentioned

## Part 0 · The Big Picture ◆ **A · THE WORLD**


![A campus with a ramped building and a stepped building side by side, a lift with a notice on it, a worn path across grass, a propped fire door and a bell marked ring for assistance.](figures/fig_b13_u02_p00_v01.png){width=6.6in}

*Two buildings, one budget, Thursday, 09.40 — Twelve things to find. Four are the official provision. Four are what people actually do.*


# Who is left out?

**By the end of this unit you can audit a space or a process for who it excludes, and report
what you find.**

| ◆ **A · THE WORLD** | A campus that works for almost everybody, and the budget for one lift |
|---|---|
| ◆ **B · THE SYSTEM** | participle clauses, present and past · reduced relatives · 23 words for access and exclusion · 22 participle and linking forms · the rhythm of a reduced clause |
| ◆ **C · THE PERFORMANCE** | An audit, written so that somebody can act on it |

> a bell with a sign saying ring for assistance · a desk with nobody at it ·
> a lecture theatre with one flat row · a bike left across a kerb cut ·
> a map on a post · an accessible toilet being used as a store

**Before you listen.** Bec has walked both buildings with a measuring tape. There is money for

## Part 3 · Grammar Lab 1 ◆ **B · THE SYSTEM**

### Clauses with the subject taken out

### 3A · Notice

Look at the four sentences in Part 2.

1. In sentence 1, who walked the routes?
2. In sentence 3, who took the route? **And who is the subject of the main clause?**
3. **Rewrite sentence 4 in full.** What word did the reduction remove?
4. *Having walked* and *walking*. **What does the perfect participle add?**
5. Try *"Lacking a ramp, the route is impossible."* **Who lacks the ramp?**

### 3B · See


![One sentence shown with brackets marking the participle clause and arrows to the subject of the main clause, correct in one version and absurd in the other.](figures/fig_b13_u02_p03_v09.png){width=6.6in}

*Whose subject is the participle borrowing? — The same opening clause, twice. Once it attaches to a building and once to a route.*


### 3C · Know

| | Shape | Replaces |
|---|---|---|
| **present participle** | *Lacking a ramp, B has no route.* | *Because it lacks…* |
| **perfect participle** | *Having walked both, I would choose B.* | *After I had walked…* |
| **past participle** | *Taken together, the figures show…* | *If they are taken…* |
| **reduced relative** | *the route marked on the map* | *the route **which is** marked* |
| ***given* / *considering*** | *Given the numbers…* | prepositional, and no subject needed |

> **The hanging participle.** A participle clause takes its subject from the **main clause**.
> If they do not match, the sentence says something absurd.
> ✗ *Lacking a ramp, the route is impossible.* — the route does not lack a ramp.
> ✓ *Lacking a ramp, the building has no route.*

> ***Given*, *considering*, *owing to* and *due to* are not participles any more.** They have
> become prepositions and do not need to agree with anything, which is why they are safe.

> **A reduced relative drops *which is* / *who are*.**
> *the route **which is** marked* → *the route marked* ·
> *the students **who are** waiting* → *the students waiting*

> **Why it matters.** An audit written in participle clauses is half the length and twice as
> likely to attach a finding to the wrong thing.

### 3D · Use — controlled

> (1) __________ (walk) both routes with a tape, I would put the lift in B.
> (2) __________ the numbers, A is the obvious choice: it takes 2,100 people a week against
> B's 400. The route (3) __________ (mark) on the campus map, (4) __________ (take) by
> almost nobody, adds four hundred metres. (5) __________ (lack) any step-free entrance at
> all, B excludes people completely rather than inconveniencing them.
> (6) __________ (take) together, the two facts do not point the same way, and
> (7) __________ said that, one of them is about numbers and the other is about whether a
> person can enter a building.

### 3E · Use — guided

Reduce each one to a participle clause. **Two of them cannot be reduced safely — say why.**

1. Because B lacks a ramp, it has no step-free route.
2. After I had measured both, I changed my mind.
3. The route which is marked on the map is not the one people use.
4. Because the lift was out of order, the lecture was moved.
5. If the figures are taken together, they do not agree.

### 3F · Use — open

**In pairs.** Describe a building you both know, in six sentences, four of which must begin with
a participle clause. **Your partner's job** is to catch any hanging participle.

### 3G · Error autopsy


![Two panels comparing lacking a ramp the route is impossible with lacking a ramp the building has no route.](figures/fig_b13_u02_p03_v11.png){width=6.6in}

*The hanging participle, and what it actually claims — The hanging participle, and what it actually claims*


**Read both versions. Then say what the first one claims about the route.**

### 3H · Check — eight items

> 1. ______ (walk) both routes, I would choose B.
> 2. ______ the numbers, A is the obvious choice.
> 3. The route ______ (mark) on the map adds 400 m.
> 4. ______ (lack) a ramp, the building has no route at all.
> 5. ______ (take) together, the figures disagree.
> 6. ✗ *Lacking a ramp, the route is impossible.* → correct it.
> 7. ______ (have) said that, the numbers are not the only question.
> 8. ______ to the lift being out, the class was moved. *(preposition)*

## Part 4 · Speaking ◆ **C · THE PERFORMANCE**

### 4A · Fluency starter — reduce it

One learner gives a two-clause sentence. The next reduces it to a participle clause.
The next says whether the subjects match. **The chain breaks on a hanging participle.**

### 4B · The functional core — reporting an audit


![Two panels compared: a printed access statement listing step-free entrances and an audit sheet with measurements and a photograph of a plant room.](figures/fig_b13_u02_p04_v04.png){width=6.6in}

*The access statement, and the tape measure — Learner A has the statement. Learner B has the audit. Four claims do not survive.*


**Language Bank**

| | |
|---|---|
| **the method** | *Walking both routes with a tape…* · *Over two weeks I counted…* |
| **the finding** | *Lacking a ramp, B…* · *The route marked on the map…* |
| **the exclusion, named** | *A person using a wheelchair cannot…* · *At step four, they stop.* |
| **the honest limit** | *I did not test…* · *Given the circumstances, this is an estimate.* |

**Information gap.** Learner A has the official access statement. Learner B has the audit.
**Find the four claims in the statement that the audit contradicts.**

### 4C · The performance — three minutes ⏺

Report **an audit of a space or a process**. **Three minutes.** You must include:

> your method, with a number · who is excluded, named by what they cannot do ·
> the exact step at which they stop · one thing you did not test

**Record it.**

### 4D · Observer

One person listens for **one thing**: was the exclusion described as a property of the building
or of the person?

### 4E · Three criteria

> ☐ Did I use four participle clauses, none of them hanging?
> ☐ Is the exclusion located at a specific step?
> ☐ Did I say what I did not test?

## Part 5 · Reading ◆ **A · THE WORLD**

### 5A · Predict

Two letters. The title is **"Re: the lift in the Menzies building."**
**What do you expect the second letter to concede?** Two sentences.

### 5B · Read — two minutes and a quarter

---

**From: Bec Tran, Student Union**
**To: the Director of Estates**
**Re: the lift in the Menzies building**

I have walked both buildings with a tape measure and I am going to argue against our own
submission.

We asked for the lift in Menzies because Menzies is busier. It takes about 2,100 people a week
against Harcourt's 400. On every measure of volume, Menzies wins, and that is the case we made.

Menzies has a ramp. It is a bad ramp — it is 1:11 where it should be 1:14, and it ends at a door
that is heavier than the standard allows — but a person using a wheelchair can get into Menzies,
tired and annoyed.

Harcourt has six steps and no ramp and no lift. There is no route. The access statement describes
a step-free entrance at the rear; there is a rear entrance, it is step-free, and it opens into a
plant room.

So the honest sentence is this: in Menzies we would be improving access for people who have it
badly. In Harcourt we would be creating it for people who do not have it at all.

I am aware that 400 is a smaller number than 2,100 and I am not going to pretend otherwise.

---

**From: the Director of Estates**
**Re: your letter**

You are right about Harcourt and I did not know about the plant room, which I have now seen.

I want to put one thing on the record, not as a counter-argument but because it will come up.
There is a reason the budget goes to the busier building and it is not laziness: lifts are
funded from a capital line that is assessed on usage, and a lift serving 400 people a week will
score below the threshold and will be queried every year for fifteen years by somebody who has
not read your letter.

I am going to fund Harcourt. I am also going to write the paragraph you have just written into
the capital submission, because in four years I will not remember it and neither will anybody
else.

---

### 5C · Comprehension — eight questions

1. What does Bec say she is going to do in her first sentence?
2. **What are the two volume figures, and what case did they originally support?**
3. What is wrong with the Menzies ramp? Give both faults.
4. **What does the access statement claim about Harcourt, and what is actually there?**
5. What is Bec's "honest sentence"?
6. What does the Director concede, and what does he say he did not know?
7. **What is the funding problem he puts on the record, and why does he raise it?**
8. What two things does he say he is going to do?

### 5D · Vocabulary in context

Guess from the text. Then check.

> argue against our own submission · on every measure of volume · the standard allows ·
> a capital line · will be queried

### 5E · The counter-text


![A university access statement beside an audit sheet of measurements, with the contradicted claims marked and one row noted as untested.](figures/fig_b13_u02_p05_v05.png){width=6.6in}

*The access statement and the audit sheet — Two documents about the same two buildings, printed eleven years apart.*


**Read the access statement and the audit sheet, and answer.**

1. What does the access statement say about each of the two buildings?
2. **Which four of its claims does the audit contradict, and how?**
3. What is the gradient of the Menzies ramp, and what should it be?
4. How long has the Harcourt rear entrance been described as step-free?
5. One row of the audit has a note about something the auditor did not test. Which?

### 5F · Two texts

The access statement says Harcourt has a **step-free rear entrance**.
The audit says that entrance **opens into a plant room**.

**Put those two facts together. Is the statement false?** Three sentences.

## Part 6 · Vocabulary Lab 2 ◆ **B · THE SYSTEM**

### Reduced clauses and the words that replace them

### 6A · Sort — present participle, past participle, or preposition?

Three columns.

> given · due · based · followed · taken · lacking · surrounded · facing · having ·
> considering · seen

### 6B · Chunk completion

1. **Having ______ that** , four hundred is still four hundred.
2. **Given the ______** , the estimate is as good as it is going to get.
3. **______ to** the lift being out, two classes were moved.
4. The figures, **______ together** , do not point the same way.
5. **All things ______** , Harcourt is the stronger case.
6. The audit does not **______ for** the days the lift was out.

### 6C · Three that are not participles any more

**given · considering · due to**

> All three began as participles and have become prepositions. That is why they cannot hang:
> they are not looking for a subject.

**Rewrite each sentence as a full clause, and say what is lost.**

1. Given the numbers, A is the obvious choice.
2. Considering the gradient, the ramp is unusable in the wet.
3. Due to the lift being out, the class was moved.

### 6D · Two that need a preposition to finish

Some past participles will not stand alone in front of a noun. They need a small word
behind them, and the small word is not optional.

> **c** The estimate is **based on** the 2022 survey.
> **c** Each claim in the audit is **followed by** a photograph.

**Not:** *~~The estimate is based the 2022 survey.~~* · *~~Each claim is followed a photograph.~~*

**Complete each one with the right half of the pair.**

1. The exemption was granted on a statement ______ ______ one visit in 2019.
2. The site visit was ______ ______ a letter nobody answered.
3. A figure ______ ______ a single measurement is a figure worth checking.

### 6E · The fixed expression

> **f** **That being the case.**

A formal connector that is itself a participle clause.

> **Write the sentence before it and the sentence after it**, in a letter you might have to send.

### 6F · Contrast Clinic

Eight items. Reduce each one, or say why it cannot be reduced.

1. Because the lift was out, the class was moved. *(watch the subject)*
2. Since nobody asked the students, the statement went unchallenged.
3. Because I had walked it, I knew the map was wrong.
4. The students who are waiting at the rear door cannot get in.
5. If the figures are taken together, they disagree.
6. After she had measured both, she changed her mind.
7. The route which is marked on the map adds 400 m.
8. Because it lacks a ramp, the building excludes people.

## Part 7 · Pronunciation & Fluency Lab ◆ **B · THE SYSTEM**

### The rhythm of a clause with no subject


![Four phrases marked for syllable stress, comparing a participle clause with its full clause equivalent.](figures/fig_b13_u02_p07_v10.png){width=6.6in}

*A reduced clause keeps the beats and drops the syllables — Three stresses before the pause, whichever version you say.*


### 7A · Hear 🔊 Track 2.3

> *Having walked both routes,* | *I would choose B.*
> **Three stresses in the first unit, three in the second, and a clear pause between them.**

Now the full version:

> *After I had walked both routes, I would choose B.*
> **Same number of stresses. Four more syllables, all of them weak.**

### 7B · Say

Say both. Then say this one three times, faster:

> *Lacking a ramp and lacking a lift, the building has no route at all.*

### 7C · Make it mean something

Say these two:

> *Having said that, four hundred is still four hundred.* *(a concession is coming)*
> *Having said that four hundred is still four hundred…* *(no pause — and it is a report)*

**One pause, and the sentence changes job.** Which is which?

### 7D · Fluency 30 ⏺

Thirty seconds: **four sentences beginning with a participle clause.**
Three times, faster, keeping the pause.

## Part 8 · Writing ◆ **C · THE PERFORMANCE**

### An access audit · 180 words


![A cutaway of a rear entrance showing a step-free threshold leading into a plant room, with the database field that still describes it as an entrance.](figures/fig_b13_u02_p08_v02.png){width=6.6in}

*The Harcourt rear entrance, in section — Four layers. Each one was true when it was written.*


### 8A · Read the model

> Walking both routes with a tape, I measured the Menzies ramp at 1:11 against a standard of
> 1:14, and the entrance door at 44 newtons against a maximum of 30.
> `[the method and two measurements, both against a published standard]`
>
> Harcourt has six steps, no ramp and no lift. The rear entrance, described in the access
> statement as step-free, opens into a plant room. `[the finding, with a reduced relative carrying
> the contradiction inside the sentence]`
>
> A person using a wheelchair can enter Menzies, with difficulty. They stop at step one in
> Harcourt and there is no further step to describe.
> `[the exclusion, located exactly, and the second sentence is the whole argument]`
>
> I did not test the fire evacuation route in either building, lacking the authority to open the
> doors. `[what was not tested, and why — and the participle agrees]`

**Questions about the writer's choices:**

1. Both measurements are given against a standard. **What would a measurement alone be worth?**
2. The exclusion is *at step one*. **Why does locating it matter more than describing it?**
3. The last paragraph names something untested. **Does that weaken the audit?**

### 8B · The anti-model

> Accessibility across the campus is variable and some buildings present more challenges than
> others. Students with mobility needs may find certain routes difficult. The university is
> committed to improving access and welcomes feedback from all members of the community.

Correct English, and nobody could act on it. **Name three things that are missing**,
and say what *present more challenges* is doing.

### 8C · Plan

> the method, with a number → the finding, against a standard → the exclusion, located at a step
> → what you did not test, and why

### 8D · Write — 180 words
### 8E · Peer review — three criteria

> ☐ Is every measurement given against a standard?
> ☐ Is the exclusion located at a specific step?
> ☐ Does every participle clause agree with its main subject?

### 8F · Write it again · 8G · Publish

On the wall, with the access statement from Part 5E beside it.

## Part 9 · The File ◆ **A · THE WORLD + C · THE PERFORMANCE**

# STUDY FILE · The Question


![A record of nine seminar questions sorted into clarification, extension, challenge and display, with the length of each answer.](figures/fig_b13_u02_p09_v05.png){width=6.6in}

*Nine minutes, nine questions — A seminar on research design. One question took four of the nine minutes.*


### 9A · Nine minutes of questions 🔊 Track 2.4

A seminar on research design. The tutor takes nine questions. **Listen.**

1. Which question gets the longest answer, and is it the best question?
2. **Which question does the tutor say she cannot answer, and what does she do instead?**
3. One question is asked in a way that makes it impossible to answer. What is wrong with it?

### 9B · Four kinds of question

> **the clarification** — I did not follow something
> **the extension** — I followed it and want more
> **the challenge** — I followed it and I am not convinced
> **the display** — I want you to know that I know something

**Which of the four is hardest to ask, and which is easiest to disguise as another?**

### 9C · Asking a question you are afraid of

Thanh writes better than he speaks. In nine weeks he has asked one question.

**Which of these would you advise him to use, and why?**

1. *"Sorry, could you say that again?"*
2. *"Can I check I've understood: are you saying that…?"*
3. *"I don't follow the step from the second graph to the conclusion."*
4. *"This might be a stupid question, but…"*

### 9D · Your turn

In fours. **Twelve minutes.** One person presents anything for two minutes.
**Everybody else must ask one question of each of the four kinds**, in writing first, then aloud.
Then: which was hardest to write?

### 9E · Transfer

**Count the questions asked in your next class or meeting**, and sort them into the four kinds.
Bring the four numbers.

Half the class sees the access statement. Half sees the audit.
**Rebuild both buildings by speaking.** Twelve minutes.

Explain the audit from Part 5E to a student who is about to be timetabled into Harcourt.

## Part 11 · The Decision ◆ **C · THE PERFORMANCE**

# The busier building, or the one with no route

There is money for one lift. Menzies takes 2,100 people a week and has a bad ramp. Harcourt
takes 400 and has nothing.

- **Menzies.** The volume case, the capital-line case, and about sixty people a week who
  currently enter by a 1:11 ramp and a door they cannot open alone.
- **Harcourt.** Four hundred people a week, of whom perhaps eight cannot enter at all, and a
  building that will be queried every year for fifteen years as under-used.
- **Neither yet.** Fix the Menzies ramp and the door for a fifth of the cost, and bid again for
  Harcourt next year with the audit attached.

### 11A · The evidence


![A grouped bar chart comparing Menzies, Harcourt and the ramp-only option by users per week, people excluded and cost.](figures/fig_b13_u02_p11_v06.png){width=6.6in}

*Users, exclusions and cost for each option — Three options, and the two columns the capital line reads are not the same column.*


1. **The access statement and the audit** from Part 5E — and the untested row.
2. **The chart** — users, exclusions and cost for each option.
3. **The voice.** A student, thirty seconds: *"I have been told four times that Harcourt has a
   step-free entrance. I have been to it. It is a step-free entrance into a plant room. I would
   like somebody to explain how a sentence like that survives for eleven years, because I do not
   think anybody lied."*

### 11B · Talk about it

In fours. Everybody speaks twice. Use the frame.

> *______ (participle clause), the building ______ . That being the case, ______ .*

**Words you can use:** *access · barrier · provision · step-free · reasonable adjustment ·
the only route · shut out · work around · on paper*

### 11C · Decide

Write **three sentences**. Choose, say why, and name what it costs.

> I think the university should ______________________ ,
> because ______________________ .
> What this costs is ______________________ .

### 11D · Model decision

> Fund Harcourt, and fix the Menzies ramp from maintenance rather than capital, which is where a
> gradient fault belongs anyway. The real problem in this decision is that one of the two options
> is counted and the other is not: 2,100 is a number the capital line understands and *eight
> people who cannot enter a building* is not. Writing the audit into the submission is the only
> part of this that will still be working in four years.

### 11E · The Reveal

> **What happened.** Harcourt got the lift. The Menzies ramp was regraded from the maintenance
> budget in the same year, at about a fifth of the cost, which nobody had previously suggested
> because the two budgets are held by different people who meet twice a year.
>
> The access statement was corrected. It took eleven months, because the statement is generated
> from a database field, and the field had been correct in 2014 when the rear entrance was still
> an entrance.
>
> The Harcourt lift has been queried in each of the three capital reviews since. The Director's
> paragraph has been read out at two of them.

**The lift went in. The ramp was a maintenance job all along. And a sentence stayed wrong for
eleven years because the room behind it changed and the field did not.**

> **Was it the right decision?** Say why — and say what should happen to the database field.

---

> 1. ______ (walk) both routes, I would choose B.
> 2. ______ the numbers, A is the obvious choice.
> 3. The route ______ (mark) on the map adds 400 m.
> 4. ______ (lack) a ramp, the building has no route.
> 5. There is a **step-______** route, and it adds 400 m.

> 8. She has learned to **work ______** it.
> 9. *(Unit 1)* Forty-one students, most ______ ______ work, passed.
> 10. *(Unit 1)* Attendance has halved, ______ nobody disputes.
> 11. *(B1.2 U6)* She ______ (have) it done on Thursday.
> 12. *(A2.2 U8)* The hall, ______ was built in 1884, is the oldest.

**One hundred and eighty words.** An audit of a space or a process. Your method with a number,
a measurement against a standard, an exclusion located at a step, and one thing you did not test.

| I can… | My evidence | Sure? |
|---|---|---|
| reduce a clause without leaving the participle hanging | Part 3H score | ◯ ◑ ● |
| report an audit in three minutes | Part 4C recording | ◯ ◑ ● |

> **THE BUILDING** · access · barrier · ramp · lift · step · signage · step-free · the only route · out of order
> **THE POLICY** · provision · accommodation · exemption · survey · inclusion · reasonable adjustment · on paper · at short notice
> **THE PERSON** · disability · shut out · work around · get by · point out
> **WHAT PEOPLE SAY** · nobody asked us · it has been like that for years
> **REDUCED CLAUSES** · having · lacking · facing · seen · taken · left · based on · followed by · surrounded · having said that · taken together
> **PREPOSITIONS NOW** · given · considering · due to · given the circumstances · all things considered · that being the case · allow for · rule out

## Part 0 · The Big Picture ◆ **A · THE WORLD**


![A humanities building with a seminar room, a clock at 11.52, a half-raised hand, a propped door and a tutor with a tally sheet.](figures/fig_b13_u03_p00_v01.png){width=6.6in}

*Seminar room 2.14, week nine, 11.52 — Twelve things to find. Four belong to the person speaking. Four belong to the people who are not.*


# Is disagreement useful?

**By the end of this unit you can write and speak an argument that concedes properly before it
disagrees.**

| ◆ **A · THE WORLD** | A seminar room where one person is right more often than anybody else |
|---|---|
| ◆ **B · THE SYSTEM** | contrast and concession: *although · despite · however · whereas* · 24 words for argument and dissent · 21 concession forms · the intonation of a concession |
| ◆ **C · THE PERFORMANCE** | An argument that gives the other side its best version first |

---

### 0A · Find it

**Find twelve things in this picture.** Four of them belong to the person speaking. Four belong
to the people who are not.

> a hand half-raised and lowered again · a clock at 11.52 · a laptop with nothing typed since
> 11.20 · a seminar table with two empty chairs on one side · a whiteboard with one word on it ·
> a reading nobody finished · a phone face down · a tutor's tally sheet · a door propped open ·
> a coffee gone cold · a person leaning back out of the circle · a notebook with a question
> written and crossed out

### 0B · Guess it

**Two of the twelve are the same person's silence, shown twice.** Which, and what is the
difference between the two silences?

### 0C · Say it ⏺

**Record forty-five seconds.** *Describe a conversation where somebody was right and you stopped
arguing anyway. Say why you stopped.* You will record it again at the end of this unit.

## Part 1 · Vocabulary Lab 1 ◆ **B · THE SYSTEM**


![A network from a position and a premise through an objection and a rebuttal to common ground, with shout down shown as a shortcut that skips the rebuttal.](figures/fig_b13_u03_p01_v08.png){width=6.6in}

*What a disagreement is made of — Six nodes. Two of them are the same move made at different volumes.*


### 1A · Meet — the words of a disagreement

> **argument** · **debate** · **dissent** · **objection** · **position** · **premise** ·
> **rebuttal** · **compromise** · **agreement**

1. Which two of these can be a thing you *hold* rather than a thing you *make*?
2. **A *rebuttal* answers something. What?**
3. *Dissent* and *objection*: which one is usually about a decision, and which about a direction?
4. **What is the difference between a *premise* and a *position*?**

> **An *argument* in this unit is not a row.** It is a set of reasons arranged so that somebody
> else can follow them, and the ordinary English word carries both meanings without warning you.

### 1B · Sort — the room, the argument, or the person?

> play devil's advocate · defer · take a position · dominate · common ground ·
> concede the point · silence · the floor · talk over

**Three columns. Two of them belong in more than one.** Say which and why.

### 1C · Meet — the phrase that does most of the work

> **c** **common ground**

Not agreement. The part of the ground you are both standing on *before* the disagreement starts.

1. **Why is naming it first a stronger move than naming it last?**
2. Find the common ground between: *we should ban cars from campus* and *we should build a
   second car park*.

### 1D · Collocation Lab

| | |
|---|---|
| **take** | a **position** · a view · the point |
| **concede** | the **point** · a round · ground |
| **the floor** | hold ~ · give up ~ · take ~ |
| **common ground** | find ~ · establish ~ · lose ~ |

**Which of these can you do to somebody, and which only with them?**

### 1E · The fixed expressions

> **f** **I take your point.** — I have understood it and I am about to disagree.
> **f** **That is not what I said.** — the sentence that stops a misquotation, and it has to be
> said calmly or it sounds like the thing it is denying.

**Where would each one fall in a four-turn exchange?**

### 1F · Own it

The four phrasal verbs of a disagreement.

> **back down** · **hold back** · **shout down** · **come round**

1. Which two are about volume, and which two about time?
2. **Who does *shout down* describe — the loud one, or the room?**
3. *Hold back* has two meanings here. Give both.
4. Write one sentence in which somebody does **come round** without ever saying they were wrong.

## Part 2 · Listening ◆ **A · THE WORLD**


![A student speaking across a seminar table and a tutor listening with a tally sheet, their speech shown as concession clauses.](figures/fig_b13_u03_p02_v07.png){width=6.6in}

*The seminar, minute thirty-one — One of them has read everything. One of them has the tally sheet.*


### 2A · Fifty minutes, one voice 🔊 Track 3.1

**Before you listen.** Priya tutors a first-year seminar and marks participation.
**Guess: how many of the eleven students speak in a fifty-minute class?**

**Listen once. One question.** What is the seminar about, and who decides?

**Listen again. Complete the table.**

| | Minutes spoken | Times interrupted | Times they interrupted |
|---|---|---|---|
| Marcus | | | |
| Thanh | | | |
| the rest | | | |

**Listen again. Choose the best answer.**

1. Marcus is **hard to say / usually wrong / usually right**.
2. Priya intervenes **not at all / once / twice**.
3. Thanh **speaks once / speaks twice / does not speak**.
4. The seminar reaches **neither / a compromise / a conclusion**.

**Noticing.** Four sentences from the recording.

1. *Although he is right, he is right at everyone else's expense.*
2. *Despite the quality of what he says, nobody else has said anything.*
3. *He is right. However, so was the person he talked over.*
4. *Marcus argues; Thanh writes. Whereas one of those is graded, the other is not.*

**All four concede something before they disagree. Which of the four concedes the most?**

### 2B · Decoding clinic 🔊 Track 3.2

Thirty seconds of Priya. Four jobs.

1. How many concession words can you count?
2. In *although he is right*, which word carries the stress?
3. Catch the two numbers.
4. She says one thing twice with different intonation. What changes?

> Transcripts, page 152.

## Part 3 · Grammar Lab 1 ◆ **B · THE SYSTEM**

### Conceding before you disagree

### 3A · Notice

Look at the four sentences in Part 2.

1. In sentence 1, which half is the concession and which is the point?
2. **In sentence 2, what follows *despite* — a clause or a noun phrase?**
3. **Rewrite sentence 3 as one sentence** using *although*. What punctuation changes?
4. Sentence 4 uses *whereas*. **Could you use *although* instead? What would be lost?**
5. Try *"Although he is right, but he is loud."* **What is wrong with it?**

### 3B · See


![One sentence with brackets marking the although clause as the concession and the main clause as the point that is kept.](figures/fig_b13_u03_p03_v09.png){width=6.6in}

*Which half survives the sentence? — A concession clause is the half the writer is prepared to lose.*


### 3C · Know

| | Followed by | Example |
|---|---|---|
| ***although* / *though*** | a clause | *Although he is right, nobody else spoke.* |
| ***despite* / *in spite of*** | a noun or *-ing* | *Despite being right, he lost the room.* |
| ***however* / *nevertheless***| a new sentence | *He is right. However, so was Thanh.* |
| ***whereas*** | a clause, for a parallel | *Marcus argues, whereas Thanh writes.* |
| ***yet* / *still*** | a clause, joined | *He is right, yet nobody agrees.* |

> **One concession word per sentence.** English does not allow *although… but…* or
> *despite… however…*. The second one is the one to delete.
> ✗ *Although he is right, but he is loud.* ✓ *Although he is right, he is loud.*

> ***Though* goes where *although* goes, and in one more place: the end.**
> *He is right. He is loud, though.* — informal, and extremely common in speech.

> ***Whereas* is not a concession at all.** It sets two things side by side without saying
> either is a problem. *Although* says the first one is in the way of the second.

> **Why it matters.** An argument that concedes nothing reads as if it has not understood the
> other side. An argument that concedes in the wrong clause gives away the thing it meant to keep.

### 3D · Use — controlled

> (1) __________ Marcus is right more often than anybody in the room, the room has stopped
> talking. (2) __________ his accuracy, the seminar has eleven people in it and two of them
> have spoken since week three. He prepares, he reads, and he is not rude.
> (3) __________ , an argument is not only a set of true statements. Priya marks participation,
> (4) __________ the department marks essays, and the two numbers have stopped agreeing.
> (5) __________ of the grade, Thanh has not spoken since week two. He is right too, on paper,
> (6) __________ nobody in the room has heard him be right.
> (7) __________ , somebody has to decide whether that is a problem with Marcus or with the
> format.

### 3E · Use — guided

Join each pair with the word in brackets. **Two of them need a change of word class.**

1. He is right. Nobody else has spoken. *(although)*
2. The quality of his argument. The room has gone quiet. *(despite)*
3. Marcus speaks for nineteen minutes. Thanh speaks for forty seconds. *(whereas)*
4. She knows it is a problem. She has not intervened. *(yet)*
5. He prepares properly. He takes the whole hour. *(in spite of)*

### 3F · Use — open

**In pairs.** Defend a position you do not hold, in six sentences, three of which must concede
something real before they disagree. **Your partner's job** is to catch any concession that is
not a real one.

### 3G · Error autopsy


![Two panels comparing although he is right but he is loud with although he is right he is loud.](figures/fig_b13_u03_p03_v11.png){width=6.6in}

*Although and but, doing the same job twice — Although and but, doing the same job twice*


**Read both versions. Then say which clause the writer actually meant to keep.**

### 3H · Check — eight items

> 1. ______ he is right, nobody else has spoken. *(clause)*
> 2. ______ being right, he lost the room. *(-ing)*
> 3. He is right. ______ , so was Thanh. *(new sentence)*
> 4. Marcus argues, ______ Thanh writes. *(parallel)*
> 5. ______ of the grade, Thanh has not spoken. *(noun phrase)*
> 6. ✗ *Although he is right, but he is loud.* → correct it.
> 7. He is right, ______ nobody agrees. *(joined clause)*
> 8. He is loud, ______ . *(end position, informal)*

**0–4 →** Workbook pp. 14–17. **5–6 →** Part 6. **7–8 →** page 63.

## Part 4 · Speaking ◆ **C · THE PERFORMANCE**

### 4A · Fluency starter — concede it

One learner states a position. The next must say the strongest thing they can **for** it, then
disagree. **The chain breaks on a concession that is not real.**

### 4B · The functional core — disagreeing well


![Two panels compared: a bar of airtime dominated by one speaker and a list of the ideas the seminar actually produced.](figures/fig_b13_u03_p04_v04.png){width=6.6in}

*Fifty minutes, two ways of counting them — Learner A has who spoke. Learner B has what was said.*


**Language Bank**

| | |
|---|---|
| **the real concession** | *To be fair, …* · *Admittedly, …* · *Granted, …* · *Up to a point, yes.* |
| **the limit of it** | *That said, …* · *Even so, …* · *All the same, …* · *And yet …* |
| **the point** | *The thing it does not account for is…* · *On the other hand, …* |
| **the repair** | *I take your point.* · *That is not what I said.* |

**Information gap.** Learner A has Marcus's three best arguments. Learner B has the three
objections nobody got to make. **Find the one place where they agree completely.**

### 4C · The performance — three minutes ⏺

Argue **a position you do not hold**. **Three minutes.** You must include:

> the strongest version of the other side, stated first · one real concession ·
> the exact point where you stop agreeing · what would change your mind

**Record it.**

### 4D · Observer

One person listens for **one thing**: was the concession a real one, or a sentence shaped like
one?

### 4E · Three criteria

> ☐ Did I state the other side at its strongest before I disagreed?
> ☐ Is there exactly one concession word per sentence?
> ☐ Did I name what would change my mind?

---

## Part 5 · Reading ◆ **A · THE WORLD**

### 5A · Predict

A report extract, titled **"Participation: a note on measurement."**
**What do you expect it to recommend?** Two sentences.

### 5B · Read — two minutes and a quarter

---

**FACULTY OF ARTS · TEACHING QUALITY REVIEW · EXTRACT 4 OF 11**
**Participation: a note on measurement**

Participation is worth fifteen per cent of the mark in sixty-one of our units. In fifty-eight of
those, the published criterion is some version of *contributes regularly to seminar discussion*.
We asked eleven tutors what they record. Nine record nothing during the class and reconstruct a
figure in week twelve.

We then recorded airtime in nine seminars. The distribution is not unusual and it is not
defensible. In a seminar of eleven students over fifty minutes, the top speaker took a median of
17 minutes and the bottom four took a median of 40 seconds between them. The top speaker was
the same person in all nine weeks in seven of the nine groups.

Admittedly, airtime is a crude measure and nobody has proposed grading it directly. Still, it
is the only measure we have of a thing that carries fifteen per cent of a mark, and it says that
in most of our seminars the grade is being distributed on a quantity that four students in
eleven have almost no access to.

The obvious objection is that the quiet students could speak and choose not to. That is true up
to a point. It is also true that in every group we observed, the gap between the top speaker and
the rest widened between week three and week nine, which is not what a free choice usually does.

We are not recommending that tutors intervene. Intervention in a seminar is a blunt instrument
and the tutors who do it most are not the ones with the best participation spread. We are
recommending that the criterion be rewritten, because *contributes regularly to seminar
discussion* is a description of a behaviour and what we say we are assessing is a capacity.

One tutor, asked what she would do, said: "I would mark the question they ask, not the number of
times they open their mouth. And yet I know exactly who would lose by that too."

---

### 5C · Comprehension — eight questions

1. How many units carry a participation mark, and what is it worth?
2. **What do nine of the eleven tutors actually record, and when?**
3. Give the two airtime medians.
4. **What does the report concede about airtime as a measure, and what does it say anyway?**
5. What is "the obvious objection", and how is it answered?
6. **What happens between week three and week nine, and why is that significant?**
7. Why does the report *not* recommend intervention?
8. What does the last tutor say she would do, and what does she immediately admit?

### 5D · Vocabulary in context

Guess from the text. Then check.

> not defensible · a crude measure · a blunt instrument · the participation spread ·
> a capacity rather than a behaviour

### 5E · The counter-text


![A seminar transcript extract beside an airtime tally sheet, with interruptions marked and one row left blank by the tutor.](figures/fig_b13_u03_p05_v05.png){width=6.6in}

*The transcript and the airtime sheet — Fifty minutes of seminar 2.14, week nine, recorded for the review.*


**Read the seminar transcript extract and the airtime sheet, and answer.**

1. How long does each of the four named students speak?
2. **Where does Marcus interrupt, and where is he interrupted?**
3. One student speaks once, for eleven seconds. What does she say, and what happens next?
4. Find the one moment where somebody concedes the point properly.
5. The sheet has a row the tutor left blank. Which, and why might she have?

### 5F · Two texts

The report says the gap **widens** between week three and week nine.
The transcript shows one student being talked over **twice** in week nine.

**Do the two facts explain each other?** Three sentences.

## Part 6 · Vocabulary Lab 2 ◆ **B · THE SYSTEM**

### The words that buy you the right to disagree

### 6A · Sort — how much does each one concede?

Three columns: **a lot · a little · none at all**.

> even so · and yet · nevertheless · admittedly · still · granted · all the same ·
> up to a point · nonetheless · that said · to be fair · on the other hand

### 6B · Chunk completion

1. **______ be fair** , he had read the whole thing and nobody else had.
2. **Up to a ______** , yes — and then it stops being true.
3. **That ______** , the room has eleven people in it.
4. **Even ______** , somebody has to go first.
5. **All the ______** , I would rather be right than quiet.
6. **On the ______ hand** , the quiet students are not being stopped.

### 6C · Two that are almost the same

**nevertheless · nonetheless**

> Interchangeable in almost every context. *Nonetheless* is very slightly more formal and
> slightly more common in writing. If you can only learn one, learn *nevertheless*.

**Use each one once, in a sentence about the report in Part 5.**

### 6D · Two verbs that concede

> **p** **allow that** · **p** **grant that**

> *I allow that he prepares properly.* · *I grant that the measure is crude.*

Both are formal, both take a clause, and both are the written equivalent of *fair enough*.

**Rewrite three sentences from Part 5B using one of them.**

### 6E · The fixed expression

> **f** **And yet.**

It can be a whole sentence. Say the strongest case for something, stop, and write it.

> **Write the sentence before it and the sentence after it.**

### 6F · Contrast Clinic

Eight items. **Four are wrong. Correct them and say what the rule is.**

1. He is right, however nobody agrees.
2. In spite of being right, he lost the room.
3. Marcus speaks a lot, whereas Thanh is quiet.
4. Nevertheless of the evidence, she did nothing.
5. Although he is right, but nobody agrees.
6. Though, he is right.
7. He is loud. He is right, though.
8. Despite he is right, nobody agrees.

## Part 7 · Pronunciation & Fluency Lab ◆ **B · THE SYSTEM**

### The intonation of a concession


![Three pitch contours comparing admittedly with a rise, admittedly flat, and though in end position.](figures/fig_b13_u03_p07_v10.png){width=6.6in}

*The rise that promises a disagreement — Three versions. Only one of them means something is coming.*


### 7A · Hear 🔊 Track 3.3

> *Admittedly,* ↗ | *he is right.* ↘
> **The rise on *admittedly* is a promise. Something is coming.**

Now the same words with no rise:

> *Admittedly he is right.* ↘
> **Nothing is coming. It is just an agreement, and it sounds like giving up.**

### 7B · Say

Say both. Then say this one three times, faster:

> *Granted, up to a point, and even so — no.*

### 7C · Make it mean something

Say these two:

> *He is right, though.* *(and that is the end of it)*
> *He is right, though…* *(and something is coming)*

**One word, two jobs.** Which is which?

### 7D · Fluency 30 ⏺

Thirty seconds: **four concessions, each with the rise, each followed by a disagreement.**
Three times, faster, keeping the rise.

## Part 8 · Writing ◆ **C · THE PERFORMANCE**

### An argument that concedes · 180 words


![A cutaway of a written argument in four layers from the opposing case through the concession and the turn to the falsifier.](figures/fig_b13_u03_p08_v02.png){width=6.6in}

*An argument that concedes, in section — Four layers. The top one is the other side, written by you, at its best.*


### 8A · Read the model

> Marcus has read every text and he is right more often than I am. That is not in dispute and
> I am not going to pretend it is. `[the other side at its strongest, in the writer's own voice]`
>
> Admittedly, nobody is stopping the rest of us. The door is not locked and there is no rule
> about who speaks. `[the concession, and it is a real one — it costs the writer something]`
>
> Even so, the gap between him and the rest widened every week from week three, and a gap that
> widens is not a gap people are choosing. `[the turn, with the evidence inside the sentence]`
>
> What would change my mind is a group where the gap stayed flat. I have not seen one, and I
> have been in four. `[the falsifier — what evidence would move the writer]`

**Questions about the writer's choices:**

1. The first paragraph gives Marcus his best case. **What does the writer buy with it?**
2. The concession in paragraph two is true. **Why is it not enough on its own?**
3. The last paragraph names what would change the writer's mind. **What would an argument
   without that line be?**

### 8B · The anti-model

> While it is of course true that all students should feel able to contribute, it is also the
> case that some students are naturally more vocal than others. There are arguments on both
> sides and the issue is a complex one that deserves further consideration by all stakeholders.

Correct English, and it concedes nothing because it claims nothing. **Name the three moves it
is using to avoid taking a position.**

### 8C · Plan

> the other side at its strongest → a real concession → the turn, with evidence →
> what would change your mind

### 8D · Write — 180 words
### 8E · Peer review — three criteria

> ☐ Is the other side stated at its strongest, before any disagreement?
> ☐ Does the concession cost the writer something?
> ☐ Is there a falsifier — something that would change the writer's mind?

### 8F · Write it again · 8G · Publish

On the wall, beside the one it is arguing with.

## Part 9 · The File ◆ **A · THE WORLD + C · THE PERFORMANCE**

# CULTURE FILE · Time, Lateness and Waiting


![A record of five people arriving four minutes after a meeting started, with what each one was measuring lateness against.](figures/fig_b13_u03_p09_v05.png){width=6.6in}

*Four minutes late, five explanations — The same four minutes, measured against four different things.*


### 9A · Four minutes late 🔊 Track 3.4

Five people describe arriving four minutes after a meeting started. **Listen.**

1. Which two think they are on time?
2. **Which one apologises, and what does the apology tell you about the room, not the person?**
3. One of them was waiting outside for six minutes before going in. Why?

### 9B · What lateness is measured against

> **the clock** — the meeting starts at eleven
> **the room** — the meeting starts when the people who matter are in it
> **the task** — the meeting starts when there is something to decide
> **the host** — the meeting starts when the host says so

**Which of the four is your default? Which was your parents'?**

### 9C · Waiting is not neutral

Josie has worked nights for nine years. She has been in nine o'clock meetings after a shift
that ended at seven.

**Who in an organisation does the waiting, and is it the same people who set the time?**

### 9D · Your turn

In fours. **Twelve minutes.** Each person describes a time they waited for somebody and what
they decided it meant. Then: **were you right?**

### 9E · Transfer

**Time the first four minutes of your next class or meeting.** What happens in them, and who
is not there for it? Bring one sentence.

## Part 10 · Mediation & Interaction ◆ **C · THE PERFORMANCE**


![An infographic across twelve weeks showing airtime, the number of students speaking, the format change in week ten and the committee deadline.](figures/fig_b13_u03_p10_v12.png){width=6.6in}

*One seminar, twelve weeks — Half the class sees the report. Half sees the transcript. Rebuild the term by speaking.*


### 10A · Relay

Half the class sees the report. Half sees the transcript.
**Rebuild the seminar by speaking.** Twelve minutes.

### 10B · Simplify

Explain the airtime finding to a first-year who has just been told they will be graded on
participation. **Five sentences only.**

> **Plan:** what is measured → what it actually records → what happened over nine weeks →
> what it means for them → what they can do on Tuesday

### 10C · Compress

The report extract into **forty words**, keeping the concession.

### 10D · Online

Write **one message** to Priya: you agree the format is the problem, you do not think Marcus
should be silenced, and you are proposing one specific change to one seminar.

### 10E · Your language

Does your language concede with a word, or with a tone? **Say *admittedly, he is right, but* in
both languages.** Which needs more words, and which can do it with intonation alone?
