## Part 11 · The Decision ◆ **C · THE PERFORMANCE**

# The best voice in the room, or the ten who are not using theirs

Marcus speaks for seventeen of fifty minutes and is usually right. Four students have spoken
for forty seconds in total across nine weeks. Priya has to mark all eleven on participation.

- **Intervene.** Cap contributions, go round the table, direct a question by name. It works
  immediately and it takes something from the person who prepared.
- **Let it run.** Mark what is said, not how often. Marcus earns his mark and the four get
  what the format gave them.
- **Change the format.** Written first, spoken second — everyone writes two minutes, then reads
  theirs aloud. Nobody is capped and nobody starts from nothing.

### 11A · The evidence


![A bar chart of minutes spoken and essay mark for eleven students, showing no relationship between the two.](figures/fig_b13_u03_p11_v06.png){width=6.6in}

*Airtime and essay marks, all eleven students — The two numbers the seminar produces, side by side.*


1. **The report and the transcript** from Part 5 — and the row the tutor left blank.
2. **The chart** — airtime and essay marks for all eleven students.
3. **The voice.** Thanh, thirty seconds: *"I have the answer about forty seconds after the
   moment has gone. I am not nervous and I am not unprepared. I am slow in the way that writing
   is slow, and the seminar is the one part of this degree that is not."*

### 11B · Talk about it

In fours. Everybody speaks twice. Use the frame.

> *______ (concession), ______ . And yet ______ .*

**Words you can use:** *argument · dissent · objection · position · premise · compromise ·
common ground · dominate · defer · the floor*

### 11C · Decide

Write **three sentences**. Choose, say why, and name what it costs.

> I think Priya should ______________________ ,
> because ______________________ .
> What this costs is ______________________ .

### 11D · Model decision

> Change the format. Two minutes of writing before any discussion, then round the table once,
> reading what you wrote. It is the only one of the three options that does not require Priya to
> make a judgement about a person in front of the room, and the only one that gives the slow
> students the thing they are actually short of, which is not courage but time. Marcus loses
> four minutes of the hour. He will not notice.

### 11E · The Reveal

> **What happened.** Priya changed the format in week ten. In the two remaining seminars, the
> number of students who spoke went from four to nine and from four to eleven. The median
> contribution was shorter and, on her own marking, better.
>
> Marcus's airtime fell from seventeen minutes to six. He came round to it in the second week,
> having spent the first one visibly annoyed, and said afterwards that he had not known most of
> the group had read the text.
>
> The faculty did not adopt it. The criterion still says *contributes regularly to seminar
> discussion*, because changing it requires a committee that meets twice a year and the paper
> missed the November meeting by four days.

**The format change worked in two weeks. The sentence that caused the problem is still there,
and it will be there next year, because of a deadline nobody argued about.**

> **Was it the right decision?** Say why — and say what should happen to the criterion.

### 12A · Recycle — twelve items

> 1. ______ he is right, nobody else has spoken.
> 2. ______ being right, he lost the room.
> 3. He is right. ______ , so was Thanh.
> 4. Marcus argues, ______ Thanh writes.
> 5. ______ of the grade, Thanh has not spoken.
> 6. He is right, ______ nobody agrees.
> 7. He is loud, ______ . *(end position)*
> 8. I ______ your point.
> 9. That is not what I ______ .
> 10. She finally ______ round.
> 11. He will not ______ down.
> 12. We found some common ______ .

### 12B · Consolidate

**Three columns, from memory:** concession words taking a clause · concession words taking a
noun · concession words starting a new sentence.

### 12C · Can-do, with evidence

> ☐ I can state the other side at its strongest before disagreeing. **Evidence:** 4C, 8D
> ☐ I can use *although*, *despite*, *however* and *whereas* without doubling up. **Evidence:** 3H, 6F
> ☐ I can hear and produce the rise that marks a concession. **Evidence:** 7D
> ☐ I can name what would change my mind. **Evidence:** 8D, 11C

Record 0C again. **Listen to both.** What have you got that you did not have in week one?

### 12E · Glossary — forty-five items, as a map

> **THE ARGUMENT** · argument · debate · dissent · objection · position · premise · rebuttal · compromise · agreement
> **THE ROOM** · dominate · defer · silence · the floor · talk over · shout down · hold back
> **THE MOVES** · take a position · concede the point · play devil's advocate · common ground · back down · come round
> **WHAT PEOPLE SAY** · I take your point · that is not what I said
> **CONCEDING, ONE CLAUSE** · although · though · whereas · yet · still · and yet · nevertheless · nonetheless
> **CONCEDING, A NOUN** · despite · in spite of
> **CONCEDING, A SENTENCE** · however · admittedly · granted · even so · all the same · that said · up to a point · to be fair · on the other hand
> **CONCEDING, A VERB** · allow that · grant that

> **English Animated** · *Making the Case* · a university campus, Melbourne

## Part 0 · The Big Picture ◆ **A · THE WORLD**


![A campus garden with forty metre-square beds, a greenhouse, a compost bay built from pallets, a water butt and a shed with two padlocks.](figures/fig_b13_u04_p00_v01.png){width=6.6in}

*The teaching garden, March, 08.20 — Twelve things to find. Four exist for teaching. Four exist for food.*


# Why is it done this way?

**By the end of this unit you can explain a design decision and the reasoning behind it.**

| ◆ **A · THE WORLD** | A garden that two departments both believe they own, for two different reasons |
|---|---|
| ◆ **B · THE SYSTEM** | purpose, reason and result: *so that · in order to · such … that* · 24 words for process, design and intention · 21 causal forms · stress on the purpose clause |
| ◆ **C · THE PERFORMANCE** | An explanation that gives the reason, not only the rule |

---

### 0A · Find it

**Find twelve things in this picture.** Four of them exist for teaching. Four exist for food.

> a bed divided into twelve small squares · a bed of one crop, forty metres long ·
> a sign with twelve latin names on it · a water butt with a tap at the wrong height ·
> a compost bay built from pallets · a path worn where there is no path ·
> a greenhouse with the door open · a tray of seedlings labelled by hand ·
> a stack of crates by a gate · a bench facing the wrong way · a hose running to nowhere ·
> a shed with two padlocks on it

### 0B · Guess it

**One of the twelve was built for one purpose and is used for the other.** Which, and how
can you tell?

### 0C · Say it ⏺

**Record forty-five seconds.** *Describe something near you that was designed for one job and
is used for another. Say what was lost in the swap.* You will record it again at the end of
this unit.

## Part 1 · Vocabulary Lab 1 ◆ **B · THE SYSTEM**


![A network from a design brief through requirements and constraints to a prototype and a trade-off, with use in practice as an unplanned path.](figures/fig_b13_u04_p01_v08.png){width=6.6in}

*From a brief to a thing that exists — Six nodes. The one at the bottom is the one nobody writes down.*


### 1A · Meet — the words of a design decision

> **purpose** · **intention** · **rationale** · **specification** · **requirement** ·
> **constraint** · **trade-off** · **prototype**

1. Which two of these are written down before anything is built?
2. **What is the difference between a *purpose* and a *rationale*?**
3. *Constraint* and *requirement*: which one says what it must do, and which what it cannot?
4. **Why is a *trade-off* not a failure?**

> **A *prototype* is not a small version of the thing.** It is a question asked in physical
> form, built to be wrong, and the whole value of it is how fast you find out.

### 1B · Sort — the garden, the document, or the decision?

> yield · a design brief · plot · curriculum · in practice · the whole point · by design ·
> at the expense of · harvest

**Three columns. Two of them belong in more than one.** Say which and why.

### 1C · Meet — the phrase that does most of the work

> **c** **a trade-off**

Two things you want, and a quantity of one that can only be bought with the other.

1. **Name the trade-off in a bus timetable.**
2. Why is *there is no trade-off here* almost always a sign that somebody has not measured
   one of the two sides?

### 1D · Collocation Lab

| | |
|---|---|
| **serve** | a **purpose** · a function · a need |
| **by design** | not by accident · ~ rather than by habit |
| **the whole point** | ~ of it · miss ~ |
| **at the expense of** | gained ~ · built ~ |

**Which two of these are usually said about somebody else's decision?**

### 1E · The fixed expressions

> **f** **That was never the idea.** — said when a thing is being judged against a purpose it
> never had.
> **f** **It does one of them well.** — the most useful sentence in this unit, and the hardest
> to say about your own work.

**Which one is a defence and which is a concession?**

### 1F · Own it

The four phrasal verbs of making something.

> **set out to** · **end up** · **scale down** · **build in**

1. Which two describe the gap between the plan and the result?
2. **What do you *build in* that you cannot add later?**
3. *Scale down* and *make smaller*: what does *scale down* add?
4. Write one sentence in which somebody **set out to** do one thing and **end up** doing another.

## Part 2 · Listening ◆ **A · THE WORLD**


![A garden manager and a head chef talking by a garden gate, their speech shown as purpose and result clauses.](figures/fig_b13_u04_p02_v07.png){width=6.6in}

*At the gate, Tuesday morning — One of them runs the curriculum. One of them runs a kitchen.*


### 2A · Two beds, two reasons 🔊 Track 4.1

**Before you listen.** The garden has been run by the School of Agriculture for six years. The
campus kitchen has started taking from it. **Guess: what is the argument about?**

**Listen once. One question.** What does each side want the garden to produce?

**Listen again. Complete the table.**

| | Teaching garden | Food garden |
|---|---|---|
| bed size | | |
| number of crops | | |
| kilos per year | | |

**Listen again. Choose the best answer.**

1. The twelve small squares exist **for teaching / for both / for yield**.
2. The kitchen wants **the same crops / fewer crops / more crops**.
3. The two uses are **compatible / incompatible / compatible at a small scale**.
4. Somebody has already **scaled down / changed nothing / scaled up**.

**Noticing.** Four sentences from the recording.

1. *The beds are small so that twenty students can each have one.*
2. *We grow twelve crops in order to show twelve growing cycles.*
3. *The yield is so low that the kitchen gets about four days of salad a year.*
4. *It was built as a classroom, and owing to that, it feeds nobody.*

**All four give a reason. Which of the four gives a result instead of a purpose?**

### 2B · Decoding clinic 🔊 Track 4.2

Thirty seconds of the garden manager. Four jobs.

1. How many purpose clauses can you count?
2. In *so that twenty students can each have one*, which word is stressed?
3. Catch the two quantities.
4. She says *so* twice, meaning two different things. Which two?

> Transcripts, page 153.

## Part 3 · Grammar Lab 1 ◆ **B · THE SYSTEM**

### Purpose, reason and result

### 3A · Notice

Look at the four sentences in Part 2.

1. In sentence 1, does *so that* give a purpose or a result?
2. **In sentence 3, does *so … that* give a purpose or a result?** How do you know?
3. **Rewrite sentence 2 with *so as to*.** What changes, and what cannot change?
4. Sentence 4 uses *owing to*. **Rewrite it with *because*.** Which is more formal?
5. Try *"The beds are small in order to students can have one."* **What is wrong with it?**

### 3B · See


![A branching diagram separating purpose forms from reason forms and result forms, each with an example.](figures/fig_b13_u04_p03_v09.png){width=6.6in}

*Purpose looks forward. Result looks back. — So that is the one word that does both, and the verb after it tells you which.*


### 3C · Know

| | Takes | Example |
|---|---|---|
| ***so that*** | a clause, usually with *can / will / would* | *The beds are small so that each student has one.* |
| ***in order to* / *so as to*** | an infinitive, same subject | *We grow twelve crops in order to show twelve cycles.* |
| ***so … that* / *such … that*** | adjective / noun + result | *The yield is so low that nobody notices.* |
| ***because* / *since* / *as*** | a clause, giving a reason | *Since the beds are small, the yield is low.* |
| ***owing to* / *due to*** | a noun phrase | *Owing to the bed size, the yield is low.* |

> **Purpose looks forward. Result looks back.** *So that* is the one that can do either, and
> the verb tells you which: *so that students can learn* is a purpose, *so that nobody noticed*
> is a result.

> ***In order to* needs the same subject in both halves.**
> ✗ *The beds are small in order to students can have one.*
> ✓ *The beds are small so that students can have one.*
> ✓ *We made the beds small in order to give each student one.*

> ***so* + adjective, *such* + noun.**
> *so low that…* · *such a low yield that…* — the article moves, and that is the whole
> difference.

> **Why it matters.** An explanation that gives the rule and not the reason cannot be argued
> with, improved, or applied anywhere else.

### 3D · Use — controlled

> The twelve squares were laid out (1) __________ each student could manage a plot alone.
> Twelve crops were chosen (2) __________ demonstrate twelve different growing cycles within one
> academic year. The result is a garden with (3) __________ a small bed for each crop
> (4) __________ no crop reaches the quantity a kitchen can use. The total harvest last year
> was (5) __________ small (6) __________ the campus kitchen recorded it as four days of salad.
> (7) __________ to the curriculum, the beds cannot be merged, and that is not an oversight.

### 3E · Use — guided

Rewrite each one with the structure in brackets. **Two of them need a new subject.**

1. The beds are small. Each student can have one. *(so that)*
2. We planted twelve crops. We wanted to show twelve cycles. *(in order to)*
3. The yield is very low. The kitchen hardly notices it. *(so … that)*
4. The yield is very low. The kitchen hardly notices it. *(such … that)*
5. The curriculum requires twelve crops. The beds cannot be merged. *(owing to)*

### 3F · Use — open

**In pairs.** Explain a design decision in something you both use, in six sentences, three of
which must give a purpose and one a result. **Your partner's job** is to ask *why* once more
than you have an answer for.

### 3G · Error autopsy


![Two panels comparing in order to students can have one with so that students can have one.](figures/fig_b13_u04_p03_v11.png){width=6.6in}

*In order to, and the subject it cannot change — In order to, and the subject it cannot change*


**Read both versions. Then say which one could appear in a design brief.**

### 3H · Check — eight items

> 1. The beds are small ______ each student can have one. *(purpose, clause)*
> 2. We planted twelve crops ______ show twelve cycles. *(purpose, infinitive)*
> 3. The yield is ______ low ______ nobody notices. *(result, adjective)*
> 4. It is ______ a low yield ______ nobody notices. *(result, noun)*
> 5. ______ to the curriculum, the beds cannot be merged. *(noun phrase)*
> 6. ✗ *The beds are small in order to students can have one.* → correct it.
> 7. We built it ______ as to test one thing at a time. *(purpose, infinitive)*
> 8. The beds are small; ______ , the yield is low. *(result, formal)*

**0–4 →** Workbook pp. 18–21. **5–6 →** Part 6. **7–8 →** page 85.

## Part 4 · Speaking ◆ **C · THE PERFORMANCE**

### 4A · Fluency starter — one more why

One learner states a design decision. The next asks *why*. **Five levels, and the chain breaks
when somebody says *that is just how it is done*.**

### 4B · The functional core — explaining a decision


![Two panels compared: a grid of forty metre-square beds and a field of eight long merged plots, with yield figures.](figures/fig_b13_u04_p04_v04.png){width=6.6in}

*Forty small beds, or eight merged ones — Learner A has the design brief. Learner B has last year's harvest.*


**Language Bank**

| | |
|---|---|
| **the purpose** | *It is there so that…* · *with a view to…* · *for the sake of…* |
| **the constraint** | *We had to work with…* · *The specification said…* |
| **the trade-off** | *…at the expense of…* · *We gained X and lost Y.* |
| **the result** | *The result being…* · *which is why it is there* · *and that led to…* |

**Information gap.** Learner A has the original design brief. Learner B has last year's harvest
figures. **Find the one requirement that was met exactly and still produced the wrong outcome.**

### 4C · The performance — three minutes ⏺

Explain **a design decision**, in anything. **Three minutes.** You must include:

> what it was for · what constrained it · the trade-off, named as a trade-off ·
> one thing you would do differently and why

**Record it.**

### 4D · Observer

One person listens for **one thing**: was a reason given, or only a rule restated?

### 4E · Three criteria

> ☐ Did I give the purpose before the rule?
> ☐ Did I name a trade-off, with both sides of it?
> ☐ Could somebody apply my reasoning to a different problem?

---

## Part 5 · Reading ◆ **A · THE WORLD**

### 5A · Predict

An explainer, titled **"Why the beds are a metre square."**
**What do you expect the last paragraph to concede?** Two sentences.

### 5B · Read — two minutes and a quarter

---

**SCHOOL OF AGRICULTURE · TEACHING GARDEN · NOTE FOR NEW STAFF**
**Why the beds are a metre square**

Every bed in the teaching garden is one metre by one metre. New staff ask about this roughly
once a semester, usually in the form of a suggestion that we merge them.

The beds are a metre square so that one student can reach the centre of a bed from any edge
without stepping on the soil. That is the whole rationale, and it is not about yield. A student
who steps on a bed compacts it, and a compacted bed teaches the wrong lesson for the rest of the
year because everything planted in it does slightly worse for a reason the student cannot see.

Twelve crops are grown in order to show twelve growing cycles inside one academic year. Three
of the twelve are chosen specifically because they fail reliably in Melbourne. A student who has
only grown things that work has not learned to read a plant.

The consequence of both decisions is a yield so low that it is not worth measuring. Last year
the garden produced 94 kilograms of food. The campus kitchen uses that in about four days. We
know this and it has never been a problem, because the garden was never specified as a food
source.

It has become one. The kitchen started taking from the garden two years ago, without a decision
being made anywhere, in the way that these things start. It is now on a menu board. The kitchen
has asked whether we could grow "more of the things people actually eat", which is a reasonable
question with an unreasonable answer: we could, and the garden would stop doing what it was
built for within one season.

The honest position is this. At the scale of a teaching garden the two purposes are compatible
because the food is incidental. At any scale the kitchen would find useful, they are not. It
does one of them well.

---

### 5C · Comprehension — eight questions

1. What question do new staff ask, and in what form?
2. **Why are the beds a metre square? Give the reason, not the rule.**
3. What does a compacted bed teach, and why is it hard to see?
4. **Why are three of the twelve crops chosen because they fail?**
5. What was the yield last year, and how long does the kitchen use that in?
6. **How did the kitchen start taking from the garden?**
7. What is "a reasonable question with an unreasonable answer"?
8. What exactly is the honest position in the last paragraph?

### 5D · Vocabulary in context

Guess from the text. Then check.

> compacts it · reliably · incidental · never specified as · within one season

### 5E · The counter-text


![A garden design brief listing five requirements beside a harvest record showing yield per bed and one crop that failed.](figures/fig_b13_u04_p05_v05.png){width=6.6in}

*The design brief and the harvest sheet — Written six years apart, by people who never met.*


**Read the design brief and the harvest figures, and answer.**

1. What were the five original requirements?
2. **Which requirement was met exactly, and what did meeting it cost?**
3. What is the yield per square metre, and how does it compare with a commercial bed?
4. Which crop failed, and was that a failure?
5. One line of the brief has been added by hand, later. Which, and by whom?

### 5F · Two texts

The brief says the garden must **demonstrate twelve growing cycles**.
The kitchen wants **more of the things people actually eat**.

**Is there a version of the garden that does both?** Three sentences.

## Part 6 · Vocabulary Lab 2 ◆ **B · THE SYSTEM**

### The words that carry a reason

### 6A · Sort — purpose, reason, or result?

Three columns.

> consequently · so as to · so that · such that · therefore · whereby · because ·
> owing to · as · hence · in order to · thus · since

### 6B · Chunk completion

1. **With a ______ to** merging them next year, we measured every bed.
2. **For the ______ of** the argument, assume the kitchen is right.
3. **The result ______** , nobody measures the harvest at all.
4. **So ______ to** test one variable, we planted three beds identically.
5. **Such ______** no crop reaches a usable quantity.
6. **In ______ to** show twelve cycles, we need twelve crops.

### 6C · Three that mean *therefore*

**therefore · thus · hence**

> All three are formal and all three are results. *Thus* is the most literary, *hence* the most
> compressed — it can take a noun phrase alone: *hence the twelve beds*.

**Write one sentence with each, about the garden.**

### 6D · The one that joins a rule to its reason

> **c** **whereby**

> *A system whereby each student manages one bed.*

Formal, and it introduces the mechanism rather than the purpose. **Rewrite two sentences from
Part 5B using it.**

### 6E · The fixed expression

> **f** **Which is why it is there.**

The sentence that closes an explanation. It only works if what came before it was a reason.

> **Write the two sentences before it.**

### 6F · Contrast Clinic

Eight items. **Four are wrong. Correct them and say what the rule is.**

1. It is such a low yield that nobody notices.
2. The beds cannot be merged; hence the twelve crops.
3. Owing to the yield is low, the kitchen looked elsewhere.
4. The yield is such low that nobody notices.
5. The beds are small so that students can reach the middle.
6. Owing to the low yield, the kitchen looked elsewhere.
7. We planted twelve crops so as to showing twelve cycles.
8. The beds are small in order to students can reach the middle.

## Part 7 · Pronunciation & Fluency Lab ◆ **B · THE SYSTEM**

### Where the stress falls in a purpose clause


![Four phrases marked for stress, showing the difference between stressing the purpose clause and stressing the main clause.](figures/fig_b13_u04_p07_v10.png){width=6.6in}

*The purpose clause carries the news — Same words. Move the stress and the sentence changes what it is about.*


### 7A · Hear 🔊 Track 4.3

> *The beds are small* | *so that each student can reach the* **CENTRE** *.*
> **The purpose clause carries the new information, and the stress is at the end of it.**

Now with the stress moved:

> *The beds are* **SMALL** *so that each student can reach the centre.*
> **Now the size is the news, and the purpose has become background.**

### 7B · Say

Say both. Then say this one three times, faster:

> *We planted twelve crops in order to show twelve cycles.*

### 7C · Make it mean something

Say these two:

> *We built it* **SO** *that it would fail.* *(that was the plan)*
> *We built it so that it would* **FAIL** *.* *(and the failure is the point being made)*

**One sentence, two jobs.** Which is which?

### 7D · Fluency 30 ⏺

Thirty seconds: **four sentences, each with a purpose clause, each stressed at the end.**
Three times, faster, keeping the stress where it belongs.

## Part 8 · Writing ◆ **C · THE PERFORMANCE**

### A design rationale · 180 words


![A cutaway of a written design rationale in four layers from the decision and its purpose to the trade-off and what the writer would change.](figures/fig_b13_u04_p08_v02.png){width=6.6in}

*A design rationale, in section — Four layers. The top one is a sentence anybody could argue with.*


### 8A · Read the model

> Every bed is one metre square so that a student can reach the centre from any edge without
> standing on the soil. `[the decision and its purpose, in one sentence, with the purpose last]`
>
> The constraint was the number of students, not the amount of land: twenty students, one bed
> each, one semester. `[the constraint, named as a number]`
>
> This was bought at the expense of yield. A metre-square bed produces about four kilograms a
> year, which is roughly a fifth of what the same soil would produce in a merged plot.
> `[the trade-off, with both sides measured]`
>
> If I were specifying it again I would build two merged beds at the far end, so that students
> could see what they are giving up. `[what would change, and the reason is a teaching reason]`

**Questions about the writer's choices:**

1. The purpose comes at the end of the first sentence. **Why not at the beginning?**
2. The trade-off is given with both numbers. **What would one number alone be worth?**
3. The last paragraph changes the design. **Is it conceding that the original was wrong?**

### 8B · The anti-model

> The teaching garden has been designed in accordance with pedagogical best practice and
> reflects the School's commitment to experiential learning. Bed dimensions have been optimised
> to deliver an effective student experience while maintaining appropriate sustainability
> outcomes.

Correct English, and nobody could rebuild the garden from it. **Name the three things it has
replaced with adjectives**, and say what *optimised* is hiding.

### 8C · Plan

> the decision, with its purpose → the constraint, as a number → the trade-off, both sides
> measured → what you would change and why

### 8D · Write — 180 words
### 8E · Peer review — three criteria

> ☐ Is there a purpose, not only a rule?
> ☐ Is the trade-off given with both sides measured?
> ☐ Could a stranger rebuild the thing from this?

### 8F · Write it again · 8G · Publish

On the wall, beside the thing it describes.

---

## Part 9 · The File ◆ **A · THE WORLD + C · THE PERFORMANCE**

# WORK FILE · The Client Problem


![Four client briefs transcribed with annotations identifying which are problems, solutions, wishes or budgets in disguise.](figures/fig_b13_u04_p09_v05.png){width=6.6in}

*Four briefs, and what each one actually is — A problem, a solution, a wish and a budget - and only one of them says so.*


### 9A · What the client asked for 🔊 Track 4.4

Four short briefings. **Listen.**

1. Which client knows what they want, and which knows what they do not want?
2. **Which of the four briefs contains a solution disguised as a requirement?**
3. One brief has a constraint the client did not mention until the end. Which, and why then?

### 9B · Four things a brief can be

> **a problem** — here is what is wrong; you tell us what to build
> **a solution** — here is what to build; we have already decided
> **a wish** — here is what we want it to feel like
> **a budget** — here is what we can spend; work backwards

**Which of the four is most often disguised as another, and which is hardest to argue with?**

### 9C · Asking the question that annoys the client

**Which of these would you use, and when?**

1. *"What would success look like in a year?"*
2. *"What have you already tried?"*
3. *"If you could only have one of these five, which?"*
4. *"Who has to agree to this before it happens?"*

### 9D · Your turn

In fours. **Twelve minutes.** One person is the client and briefs a change to something on your
campus. **Everybody else must ask one of the four questions and nothing else.** Then: which
question moved the brief most?

### 9E · Transfer

**Find a sign, a form or a rule near you and ask what it was for.** Bring the answer, or the
fact that nobody knows.

## Part 10 · Mediation & Interaction ◆ **C · THE PERFORMANCE**


![An infographic across six years showing beds, yield, the kitchen taking produce and the two merged beds.](figures/fig_b13_u04_p10_v12.png){width=6.6in}

*Six years of a garden with two jobs — Half the class sees the brief. Half sees the harvest. Rebuild it by speaking.*


### 10A · Relay

Half the class sees the design brief. Half sees the harvest figures.
**Rebuild the garden by speaking.** Twelve minutes.

### 10B · Simplify

Explain the metre-square decision to somebody who has just asked why you do not merge the beds.
**Five sentences only.**

> **Plan:** what the bed is for → what a student does in it → what happens if it is bigger →
> what that costs in food → what the garden is not

### 10C · Compress

The explainer in Part 5B into **forty words**, keeping the trade-off.

### 10D · Online

Write **one message** to the campus kitchen: you understand the request, you can name exactly
what it would cost, and you are proposing one specific experiment for one season.

### 10E · Your language

Does your language separate purpose from result the way English does? **Say *so that nobody
would notice* and *so that nobody noticed* in both languages.** Which distinction survives?

## Part 11 · The Decision ◆ **C · THE PERFORMANCE**

# A classroom, or a market garden

The garden produced 94 kg last year across 40 metre-square beds. A merged plot on the same land
would produce about 480 kg. The kitchen uses 94 kg in four days and 480 kg in nineteen.

- **Keep it as a classroom.** Twelve crops, forty beds, twenty students a semester. The kitchen
  takes what there is and nobody calls it a food source.
- **Convert it.** Four crops, merged plots, five times the yield. Students visit it rather than
  run it, and three of the twelve growing cycles disappear from the curriculum.
- **Split it.** Half classroom, half production. Half the beds, half the yield, and a boundary
  that will move every year in whichever direction has the louder advocate.

### 11A · The evidence


![A bar chart comparing yield in kilograms, growing cycles taught and students with a bed for three garden options.](figures/fig_b13_u04_p11_v06.png){width=6.6in}

*Yield and growing cycles, three options — The same land, three ways, and the two columns do not move together.*


1. **The brief and the harvest sheet** from Part 5E — and the line added by hand.
2. **The chart** — yield per square metre, and growing cycles taught, for each option.
3. **The voice.** A third-year, thirty seconds: *"I killed a bed of brassicas in second year by
   standing on it in March. Nobody told me I had. I worked it out in September from the yield,
   and I have never stood on soil since. You cannot teach that in a merged plot, because in a
   merged plot nobody would ever have known it was me."*

### 11B · Talk about it

In fours. Everybody speaks twice. Use the frame.

> *It was built so that ______ . In practice ______ , which is why ______ .*

**Words you can use:** *purpose · rationale · constraint · trade-off · specification ·
yield · plot · harvest · curriculum · by design*

### 11C · Decide

Write **three sentences**. Choose, say why, and name what it costs.

> I think the School should ______________________ ,
> because ______________________ .
> What this costs is ______________________ .

### 11D · Model decision

> Keep it as a classroom and build two merged beds at the far end, as the model in 8A suggests.
> The merged beds are not a compromise between the two purposes; they are part of the first one.
> A student who has managed a metre square and then seen what the same soil does at scale has
> learned the trade-off rather than been told it. The kitchen gets about twelve extra kilos,
> which is nothing, and that is also part of the lesson.

### 11E · The Reveal

> **What happened.** The two merged beds went in. They produced 51 kilograms between them, which
> is more than half of what the other forty beds produced together, and the figure is now on the
> wall of the teaching shed where every student sees it in week one.
>
> The kitchen stopped taking from the garden the following year, not because anybody asked but
> because the head chef read the explainer and said she had not realised the garden was a
> classroom. The menu board was changed in an afternoon.
>
> Two years later the School was asked to double the garden for a food-security grant. They
> said no, and the sentence they used in the application was *it does one of them well*, which
> the funder quoted back approvingly in a rejection letter.

**The two beds taught the thing the garden could not say. The chef changed a menu board because
somebody finally wrote down what the garden was for.**

> **Was it the right decision?** Say why — and say what the School should do about the grant.

### 12A · Recycle — twelve items

> 1. The beds are small ______ each student can reach the middle.
> 2. We planted twelve crops ______ show twelve cycles.
> 3. The yield is ______ low ______ nobody notices.
> 4. It is ______ a low yield ______ nobody notices.
> 5. ______ to the curriculum, the beds cannot be merged.
> 6. We built it ______ as to test one thing.
> 7. The beds are small; ______ , the yield is low.
> 8. A system ______ each student manages one bed.
> 9. We ______ out to build a classroom.
> 10. We ______ up with a market garden.
> 11. They ______ it down to twenty beds.
> 12. You have to ______ that in at the start.

### 12B · Consolidate

**Three columns, from memory:** purpose forms · reason forms · result forms.

### 12C · Can-do, with evidence

> ☐ I can explain a decision by its purpose, not its rule. **Evidence:** 4C, 8D
> ☐ I can use *so that*, *in order to* and *so … that* correctly. **Evidence:** 3H, 6F
> ☐ I can name a trade-off with both sides measured. **Evidence:** 8D, 11C
> ☐ I can stress a purpose clause where the new information is. **Evidence:** 7D

### 12D · Replay ⏺

Record 0C again. **Listen to both.** What have you got that you did not have in week one?

### 12E · Glossary — forty-five items, as a map

> **THE DESIGN** · purpose · intention · rationale · specification · requirement · constraint · trade-off · prototype · a design brief
> **THE GARDEN** · yield · plot · harvest · curriculum
> **THE MOVES** · set out to · end up · scale down · build in · by design · in practice · the whole point · serve a purpose · at the expense of
> **WHAT PEOPLE SAY** · that was never the idea · it does one of them well
> **PURPOSE** · so that · in order to · so as to · with a view to · for the sake of
> **REASON** · because · since · as · owing to · whereby
> **RESULT** · so · such · such that · therefore · thus · hence · consequently · the result being · lead to · bring about · which is why it is there

## Part 0 · The Big Picture ◆ **A · THE WORLD**


![A university corridor and tutorial room with chairs in a circle, a mark sheet with a blank row, a noticeboard with a deadline and one person in the corridor.](figures/fig_b13_u05_p00_v01.png){width=6.6in}

*Tutorial room 1.07, the last Friday of term, 16.58 — Twelve things to find. Four are the story as it was planned. Four are the story as it came out.*


# What is the point exactly?

**By the end of this unit you can tell a story with a deliberate reveal, and land it.**

| ◆ **A · THE WORLD** | A tutorial where the best writing in the group has never been said aloud |
|---|---|
| ◆ **B · THE SYSTEM** | cleft sentences: *It was… · What I…* · 24 words for storytelling and emphasis · 21 focus forms · nuclear stress placement |
| ◆ **C · THE PERFORMANCE** | A two-minute story that arrives somewhere the listener did not expect |

---

### 0A · Find it

**Find twelve things in this picture.** Four of them are the story as it was planned. Four are
the story as it came out.

> a mark sheet with one blank row · an essay with a comment in the margin ·
> a tutorial room with the chairs in a circle · a laptop open at page nine of eleven ·
> a clock at 16.58 · a window with the blind half down · a printed essay with a corner folded ·
> a corridor with one person in it · a noticeboard with a deadline on it ·
> a coffee cup with a name spelled wrong · a bag with a book in it, unopened ·
> a door with a nameplate and a timetable taped under it

### 0B · Guess it

**Two of the twelve are the same thing at two different moments.** Which, and what changed
between them?

### 0C · Say it ⏺

**Record forty-five seconds.** *Tell something that happened to you, and keep one fact back
until the last sentence.* You will record it again at the end of this unit.

## Part 1 · Vocabulary Lab 1 ◆ **B · THE SYSTEM**


![A network from a slow build through a planted detail and a withheld fact to the turn and the landing, with the surprise shown as a weaker path.](figures/fig_b13_u05_p01_v08.png){width=6.6in}

*How a reveal is built — Six nodes. The one at the far right was already in the listener's hand at node two.*


### 1A · Meet — the words of a told story

> **anecdote** · **pacing** · **detail** · **climax** · **reveal** · **twist** ·
> **punchline** · **payoff**

1. Which two of these are the end, and what is the difference between them?
2. **A *twist* and a *reveal*: which one changes what you already heard?**
3. *Pacing* is a quantity of what?
4. **Why does a story with too much detail have no detail at all?**

> **A *reveal* is not new information.** It is information the listener already had, arriving
> in the one order that makes it mean something else.

### 1B · Sort — before, during, or after the reveal?

> the thing nobody saw · foreshadow · withhold · and that is when · it turns out · focus ·
> a slow build · emphasis · save it for

**Three columns. Two of them belong in more than one.** Say which and why.

### 1C · Meet — the phrase that does most of the work

> **c** **the thing nobody saw**

Not the thing nobody knew. The thing that was in front of everybody for the whole story.

1. **Why is the second kind harder to write and better to hear?**
2. Think of a story you know where the reveal was visible from the first minute.

### 1D · Collocation Lab

| | |
|---|---|
| **the point** | **the point is** · miss ~ · get to ~ |
| **what matters** | **what matters is** · ~ here · ~ most |
| **save** | ~ **it for** the end · ~ it for later |
| **a slow build** | ~ to the reveal · too slow ~ |

**Which two of these can be said about somebody else's story while they are telling it?**

### 1E · The fixed expressions

> **f** **And that is when.** — the three words that mark the turn, and they work because the
> sentence after them is short.
> **f** **I will come back to that.** — a promise. If you do not keep it, the listener remembers.

**Which one is a storyteller's tool and which is a speaker's debt?**

### 1F · Own it

The four phrasal verbs of a reveal.

> **give away** · **hold off** · **lead up to** · **land it**

1. Which two are about timing, and which about effect?
2. **What exactly do you *give away* — the ending, or the fact that there is one?**
3. *Hold off* and *withhold*: which is a decision and which is a technique?
4. Write one sentence in which somebody fails to **land it** despite everything else being right.

*The office, Thursday, five to five — One of them has to enter a number tomorrow. One of them wrote the sentence in 2011.*

## Part 3 · Grammar Lab 1 ◆ **B · THE SYSTEM**

### Putting one part of a sentence in a spotlight

### 3A · Notice

Look at the four sentences in Part 2.

1. In sentence 1, what is being emphasised, and what is it being contrasted with?
2. **In sentence 2, which half of the sentence is the new information?**
3. **Rewrite sentence 3 as an ordinary sentence.** What is lost?
4. Sentence 4 splits one idea across two sentences. **Why not one?**
5. Try *"It was failed the criterion."* **What is missing?**

### 3B · See


![A sentence shown with brackets marking the parts that an it-cleft, a wh-cleft and an all-cleft each put in the spotlight.](figures/fig_b13_u05_p03_v09.png){width=6.6in}

*One sentence, three ways to point at it — The words do not move. The cleft decides which part the reader is to hold.*


### 3C · Know

| | Shape | Spotlights |
|---|---|---|
| ***it*-cleft** | *it was X who / that…* | a noun — a person, a thing, a time |
| ***wh*-cleft** | *What I did was read them.* | an action, or a whole clause |
| **the one / the thing** | *The thing that worries me is…* | a noun, more conversationally |
| **the reason** | *The reason why he said nothing is…* | a cause |
| **all-cleft** | *All I did was read eleven essays.* | and it means *only that, nothing more* |

> **A cleft takes one sentence and makes it two clauses, so that one part can be made
> prominent.** *He failed the criterion* becomes *It was the criterion that he failed* — and
> now the sentence is about the criterion.

> **The *all*-cleft always carries a defence or a minimisation.** *All I did was ask* means
> *and look what happened*. Native speakers hear the implication even when it was not meant.

> **The cleft needs both halves.**
> ✗ *It was failed the criterion.* ✓ *It was the criterion that failed.*

> **Why it matters.** Word order in English is fixed; emphasis in speech is carried by stress.
> In writing, a cleft is how you make somebody read the stress you meant.

### 3D · Use — controlled

> (1) __________ was not the essay that was the problem. (2) __________ worried Priya was the
> sentence in the handbook, which had been written in 2011 and had never been read by anybody
> who had to use it. (3) __________ thing that made it unusable was not its wording but its
> verb: *contributes*. (4) __________ reason why she could not simply ignore it is that marks
> are audited. (5) __________ I did was put eleven essays in order, she said, and then look at
> the mark the rule gave the person at the top. (6) __________ not until then that she saw how
> far apart the two numbers were. (7) __________ one thing she was certain of was that she was
> not going to be the only tutor in this position on Friday.

### 3E · Use — guided

Rewrite each one as a cleft, using the structure in brackets.

1. The criterion failed, not the student. *(it-cleft)*
2. She read eleven essays. That is all. *(all-cleft)*
3. She wants the rule changed, not the mark. *(wh-cleft)*
4. He said nothing because the moment had always gone. *(the reason)*
5. The audit worries her most. *(the thing)*

### 3F · Use — open

**In pairs.** Describe a decision somebody else made badly, in six sentences, three of which
must be clefts. **Your partner's job** is to say what each cleft is contrasting with.

### 3G · Error autopsy


![Two panels comparing it was failed the criterion with it was the criterion that failed.](figures/fig_b13_u05_p03_v11.png){width=6.6in}

*A cleft with only one half — A cleft with only one half*


**Read both versions. Then say what the first one accidentally emphasises.**

### 3H · Check — eight items

> 1. ______ was the criterion that failed. *(it-cleft)*
> 2. ______ worries me is the audit. *(wh-cleft)*
> 3. ______ I did was read them in order. *(all-cleft)*
> 4. The ______ why he said nothing is simple. *(cause)*
> 5. The ______ that worries me is next year. *(noun)*
> 6. ✗ *It was failed the criterion.* → correct it.
> 7. It was ______ then that she saw it. *(not + time)*
> 8. The ______ thing he asked for was a deadline. *(single)*

**0–4 →** Workbook pp. 22–25. **5–6 →** Part 6. **7–8 →** page 107.

## Part 4 · Speaking ◆ **C · THE PERFORMANCE**

### 4A · Fluency starter — move the spotlight

One learner gives a flat sentence. The next says it as a cleft. The next moves the spotlight to
a different part. **The chain breaks when there is nothing left to emphasise.**

### 4B · The functional core — telling it so it lands


![Two panels compared: a story that plants a detail early and one that explains everything at the end.](figures/fig_b13_u05_p04_v04.png){width=6.6in}

*The same four seconds, told two ways — Learner A has the first half. Learner B has the last line.*


**Language Bank**

| | |
|---|---|
| **the slow build** | *This was the third week.* · *Nothing had happened yet.* |
| **the detail you keep** | *I will come back to that.* · *There was one other thing.* |
| **the turn** | *And that is when…* · *It turns out…* · *Not until then did I…* |
| **the landing** | *The point is…* · *What matters is…* · one short sentence, and stop |

**Information gap.** Learner A has the first half of a story. Learner B has the last line.
**Build the middle so that the last line is a reveal and not a surprise.**

### 4C · The performance — two minutes ⏺

Tell **a true story with a deliberate reveal**. **Two minutes.** You must include:

> a slow build · one detail planted early and used late · a turn, marked ·
> a landing of one short sentence

**Record it.**

### 4D · Observer

One person listens for **one thing**: was the reveal visible in hindsight, or did it come from
nowhere?

### 4E · Three criteria

> ☐ Was the planted detail there before it mattered?
> ☐ Did I mark the turn, out loud?
> ☐ Was the last sentence shorter than the one before it?

## Part 5 · Reading ◆ **A · THE WORLD**

### 5A · Predict

A fiction extract, titled **"The Quiet Room."**
**What do you expect the last line to do to the first paragraph?** Two sentences.

### 5B · Read — two minutes and a quarter

---

**THE QUIET ROOM**
*from a collection of short fiction*

For eleven weeks Vinh sat in the same chair, third from the window, and did not speak. He had a
reason, and the reason was not nerves.

He had worked out in the second week that the gap between a question and an answer in that room
was about four seconds. He had timed it, once, with the stopwatch on his phone, under the table.
Four seconds, and then Marguerite would be speaking. He needed eleven. He had measured that too,
writing his answer down and then reading it aloud at the speed people talked, in a stairwell,
twice.

So he wrote. He wrote the answers to every question that was asked and most of the ones that
were not, and at the end of each session he put the sheet in his bag and went to the library,
where the gap between a question and an answer was as long as he wanted it to be.

In week nine the tutor asked a question nobody answered. The silence went on. Vinh counted it:
six seconds, seven, nine. At eleven he opened his mouth, and at eleven and a half Marguerite,
who had been looking at her phone, said something that was almost but not quite what he had been
going to say.

He wrote that down too.

At the end of term the tutor returned the essays. His had a comment on the last page that said:
*This is the best piece of writing I have had from this group in four years. Why have I never
heard any of this?*

He read it twice. Then he took out the eleven sheets of paper from eleven weeks, squared the
edges, and put them on her desk on his way out, without saying anything, which was, he realised
on the stairs, exactly the problem she had just asked him about.

---

### 5C · Comprehension — eight questions

1. Where does Vinh sit, and for how long?
2. **What is the four seconds, and how does he know?**
3. Why does he need eleven, and how did he measure that?
4. **What does he do with the written answers?**
5. What happens in week nine, second by second?
6. **What does Marguerite say, and why is "almost but not quite" the point?**
7. What does the tutor's comment say, and what does it ask?
8. What does Vinh do with the eleven sheets, and what does he realise on the stairs?

### 5D · Vocabulary in context

Guess from the text. Then check.

> under the table · at the speed people talked · squared the edges · on his way out ·
> which was, he realised

### 5E · The counter-text


![A participation mark sheet with one row left blank beside the handbook page defining the criterion, with an audit note.](figures/fig_b13_u05_p05_v05.png){width=6.6in}

*The mark sheet and the handbook page — One of them was written this week. One of them was written in 2011.*


**Read the mark sheet and the handbook page, and answer.**

1. What exactly does the published criterion say, and when was it written?
2. **What mark does the criterion give Thanh, and what mark does the essay give him?**
3. How many tutors have overridden the criterion this year, and on what grounds?
4. What does the audit note say happens to an overridden mark?
5. One row of the sheet has two marks in it, one crossed out. Which, and what does that tell you?

### 5F · Two texts

The story says the gap was **four seconds** and Vinh needed **eleven**.
The handbook says participation means **contributing regularly to seminar discussion**.

**Does the criterion measure a capacity or a speed?** Three sentences.

## Part 6 · Vocabulary Lab 2 ◆ **B · THE SYSTEM**

### The words that point

### 6A · Sort — what does each one spotlight?

Three columns: **a person or thing · an action · a degree**.

> specifically · the one · what · particularly · all · merely · precisely · the thing ·
> exactly · it · the reason

### 6B · Chunk completion

1. **It was Marguerite ______** answered, four seconds in.
2. **What I ______ is** that he had timed it.
3. **All I ______ was** read eleven essays.
4. **The ______ that** worries me is the audit.
5. **The reason ______** he said nothing is on the sheet.
6. **The ______ thing** he wanted was eleven seconds.

### 6C · Four adverbs that narrow

**precisely · exactly · specifically · particularly**

> Three of them narrow to a point. One of them narrows to a degree. **Which, and what happens
> if you swap them?**

**Write four sentences about the story, one with each.**

### 6D · Two verbs that point at something

> **p** **point up** · **p** **zero in on**

> *The comment points up the problem with the criterion.*
> *She zeroed in on the one sentence that mattered.*

**Point up** makes something visible. **Zero in on** means everything else stops mattering.

**Rewrite two sentences from Part 5B using one of them.**

### 6E · The fixed expressions

> **f** **What happened was this.** — a frame. Everything after it is the story, and the
> listener knows to stop interrupting.
> **c** **not until then** — *It was not until then that she saw it.* The delay is the point.

> **Use one of them to open a story and the other to turn it.**

### 6F · Contrast Clinic

Eight items. **Four are wrong. Correct them and say what the rule is.**

1. What I did was reading them in order.
2. All I did was ask a question.
3. It was failed the criterion.
4. The reason why he said nothing is simple.
5. It was not until then she saw it.
6. It was the criterion that failed.
7. What I did was read them in order.
8. All I did was asked a question.

## Part 7 · Pronunciation & Fluency Lab ◆ **B · THE SYSTEM**

### Where the loudest word goes


![Four phrases marked for stress showing how nuclear stress placement changes which part of a sentence is the news.](figures/fig_b13_u05_p07_v10.png){width=6.6in}

*The loudest word, and what it chooses — Four versions of four words. Only the stress tells them apart.*


### 7A · Hear 🔊 Track 5.3

> *It was the* **CRITERION** *that failed.*
> **The cleft has already chosen the word. The stress just confirms it.**

Now listen to the flat version, three times:

> *The* **CRITERION** *failed.* · *The criterion* **FAILED** *.* · **THE** *criterion failed.*
> **Three meanings, one word order, and only the stress tells them apart.**

### 7B · Say

Say all four. Then say this one three times, faster:

> *It was not until then that she saw how far apart the two numbers were.*

### 7C · Make it mean something

Say these two:

> *All I did was* **ASK** *.* *(and look what happened)*
> **ALL** *I did was ask.* *(and nothing else — I am defending myself)*

**One sentence, two defences.** Which is which?

### 7D · Fluency 30 ⏺

Thirty seconds: **four clefts, each with the stress on the spotlit word.**
Three times, faster, keeping the stress.

## Part 8 · Writing ◆ **C · THE PERFORMANCE**

### A story with a reveal · 180 words


![A cutaway of a written story in four layers from the slow build and the planted detail to the turn and the landing.](figures/fig_b13_u05_p08_v02.png){width=6.6in}

*A story with a reveal, in section — Four layers. The reader has the bottom one before they know they need it.*


### 8A · Read the model

> For eleven weeks he sat in the same chair and said nothing, and everyone in the room had a
> theory about why. `[the slow build, and it gives the reader a wrong answer to hold]`
>
> It was not shyness. He had timed the gap between a question and an answer in that room, with
> a stopwatch, and it was four seconds. `[the planted detail, given plainly, as if it were
> background]`
>
> He needed eleven. `[three words, and the reader now has everything — the reveal is already
> theirs]`
>
> And that is when the tutor's comment on his essay stopped being a compliment and became a
> question he could not answer out loud, which was the whole problem, and was on the page in
> front of her. `[the turn, and the landing is the shape of the story itself]`

**Questions about the writer's choices:**

1. The first paragraph offers a wrong explanation. **What does that buy?**
2. The third paragraph is three words. **Why is it a paragraph?**
3. The reveal is a number the reader already had. **Why is it still a reveal?**

### 8B · The anti-model

> He was a quiet student who struggled to contribute in class, which was unfortunate given
> that he was clearly very capable. In the end his tutor realised that he had a lot to offer
> and that the seminar format was not working for him, which was a shame for everyone involved.

Correct English, and nothing in it lands. **Name the three things it explains that it should
have shown**, and say what *in the end* is doing.

### 8C · Plan

> the slow build, with a wrong answer offered → the detail, planted plainly →
> the turn, marked → the landing, shorter than the sentence before it

### 8D · Write — 180 words
### 8E · Peer review — three criteria

> ☐ Was the reveal visible in hindsight?
> ☐ Is the planted detail given before it matters, and given plainly?
> ☐ Is the last sentence the shortest?

### 8F · Write it again · 8G · Publish

Read aloud, not handed round. **Watch the faces for where it lands.**

---

## Part 9 · The File ◆ **A · THE WORLD + C · THE PERFORMANCE**

# STUDY FILE · Reading at Speed


![A record of four students reading the same chapter at four speeds, with what each one retained and what the task actually required.](figures/fig_b13_u05_p09_v05.png){width=6.6in}

*Four readers, forty pages, one chapter — Same text, same week, four different jobs - and only one of them was the job set.*


### 9A · Four readers, one chapter 🔊 Track 5.4

Four students describe reading the same forty pages. **Listen.**

1. Which one read it fastest, and what do they remember?
2. **Which one read it slowest, and did they understand more?**
3. One of them did not read it at all and could still discuss it. How?

### 9B · Four speeds, four jobs

> **survey** — what is this, and do I need it? — two minutes for forty pages
> **skim** — what does it claim? — ten minutes
> **read** — what is the argument, exactly? — forty minutes
> **study** — could I reconstruct this? — two hours, with a pen

**Which of the four are you doing by default, and is it the one the task needs?**

### 9C · The thing nobody teaches

Most reading failures are not speed failures. They are **not knowing which of the four you are
supposed to be doing**, and doing the slowest one on everything until the time runs out.

**Look at this week's reading list. Mark each item with one of the four.**

### 9D · Your turn

In fours. **Twelve minutes.** Everybody surveys the same chapter for two minutes, then writes
three sentences on what it claims. **Compare.** How much of it did two minutes get?

### 9E · Transfer

**Time yourself on the next thing you read for a class.** Which of the four were you doing, and
which did the task actually need?

![An infographic from week one to two years later showing times spoken, sheets written, the mark decision and the handbook change.](figures/fig_b13_u05_p10_v12.png){width=6.6in}

*Eleven weeks, and two years afterwards — Half the class sees the story. Half sees the mark sheet. Rebuild it by speaking.*


### 10A · Relay

Half the class sees the story. Half sees the mark sheet.
**Rebuild the term by speaking.** Twelve minutes.

### 10B · Simplify

Explain the participation criterion to a student who has just received a mark they do not
understand. **Five sentences only.**

> **Plan:** what the rule says → what it measures → what it does not → what their mark is →
> what they can ask for, and by when

### 10C · Compress

The story in Part 5B into **forty words**, keeping the reveal.

### 10D · Online

Write **one message** from Thanh to Priya: you have read the comment, you want to answer the
question in it, and you are proposing one specific thing for next semester.

### 10E · Your language

Does your language have clefts? **Say *it was the criterion that failed* in both languages.**
Which uses word order, and which uses a particle or a case ending?

## Part 11 · The Decision ◆ **C · THE PERFORMANCE**

# A mark that is due on Friday

Participation is fifteen per cent. The criterion says *contributes regularly to seminar
discussion*. Thanh has spoken twice in eleven weeks and has the highest essay mark in the group.

- **Apply the rule.** 4 out of 15. It is defensible at audit, it is what the handbook says, and
  it drops his unit mark by seven points.
- **Override it.** 13 out of 15, with a written justification. The mark stands unless the audit
  pulls it, and an overridden mark is flagged for three years.
- **Mark the eleven sheets.** He wrote an answer to every question asked and never submitted
  them. Nothing in the criterion says contribution has to be spoken. Nothing says it can be
  written either.

### 11A · The evidence


![A bar chart of essay marks and participation marks for eleven students, showing one student where the two diverge sharply.](figures/fig_b13_u05_p11_v06.png){width=6.6in}

*Essay mark against participation mark, eleven students — Two columns that agree for ten people.*


1. **The mark sheet and the handbook page** from Part 5E — and the row with two marks in it.
2. **The chart** — essay mark against participation mark, for every student in the group.
3. **The voice.** Thanh, thirty seconds: *"I am not asking her to change the rule. I know she
   cannot. What I want is for somebody to write down that the rule and the thing it is supposed
   to measure came apart, because if nobody writes it down, next year there is another one of
   me and she starts from nothing."*

### 11B · Talk about it

In fours. Everybody speaks twice. Use the frame.

> *It was ______ that ______ . What ______ is ______ .*

**Words you can use:** *reveal · emphasis · focus · detail · withhold · pacing · payoff ·
the point is · what matters is · it turns out*

### 11C · Decide

Write **three sentences**. Choose, say why, and name what it costs.

> I think Priya should ______________________ ,
> because ______________________ .
> What this costs is ______________________ .

### 11D · Model decision

> Mark the sheets, and write down why. The third option is the only one that does not require
> pretending: it does not pretend the rule is right and it does not pretend she has the power to
> rewrite it on a Friday. It also produces a document. An overridden mark disappears into an
> audit file; a marked set of eleven written contributions is a precedent with a date on it,
> and the next tutor in this position has something to point at.

### 11E · The Reveal

> **What happened.** Priya marked the eleven sheets and gave 12 out of 15, with a note that the
> contributions were written because the format made spoken ones impossible for this student at
> this speed. The mark was not audited. Nobody queried it.
>
> In March the following year a different tutor, in a different department, cited it. She had
> heard about it from Priya at a teaching lunch, which is the only route by which anything in a
> university travels in under four years.
>
> The handbook sentence was finally changed two years later. The new version says *contributes
> to seminar discussion in a form agreed with the tutor*, and the person who drafted it has
> never met Thanh and does not know the sentence exists because of him.

**One mark, one note, and a sentence in a handbook that two thousand students will read and
nobody will trace back. It turns out the thing nobody saw was the eleven sheets of paper in
his bag, every week, for a whole semester.**

> **Was it the right decision?** Say why — and say what Priya should have done in week three.

> 2. ______ worries me is the audit.

> 4. The ______ why he said nothing is simple.
> 5. The ______ that worries me is next year.
> 6. It was not ______ then that she saw it.
> 7. The ______ thing he asked for was time.

> 9. The comment ______ up the problem.
> 10. He did not ______ away the ending.
> 11. She ______ off until the last line.
> 12. He could not ______ it, and the room stayed quiet.

**Three columns, from memory:** *it*-clefts · *wh*-clefts · the noun clefts (*the thing*, *the
reason*, *the one*).

### 12E · Glossary — forty-five items, as a map

> **THE STORY** · anecdote · pacing · detail · climax · reveal · twist · punchline · payoff
> **THE CRAFT** · foreshadow · withhold · emphasis · focus · a slow build · save it for · lead up to · hold off · give away · land it
> **THE MOMENT** · it turns out · the thing nobody saw · the point is · what matters is
> **WHAT PEOPLE SAY** · and that is when · I will come back to that · what happened was this
> **THE SPOTLIGHT** · it · what · all · the thing · the reason · the one · it was X who · what I mean is · all I did was · the thing that · the reason why · the one thing · not until then
> **NARROWING** · merely · precisely · exactly · specifically · particularly · point up · zero in on

## Part 0 · The Big Picture ◆ **A · THE WORLD**


![A campus with a new research wing under scaffolding, letters of a sponsor name going up, a photographer, a press release on a noticeboard and a sign still showing the old name.](figures/fig_b13_u06_p00_v01.png){width=6.6in}

*The day the letters went up — Twelve things to find. Four are what the agreement says. Four are what people think it says.*


# What are they really claiming?

**By the end of this unit you can report a negotiation accurately, attributing every claim to
whoever made it.**

| ◆ **A · THE WORLD** | Four million dollars, a name on a building, and a paragraph about publishing |
|---|---|
| ◆ **B · THE SYSTEM** | reporting verbs and their patterns: *accuse of · persuade to · insist on* · 24 words for claims, PR and negotiation · 21 verb patterns · stress on the reporting verb |
| ◆ **C · THE PERFORMANCE** | A report of a meeting in which every claim has a name on it |

---

### 0A · Find it

**Find twelve things in this picture.** Four of them are what the agreement says. Four are what
people think it says.

> a building with a name going up in letters · a scaffold with two workers on it ·
> a press release on a noticeboard · a meeting room with a long table ·
> a nine-page document with a corner turned down · a model of a new wing under glass ·
> a photographer setting up · a researcher carrying a box out of a lab ·
> a cheque the size of a door · a lanyard with a sponsor's logo ·
> a lecture theatre with a new name on the door · a sign that still says the old name

### 0B · Guess it

**Two of the twelve contradict each other.** Which, and which one is older?

### 0C · Say it ⏺

**Record forty-five seconds.** *Describe an agreement you have made where one part turned out to
matter much more than it looked.* You will record it again at the end of this unit.

## Part 1 · Vocabulary Lab 1 ◆ **B · THE SYSTEM**


![A network from a claim made in a room through minutes and a press release to public understanding, with the owner dropping away at each step.](figures/fig_b13_u06_p01_v08.png){width=6.6in}

*Where a claim loses its owner — Six nodes. The claim is the same at every one of them, and only one still has a name on it.*


### 1A · Meet — the words of a negotiation

> **claim** · **sponsor** · **clause** · **negotiation** · **concession** · **leverage** ·
> **undertaking** · **stakeholder**

1. Which two of these are things you *give*, and which two are things you *have*?
2. **What is the difference between a *clause* and an *undertaking*?**
3. *Leverage*: where does it come from, and can both sides have it at once?
4. **Why is *stakeholder* a word that usually arrives when somebody is about to be ignored?**

> **A *claim* in this unit is not a lie.** It is a statement somebody is responsible for, and
> the whole job of a report is to keep the statement attached to the person.

### 1B · Sort — the money, the document, or the story?

> naming rights · in exchange for · the small print · retraction · spin · briefing ·
> a sticking point · on the record · strings attached

**Three columns. Two of them belong in more than one.** Say which and why.

### 1C · Meet — the phrase that does most of the work

> **c** **a sticking point**

The one item on which an agreement is currently not happening. There is usually exactly one,
and it is usually not the one being discussed.

1. **Why does naming the sticking point out loud speed a negotiation up?**
2. In a flat-share argument about washing up, what is the sticking point likely to be?

### 1D · Collocation Lab

| | |
|---|---|
| **on the record** | say something ~ · put it ~ · off ~ |
| **in exchange for** | given ~ · agreed ~ |
| **the small print** | read ~ · hidden in ~ |
| **strings attached** | with ~ · no ~ |

**Which two of these are almost always said by the side with less power?**

### 1E · The fixed expressions

> **f** **That is not what was agreed.** — the sentence that needs a document behind it, or it
> is just a louder opinion.
> **f** **We are not going to say that.** — a refusal that gives no reason, and in a negotiation
> it is often the most informative thing anybody says.

**Which one needs evidence, and which one *is* the evidence?**

### 1F · Own it

The four phrasal verbs of a deal.

> **water down** · **push back** · **sign up to** · **hold out**

1. Which two describe resistance, and how do they differ?
2. **What exactly gets *watered down* — the words, or the obligation?**
3. *Sign up to* and *sign*: what does the particle add?
4. Write one sentence in which somebody **holds out** and gets less than if they had not.

![A company representative and a university lawyer across a meeting table, their speech shown as reported claims with their patterns.](figures/fig_b13_u06_p02_v07.png){width=6.6in}

*The meeting about page nine — One of them wrote the clause. One of them read all nine pages.*

**Before you listen.** A company has offered four million dollars for a new research wing. The
agreement runs to nine pages. **Guess: which page is the problem?**

**Listen again. Complete the table.**

| | The sponsor | The university |
|---|---|---|
| on the name | | |
| on publication | | |
| on the deadline | | |

**Listen again. Choose the best answer.**

1. The sticking point is **the name / the money / clause 9.4**.
2. The sponsor says the clause is **standard / negotiable / new**.
3. The Dean has **signed / refused / asked for time**.
4. Somebody uses the word *stakeholder* **four times / once / twice**.

**Noticing.** Four sentences from the recording.

1. *She accused them of writing a veto and calling it a courtesy.*
2. *They persuaded the Dean to take it to the committee rather than decide alone.*
3. *He insisted on thirty days and denied that thirty days was a veto.*
4. *She warned them against describing it as standard, because it is not.*

**All four report a claim and attach it to somebody. Which one reports two claims at once?**

Thirty seconds of the negotiation. Four jobs.

1. How many reporting verbs can you count?
2. In *accused them of writing*, which word is stressed?

## Part 3 · Grammar Lab 1 ◆ **B · THE SYSTEM**

### Reporting verbs, and the patterns they insist on

### 3A · Notice

Look at the four sentences in Part 2.

1. In sentence 1, what follows *accused them*?
2. **In sentence 2, what follows *persuaded the Dean*?** Is it the same shape?
3. **Rewrite sentence 3 without *insisted on*.** What changes?
4. Sentence 4 uses *warn against*. **What would *warn to* mean, and is it English?**
5. Try *"They accused them to write a veto."* **What is wrong with it?**

### 3B · See


![A branching diagram of reporting verb patterns, each branch showing the preposition and an example sentence.](figures/fig_b13_u06_p03_v09.png){width=6.6in}

*The preposition is part of the verb — There is no rule behind this. Learn the verb with what follows it, or you have not learned the verb.*


### 3C · Know

| Pattern | Verbs | Example |
|---|---|---|
| **verb + sb + *of* + -ing** | accuse · remind | *She accused them of writing a veto.* |
| **verb + sb + *to* + infinitive** | persuade · urge · advise · warn · remind | *They persuaded the Dean to wait.* |
| **verb + sb + *for* + -ing** | blame · congratulate | *He blamed them for the delay.* |
| **verb + sb + *on* + noun** | congratulate | *She congratulated them on the gift.* |
| **verb + sb + *against* + -ing** | warn · advise | *She warned them against saying it.* |
| **verb + *on* + -ing** | insist | *He insisted on seeing the draft.* |
| **verb + -ing** | deny · admit | *He denied writing it.* |
| **verb + *that* + clause** | insist · deny · admit · warn · remind | *He insisted that thirty days was normal.* |

> **The pattern is part of the word.** *Accuse* takes *of*; *blame* takes *for*; *congratulate*
> takes *on*. There is no rule behind this and no shortcut: learn the verb with its preposition
> or you have not learned the verb.

> **Four of them take two patterns**, and the meaning does not change: *insist on / insist
> that*, *deny doing / deny that*, *warn against / warn that*, *remind to / remind that*.

> **A preview of Unit 7.** *He is said to have written it* reports a claim without saying whose
> it is. That is the whole of next unit, and it is what this unit's grammar exists to make
> unnecessary.

> **Why it matters.** A report that loses the pattern loses the person, and a claim with no
> person attached is the most dangerous sentence in any document.

### 3D · Use — controlled

> The university's lawyer (1) __________ the company __________ writing a veto and calling it
> a courtesy. The company's representative (2) __________ that it was a veto, and
> (3) __________ on the word *review*. He then (4) __________ the Dean __________ take it to
> the committee rather than decide alone, which was good advice and was also a delay.
> The Dean (5) __________ him __________ the November deadline, and he
> (6) __________ her __________ assuming it was movable. Nobody (7) __________ anybody
> __________ anything, which in a negotiation of this size is itself worth noting.

### 3E · Use — guided

Report each one with the verb in brackets.

1. "You have written a veto and called it a courtesy." *(accuse)*
2. "Take it to the committee." *(persuade)*
3. "Thirty days is standard, whatever you say." *(insist)*
4. "I did not write that paragraph." *(deny)*
5. "Do not call it standard in the press release." *(warn)*

### 3F · Use — open

**In pairs.** Report a disagreement you have both seen, in six sentences, four of which must use
a different reporting verb with its pattern. **Your partner's job** is to catch any claim that
has lost its owner.

### 3G · Error autopsy


![Two panels comparing accused them to write a veto with accused them of writing a veto.](figures/fig_b13_u06_p03_v11.png){width=6.6in}

*A verb with somebody else's preposition — A verb with somebody else's preposition*


**Read both versions. Then say who, in the first one, is responsible for the claim.**

### 3H · Check — eight items

> 1. She accused them ______ writing a veto.
> 2. They persuaded the Dean ______ wait.
> 3. He blamed them ______ the delay.
> 4. She congratulated them ______ the gift.
> 5. She warned them ______ saying it.
> 6. He insisted ______ seeing the draft.
> 7. He denied ______ (write) it.
> 8. ✗ *They accused them to write a veto.* → correct it.

**0–4 →** Workbook pp. 26–29. **5–6 →** Part 6. **7–8 →** page 129.

## Part 4 · Speaking ◆ **C · THE PERFORMANCE**

### 4A · Fluency starter — attach it

One learner gives a claim with no owner. The next attaches it to somebody with a reporting verb.
**The chain breaks on a wrong preposition.**

### 4B · The functional core — reporting a negotiation


![Two panels compared: formal meeting minutes and an informal corridor conversation, with one claim appearing in only one of them.](figures/fig_b13_u06_p04_v04.png){width=6.6in}

*The minutes, and the corridor — Learner A has what was minuted. Learner B has what two people said on the way out.*


**Language Bank**

| | |
|---|---|
| **what was said** | *X insisted on…* · *Y denied…* · *Z admitted…* |
| **what was offered** | *…in exchange for…* · *The concession was…* |
| **the sticking point** | *The one thing they would not move on was…* |
| **what was not said** | *Nobody mentioned…* · *It was not put on the record.* |

**Information gap.** Learner A has the minutes. Learner B has what two people said in the
corridor afterwards. **Find the claim that appears in one and not the other.**

### 4C · The performance — three minutes ⏺

Report **a negotiation**, real or from the unit. **Three minutes.** You must include:

> every claim attached to a named person · what each side gave up · the sticking point ·
> one thing nobody said out loud

**Record it.**

### 4D · Observer

One person listens for **one thing**: did any claim arrive without an owner?

### 4E · Three criteria

> ☐ Does every claim have a name on it?
> ☐ Did I use four different reporting verbs with the right pattern?
> ☐ Did I separate what was agreed from what was assumed?

## Part 5 · Reading ◆ **A · THE WORLD**

### 5A · Predict

A personal essay, titled **"I wrote the press release."**
**What do you expect the writer to admit?** Two sentences.

### 5B · Read — two minutes and a quarter

---

**I WROTE THE PRESS RELEASE**
*a personal essay*

I have worked in university communications for eleven years and I have written somewhere around
nine hundred press releases. I am going to describe one paragraph of one of them, because it is
the only thing I have written that I still think about.

The gift was four million dollars for a research wing. The agreement had nine pages. Clause 9.4
said that the sponsor would be given thirty days' sight of any publication arising from work
done in the wing, and could request amendments. Not require. Request.

I was given the agreement on a Tuesday and asked for a release by Thursday. I read all nine
pages, which I want on the record, because the thing people assume about my job is that we do
not read them.

I wrote: *The partnership includes standard arrangements for the review of research outputs
prior to publication.*

Every word in that sentence is true. *Standard* is doing the most work. The arrangement is not
standard — I checked four comparable agreements and three had no review clause at all — but the
sentence does not say it is common. It says it is standard, which is a word that means *of a
known type* and is heard as *normal*. I knew that when I wrote it. That is the craft, and I am
not going to pretend it was an accident.

What I did not do was lie, and what I also did not do was put the clause in the release, which
would have taken eleven words.

A researcher found it eighteen months later, in the agreement, which had been public the whole
time on a page three clicks from the homepage. She accused the university of hiding it. She was
wrong about hiding and right about everything else, and the thing I have not been able to get
past is that if she had been wrong about everything, I would have been fine.

---

### 5C · Comprehension — eight questions

1. How long has the writer worked in communications, and how many releases?
2. **What exactly does clause 9.4 allow the sponsor to do?** Give both verbs.
3. What does the writer want on the record, and why?
4. **Which word in the written sentence is "doing the most work", and how?**
5. What did the writer check, and what did they find?
6. **What two things does the writer say they did not do?**
7. How was the clause found, and how long had it been public?
8. What is the writer "not able to get past"?

### 5D · Vocabulary in context

Guess from the text. Then check.

> arising from · doing the most work · of a known type · three clicks from the homepage ·
> right about everything else

### 5E · The counter-text


![The text of a sponsorship review clause beside the press release sentence describing it, with four comparable agreements and a handwritten amendment.](figures/fig_b13_u06_p05_v05.png){width=6.6in}

*Clause 9.4 and the sentence that covered it — Nine pages, one paragraph, and eleven words that were not written.*


**Read clause 9.4 and the press release, and answer.**

1. What exactly does 9.4 say? Quote the two verbs.
2. **Which sentence of the release covers 9.4, and what does it leave out?**
3. How many comparable agreements were checked, and what did they contain?
4. What does the sponsor's internal briefing note say the clause is for?
5. One line of the agreement has been amended in handwriting and initialled. Which?

### 5F · Two texts

The release says the arrangements are **standard**.
Three of four comparable agreements have **no review clause at all**.

**Is the release false?** Three sentences.
