## Part 6 · Vocabulary Lab 2 ◆ **B · THE SYSTEM**

### Learning the preposition with the verb

### 6A · Sort — which preposition?

Four columns: ***of* · *for* · *on* · *against***.

> insist · congratulate somebody · advise somebody · warn somebody · accuse somebody ·
> blame somebody · remind somebody

### 6B · Chunk completion

1. **accuse somebody ______** writing it
2. **persuade somebody ______** take it to committee
3. **blame somebody ______** the delay
4. **congratulate somebody ______** the gift
5. **warn somebody ______** saying it
6. **insist ______** seeing the draft

### 6C · Two that change meaning with the pattern

**remind · warn**

> *He reminded her to read it.* — she was going to forget.
> *He reminded her that she had read it.* — she had forgotten that she already knew.
> *She warned them against saying it.* — do not.
> *She warned them that it was not standard.* — information, not instruction.

**Write four sentences about the essay in Part 5, one of each.**

### 6D · The two that persuade

> **p** **talk into** · **p** **talk out of**

> *They talked her into signing.* · *He talked them out of using the word.*

Both take *-ing*, both imply the other person did not want to, and both are warmer and less
formal than *persuade*.

**Rewrite two sentences from Part 2 using them.**

### 6E · Deny, admit, and the one that has no object

> **deny doing** · **admit doing** · and *insist* which takes no object at all.

He **denied doing** it. He **admitted** writing the first draft. He **insisted on** both.

> **Write three sentences about the writer of the essay.**

### 6F · Contrast Clinic

Eight items. **Four are wrong. Correct them and say what the rule is.**

1. They accused them of writing a veto.
2. She warned them against saying it.
3. She congratulated them for the gift.
4. He insisted to see the draft.
5. He denied to write it.
6. He reminded her to read it.
7. They accused them to write a veto.
8. He insisted on seeing the draft.

## Part 7 · Pronunciation & Fluency Lab ◆ **B · THE SYSTEM**

### The reporting verb carries the weight


![Four reported sentences marked for stress, showing how stressing the reporting verb, the object or the complement changes what is new.](figures/fig_b13_u06_p07_v10.png){width=6.6in}

*Stress the verb, and the report is the news — Four versions. The words do not change and the story does.*


### 7A · Hear 🔊 Track 6.3

> *She* **ACCUSED** *them of writing a veto.*
> **The verb is the news. Everything after it is the content of the accusation.**

Now with the stress moved:

> *She accused* **THEM** *of writing a veto.*
> **Now the news is who, and the accusation is old information.**

### 7B · Say

Say both. Then say this one three times, faster:

> *He insisted on thirty days and denied that thirty days was a veto.*

### 7C · Make it mean something

Say these two:

> *He* **ADMITTED** *writing it.* *(and that is a surprise)*
> *He admitted* **WRITING** *it.* *(as opposed to approving it, or circulating it)*

**One sentence, two stories.** Which is which?

### 7D · Fluency 30 ⏺

Thirty seconds: **four reports, four different verbs, the stress on the verb each time.**
Three times, faster.

## Part 8 · Writing ◆ **C · THE PERFORMANCE**

### A report of a meeting · 190 words


![A cutaway of a meeting report in four layers from the claims and the counter-claims to the sticking point and the silences.](figures/fig_b13_u06_p08_v02.png){width=6.6in}

*A report of a meeting, in section — Four layers. Every claim in the top two has a name on it.*


### 8A · Read the model

> The sponsor's representative insisted on retaining clause 9.4 and denied that a thirty-day
> review amounted to a veto. `[two claims, one sentence, both attached to the same named person]`
>
> The university's lawyer accused the company of writing a veto and calling it a courtesy. She
> warned them against describing the clause as standard in any public document.
> `[the opposing claims, with their patterns intact]`
>
> The sticking point was not the money or the name. It was the word *request*, which the sponsor
> would not change to *suggest* and the university would not accept as it stood.
> `[the sticking point, named, and it is a single word]`
>
> Nobody raised the comparable agreements. They were in the room, in a folder, and they were not
> mentioned. `[what was not said, which a report should carry]`

**Questions about the writer's choices:**

1. Every claim has a name. **What would the report be worth without them?**
2. The sticking point is one word. **Why is that more useful than "they disagreed about
   publication"?**
3. The last paragraph reports a silence. **Can a report do that fairly?**

### 8B · The anti-model

> There was a frank exchange of views regarding the publication provisions. Both parties
> expressed their positions and it was felt that further discussion would be beneficial. A
> number of concerns were raised and will be taken forward.

Correct English, and nobody in it did anything. **Find the three passives that have deleted a
person**, and say who *it was felt* belongs to.

### 8C · Plan

> the claims, each with its owner → the opposing claims, same treatment → the sticking point,
> named as precisely as you can → what was not said

### 8D · Write — 190 words
### 8E · Peer review — three criteria

> ☐ Does every claim have a named owner?
> ☐ Is the sticking point as precise as the evidence allows?
> ☐ Have I reported what was not said as well as what was?

### 8F · Write it again · 8G · Publish

Circulate to the people you reported. **That is the test.**

## Part 9 · The File ◆ **A · THE WORLD + C · THE PERFORMANCE**

# CULTURE FILE · Silence, Interruption and Turn-taking


![Four conversations with the same four-second pause annotated for whether it was comfortable, filled, or taken as a turn.](figures/fig_b13_u06_p09_v05.png){width=6.6in}

*The same four seconds, four conversations — A pause does not mean anything by itself. It means something against a local average.*


### 9A · Four seconds of nothing 🔊 Track 6.4

The same pause, in four conversations. **Listen.**

1. In which one is the pause comfortable?
2. **In which one does somebody fill it, and what does filling it do?**
3. One of the four has no pauses at all, and nobody is being rude. How does that work?

### 9B · Three ways a turn can end

> **a gap** — one speaker stops, a beat, the next begins
> **an overlap** — the next begins slightly early, as agreement
> **a competitive interruption** — the next begins early, to take the floor

**Which two are easily mistaken for each other, and by whom?**

### 9C · The length of a comfortable silence

Reported averages: around **0.2 seconds** between turns in much of northern Europe; longer in
some conversational cultures, and noticeably longer in others again. The number matters less
than this: **whatever yours is, a pause longer than it feels like a problem**, and you will
start talking to fix a problem that is not there.

**Whose silence have you filled this week?**

### 9D · Your turn

In fours. **Twelve minutes.** Discuss anything, with one rule: **three seconds of silence after
every turn, timed.** Then: who found it hardest, and what got said that would not have?

### 9E · Transfer

**Count the interruptions in your next meeting**, and sort them into the three kinds. Bring
the three numbers.

*Four million dollars, eighteen months, four days — Half the class sees the clause. Half sees the release. Rebuild it by speaking.*


### 10A · Relay

Half the class sees clause 9.4. Half sees the press release.
**Rebuild the agreement by speaking.** Twelve minutes.

### 10B · Simplify

Explain clause 9.4 to a PhD student about to start work in the new wing. **Five sentences only.**

> **Plan:** what it says → what it does not say → what it would feel like →
> what they can do before signing → who to ask

### 10C · Compress

The essay in Part 5B into **forty words**, keeping the admission.

### 10D · Online

Write **one message** to the communications office: you are not accusing anybody of lying, you
have one specific question about one word, and you want an answer in writing.

### 10E · Your language

Does your language attach claims to people the way English does? **Say *she accused them of
writing it* in both languages.** Which marks the accusation on the verb, and which on a particle?

## Part 11 · The Decision ◆ **C · THE PERFORMANCE**

# Four million dollars, and a paragraph about publishing

The gift funds a research wing for fifteen years. Clause 9.4 gives the sponsor thirty days'
sight of any publication and the right to request amendments. The sponsor will not remove it.
The Dean has until November.

- **Sign it.** Four million, a wing, forty researchers. The clause says *request*, not *require*,
  and no comparable agreement has ever been used to block anything.
- **Refuse the clause.** The sponsor has said it is not negotiable. They have said that about
  two other things already and moved on both.
- **Sign it and publish it.** Take the clause, and put it, in full, on the front page of the
  wing's website, alongside every request ever made under it.

### 11A · The evidence


![A bar chart of twelve comparable research agreements showing review periods in days and the number of amendment requests made.](figures/fig_b13_u06_p11_v06.png){width=6.6in}

*Review clauses in twelve comparable agreements — How many have one, how long it runs, and what was ever requested under it.*


1. **Clause 9.4 and the press release** from Part 5E — and the handwritten amendment.
2. **The chart** — review clauses in twelve comparable agreements, and what was requested under
   each.
3. **The voice.** A postdoctoral researcher, thirty seconds: *"Nobody will ever block a paper.
   They will not need to. I will know on the day I start what the thirty days are for, and in
   four years nobody will be able to point at a single thing I did not write, including me."*

### 11B · Talk about it

In fours. Everybody speaks twice. Use the frame.

> *They ______ us of ______ . We ______ on ______ . The sticking point is ______ .*

**Words you can use:** *claim · clause · leverage · concession · undertaking · stakeholder ·
spin · retraction · the small print · strings attached*

### 11C · Decide

Write **three sentences**. Choose, say why, and name what it costs.

> I think the university should ______________________ ,
> because ______________________ .
> What this costs is ______________________ .

### 11D · Model decision

> Sign it and publish it. The clause is not the danger; the clause plus silence is. A review
> right that everybody can see is a review right nobody can use quietly, and publishing every
> request made under it turns the sponsor's leverage into a public record of how little they
> use it — or into evidence, if they use it a lot. The cost is that the sponsor will read this
> decision as hostile, and they will be partly right.

### 11E · The Reveal

> **What happened.** The university signed and published. The clause went on the wing's website
> in week one, with a page beside it headed *Requests made under clause 9.4*, which stayed empty
> for two years and eleven months.
>
> Then one request was made, on a paper about a competitor's product. It asked for the removal
> of one sentence. The university refused, published the request and the refusal on the same
> page, and the paper went out unchanged four days later.
>
> The sponsor renewed for a second fifteen years. Their head of partnerships said in a
> conference talk, without mentioning the university by name, that the published-requests page
> was the single most useful thing any partner had ever done for them, because it meant nobody
> internally ever asked them to make a request again.

**The clause was never the problem. Writing it down where everybody could see it was what made
it harmless, and it took four days and one refusal to prove it.**

> **Was it the right decision?** Say why — and say what the researcher in 11A would say now.

> **THE STORY** · spin · briefing · retraction · on the record
> **THE MOVES** · in exchange for · a sticking point · walk away · the small print · strings attached · water down · push back · sign up to · hold out

> **REPORTING VERBS** · accuse · persuade · blame · congratulate · warn · remind · insist · deny · admit · urge · advise
> **THE PATTERNS** · accuse somebody of · persuade somebody to · blame somebody for · congratulate somebody on · warn somebody against · insist on · deny doing · talk into · talk out of

## Part 0 · The Big Picture ◆ **A · THE WORLD**


![A department building with a photocopier conversation, a pigeonhole with one envelope, a door with the nameplate removed and a procedure pinned to a noticeboard.](figures/fig_b13_u07_p00_v01.png){width=6.6in}

*The third week, Tuesday, 11.10 — Twelve things to find. Four are the claim. Four are the machinery that should have handled it.*


# Who says so?

**By the end of this unit you can write 190 words that report unverified claims without
endorsing them.**

| ◆ **A · THE WORLD** | Something is going round the department, and nobody can say where it started |
|---|---|
| ◆ **B · THE SYSTEM** | passive reporting: *it is said that · is thought to* · 24 words for rumour and evidence · 21 distancing forms · weak forms in passive chains |
| ◆ **C · THE PERFORMANCE** | A piece of writing that reports a claim and takes no position on it |

---

### 0A · Find it

**Find twelve things in this picture.** Four of them are the claim. Four are the machinery that
should have handled it.

> two people talking at a photocopier · a staff pigeonhole with one envelope in it ·
> a door with the nameplate removed · a timetable with one slot greyed out ·
> a group chat open on a phone · a noticeboard with a procedure pinned to it ·
> an office with the blind down at eleven in the morning · a corridor with people not looking ·
> a meeting room with four chairs and five people · a lanyard on a desk ·
> a form headed *Concern — Stage 1* · a printed email with a name blacked out

### 0B · Guess it

**One of the twelve was put there by the procedure, and eleven were not.** Which, and what does
that tell you?

### 0C · Say it ⏺

**Record forty-five seconds.** *Describe something you were told about somebody, that you never
checked, and that you still half believe.* You will record it again at the end of this unit.

## Part 1 · Vocabulary Lab 1 ◆ **B · THE SYSTEM**


![A network from something said at a photocopier through repetition and belief, with the complaint and the record shown as empty nodes nothing reaches.](figures/fig_b13_u07_p01_v08.png){width=6.6in}

*A claim with nobody holding it — Six nodes. The one in the middle is the only one anybody could act on, and it is empty.*


### 1A · Meet — the words of an unverified claim

> **rumour** · **hearsay** · **allegation** · **consensus** · **corroboration** ·
> **anonymity** · **due process** · **harm**

1. Which two of these are about *who else says it*?
2. **What is the difference between a *rumour* and an *allegation*?**
3. *Anonymity* protects somebody. **Who, and at whose cost?**
4. **Why is *due process* slow on purpose?**

> **A *rumour* has no author.** That is not a detail about it; it is the definition. The moment
> somebody will put their name to it, it becomes an allegation and a different set of machinery
> applies.

### 1B · Sort — the claim, the checking, or the damage?

> substantiate · take it seriously · no evidence that · it is said that · credible ·
> circulate · a matter of record · widely believed · unverified

**Three columns. Two of them belong in more than one.** Say which and why.

### 1C · Meet — the phrase that does most of the work

> **c** **no evidence that**

Not *evidence that it is false*. The absence of the one and the presence of the other get
reported in the same sentence shape, and almost nobody notices.

1. **Say the difference out loud, in two sentences.**
2. Which of the two can a one-week investigation produce?

### 1D · Collocation Lab

| | |
|---|---|
| **take it seriously** | ~ without believing it · be seen to ~ |
| **look into it** | we will ~ · nobody ever did ~ |
| **a matter of record** | now ~ · not ~ |
| **widely believed** | ~ and never checked · ~ to be |

**Which two of these can be true of something false?**

### 1E · The fixed expressions

> **f** **There is no smoke without fire.** — a proverb that does the work of an investigation
> without doing any of it. **Why is it so hard to answer?**
> **f** **Somebody told somebody.** — the whole evidential chain, said out loud, and it collapses
> the moment you write it down.

**Which one would you be ashamed to write in a report, and why is that useful?**

### 1F · Own it

The four phrasal verbs of a rumour.

> **go round** · **die down** · **come forward** · **hush up**

1. Which two describe what the claim does, and which what people do?
2. **What is the difference between a rumour that *dies down* and one that is resolved?**
3. *Come forward* implies a direction. Towards what?
4. Write one sentence in which somebody tries to **hush up** something that is true and harmless.

![A head of department and a colleague in a corridor, their speech shown as passive reporting structures.](figures/fig_b13_u07_p02_v07.png){width=6.6in}

*The head of department, week three — One of them has heard three versions. One of them has heard one, from a student.*

**Before you listen.** Something has been circulating about a member of staff for three weeks.
Nobody has made a complaint. **Guess: what does the head of department do?**

**Listen again. Complete the table.**

| | What is claimed | Who says so | Checkable? |
|---|---|---|---|
| version 1 | | | |
| version 2 | | | |
| version 3 | | | |

**Listen again. Choose the best answer.**

1. The three versions are **the same / related / unconnected**.
2. A formal complaint has been made **by one person / by the department / by nobody**.
3. The head of department wants to **find the source / act now / wait**.

**Noticing.** Four sentences from the recording.

1. *It is said that he was asked to leave his last post.*
2. *He is thought to have left for family reasons.*
3. *The claim is understood to have originated in a different department.*
4. *None of which has been established.*

**All four report without endorsing. Which one does it most completely?**

### 2B · Decoding clinic 🔊 Track 7.2

Thirty seconds of the head of department. Four jobs.

1. How many passive reporting structures can you count?
2. In *is thought to have left*, how many of the words are weak?

## Part 3 · Grammar Lab 1 ◆ **B · THE SYSTEM**

### Reporting without taking responsibility for it

### 3A · Notice

Look at the four sentences in Part 2.

1. In sentence 1, who says it? **Can you tell from the sentence?**
2. **In sentence 2, what is the subject, and where did the claimers go?**
3. **Rewrite sentence 3 starting with *It*.** What is gained and what is lost?
4. Sentence 4 is not a sentence. **What is it attached to?**
5. Try *"He is said that he left."* **What is wrong with it?**

### 3B · See


![Two sentence diagrams comparing people say he was asked to leave with he is said to have been asked to leave, with the claimers shown as a deleted element.](figures/fig_b13_u07_p03_v09.png){width=6.6in}

*Where the people who say it go — The same claim, twice. In the second one nobody is left to ask.*


### 3C · Know

| Shape | Example | Where the claimer went |
|---|---|---|
| ***It* + passive + *that*** | *It is said that he left.* | deleted, and the claim is foregrounded |
| **subject + passive + *to*** | *He is said to have left.* | deleted, and the person is foregrounded |
| **verbs that do this** | *say · think · believe · understand · allege · report · expect* | |
| **present** | *is thought to be* | now |
| **past** | *is thought to have been* | then — and the reporting is still now |

> **Two shapes, one meaning, different first words.** *It is thought that he left* and *He is
> thought to have left* report the same claim. The second one puts the person in the subject
> slot, which is why newspapers prefer it and why it does more damage.

> ***to have* + past participle moves the claim backwards, not the reporting.**
> *is said to be* = he is, now. *is said to have been* = he was, then. The *is said* stays in
> the present in both, because the saying is happening now.

> **The claimer is deleted, and that is the point.** These structures exist so that a claim can
> be printed when nobody will attach their name to it. **Reporting responsibly means putting the
> name back when you have one.**

> **Why it matters.** This is the grammar of every sentence you will ever read about something
> nobody has proved. Learn to see it and you can read a newspaper twice.

### 3D · Use — controlled

> (1) __________ is said that the appointment was rushed. He (2) __________ thought
> __________ have been interviewed by two people rather than the usual four, although the panel
> record shows three. He is also (3) __________ to have left his previous post at short notice,
> which is (4) __________ believed in this department and is (5) __________ a matter of record
> anywhere. (6) __________ to sources, a complaint was made in 2019. The complaints register
> for 2019 is three lines long and contains no such entry, (7) __________ of which has been
> established, and all of which has been repeated.

### 3E · Use — guided

Rewrite each one as a passive report. **Use the shape in brackets.**

1. People say he was asked to leave. *(It…)*
2. People say he was asked to leave. *(He…)*
3. Everyone believes the appointment was rushed. *(It…)*
4. They understand the claim started elsewhere. *(The claim…)*
5. Nobody has proved any of it. *(none of which…)*

### 3F · Use — open

**In pairs.** Report something going round your institution, in six sentences, four of which must
use a passive report. **Your partner's job** is to ask, after each one, *who says so* — and to
note how many times you cannot answer.

### 3G · Error autopsy


![Two panels comparing he is said that he left with he is said to have left.](figures/fig_b13_u07_p03_v11.png){width=6.6in}

*The passive report with two subjects — The passive report with two subjects*


**Read both versions. Then say what the first one claims that the writer did not mean.**

### 3H · Check — eight items

> 1. ______ is said that he left. *(It-shape)*
> 2. He ______ said ______ have left. *(subject-shape, past)*
> 3. He is thought ______ be fifty. *(present)*
> 4. The claim is ______ to have started elsewhere. *(understand)*
> 5. It is ______ that a complaint was made. *(allege)*
> 6. ✗ *He is said that he left.* → correct it.
> 7. ______ to sources, nobody has complained. *(preposition phrase)*
> 8. ______ of which has been established. *(relative)*

**0–4 →** Workbook pp. 30–33. **5–6 →** Part 6. **7–8 →** page 151.

## Part 4 · Speaking ◆ **C · THE PERFORMANCE**

### 4A · Fluency starter — who says so

One learner reports a claim passively. The next asks *who says so*. **The chain breaks when
somebody can actually answer.**

### 4B · The functional core — reporting what you cannot verify


![Two panels compared: three spoken versions of a claim and a four-line complaints register that does not contain it.](figures/fig_b13_u07_p04_v04.png){width=6.6in}

*Three versions, and four lines of record — Learner A has what is said. Learner B has what is written down.*


**Language Bank**

| | |
|---|---|
| **the claim** | *It is alleged that…* · *He is said to have…* · *Reportedly, …* |
| **the status** | *This is unverified.* · *None of which has been established.* |
| **what is checkable** | *What is a matter of record is…* · *As far as we know, …* |
| **the harm** | *Repeating it costs…* · *The question is who is damaged while we wait.* |

**Information gap.** Learner A has the three versions of the rumour. Learner B has the
documentary record. **Find the one element that appears in both.**

### 4C · The performance — three minutes ⏺

Report **something unverified**, from your own life or the unit. **Three minutes.** You must
include:

> the claim, reported and not endorsed · its status, said plainly ·
> one thing that is checkable, and what the record says · who is harmed while nobody checks

**Record it.**

### 4D · Observer

One person listens for **one thing**: did the speaker ever slip from reporting into endorsing?

### 4E · Three criteria

> ☐ Is every unverified claim marked as unverified?
> ☐ Did I separate what is a matter of record from what is said?
> ☐ Did I name who is harmed by the delay, and by the repetition?

## Part 5 · Reading ◆ **A · THE WORLD**

### 5A · Predict

A review, titled **"Four Weeks, at the Malthouse."**
**What do you expect the reviewer to say about the ending?** Two sentences.

### 5B · Read — two minutes and a quarter

---

**FOUR WEEKS**
*Malthouse Theatre · a review*

*Four Weeks* is built entirely from documents. There is no invented dialogue in it, which the
programme tells you twice, and which you stop noticing about ten minutes in, and then notice
again very sharply at the end.

The subject is a school teacher in a regional town, about whom something began to circulate in
2017. The play never tells us what. This is its one structural decision and everything else
follows from it: we hear eleven people describe a claim none of them can state, in the form
*it is said that*, *apparently*, *I was told*, and, nine times across ninety minutes, *there is
no smoke without fire*.

What the documents establish is this. No complaint was ever made. The teacher was moved to a
non-teaching role for what the letter calls *a period of review*, which lasted four weeks and
was then extended twice. He resigned in the eleventh week. The claim was never put to him in
writing, because there was nothing to put, and the play's most uncomfortable scene is a
nineteen-minute reconstruction of a meeting in which three senior people try to find a form of
words for a thing none of them has heard first-hand.

The second act is the town. It is also the weakest part, because the writer cannot resist the
chorus, and a chorus implies a collective that in a rumour does not exist — what exists is two
hundred separate conversations, each of which thought it was the only one.

The ending is a projection of the complaints register for that year. It is four lines long. It
is on screen for ninety seconds, which is far too long and exactly right, and the man's name is
not on it.

Go. Then notice, on the way out, how much of what you now believe about him came from a play
that was careful to tell you nothing.

---

### 5C · Comprehension — eight questions

1. What is the play built from, and what does the programme say twice?
2. **What is the play's "one structural decision"?**
3. How many people describe the claim, and in what forms?
4. **What do the documents establish? Give four facts.**
5. How long was the review period, and when did he resign?
6. **Why was the claim never put to him in writing?**
7. What is the reviewer's objection to the second act?
8. What is on screen at the end, for how long, and what is not on it?

### 5D · Vocabulary in context

Guess from the text. Then check.

> began to circulate · a period of review · put to him in writing · the writer cannot resist ·
> far too long and exactly right

### 5E · The counter-text


![Three versions of a circulating claim transcribed beside a panel record, a complaints register and an email correction.](figures/fig_b13_u07_p05_v05.png){width=6.6in}

*The three versions and the documentary record — What is said, set against what can be looked up.*


**Read the three versions of the claim and the documentary record, and answer.**

1. What does each of the three versions say?
2. **Which element appears in all three, and is it checkable?**
3. What does the panel record show about the interview?
4. How many entries are in the 2019 complaints register, and what are they?
5. One version contains a detail that is demonstrably false. Which, and how do we know?

### 5F · Two texts

The rumour says the appointment was **rushed**.
The panel record shows **three interviewers** where the usual number is four.

**Does the record support the rumour?** Three sentences.

## Part 6 · Vocabulary Lab 2 ◆ **B · THE SYSTEM**

### The adverbs that put a claim at arm's length

### 6A · Sort — how far away does each one hold it?

Three columns: **neutral · doubtful · openly sceptical**.

> reportedly · ostensibly · apparently · supposedly · purportedly · allegedly ·
> seemingly · presumably · arguably

### 6B · Chunk completion

1. **is ______ to** have left at short notice *(think)*
2. **is ______ to** be fifty *(say)*
3. **is ______ to** have been interviewed twice *(believe)*
4. **is ______ to** have started elsewhere *(understand)*
5. **it is ______ that** a complaint was made *(allege)*
6. **______ to sources** , nobody has complained

### 6C · Three that are not neutral at all

**supposedly · ostensibly · purportedly**

> All three signal that the writer does not believe it. *Ostensibly* adds that the stated
> reason is a cover for another one. **Using any of them is taking a position**, which is
> exactly what the other six do not do.

**Write three sentences about the play in Part 5, one with each.**

### 6D · Two verbs for what a claim does

> **p** **put about** · **p** **stand up**

> *A story was put about that he had been asked to leave.*
> *None of it stood up.*

**Put about** names a deliberate act with no named actor. **Stand up** is what a claim does
under checking, and it is almost always negative: *did not stand up*.

**Rewrite two sentences from Part 5B using them.**

### 6E · The fixed expressions

> **f** **None of which has been established.**

A whole sentence, attached to everything before it. It is the single most useful six words in
this unit and the one most often left out.

> **Write the three sentences it is attached to.**

### 6F · Contrast Clinic

Eight items. **Four are wrong. Correct them and say what the rule is.**

1. He is believed to be fifty.
2. It is thought him to be fifty.
3. He is said that he left.
4. None of which have been established.
5. It is said that he left.
6. He is said to have left.
7. He is thought to have been interviewed by two people.
8. According to sources, nobody has complained.

## Part 7 · Pronunciation & Fluency Lab ◆ **B · THE SYSTEM**

### Weak forms in a passive chain


![Four passive reporting phrases marked for stress, showing the weak forms between the two stressed words.](figures/fig_b13_u07_p07_v10.png){width=6.6in}

*Two stresses, six weak syllables — A passive chain is almost all weak forms, and that is what makes it fast.*


### 7A · Hear 🔊 Track 7.3

> *He is* **SAID** *to have been* **ASKED** *to leave.*
> **Eight words. Two of them are stressed. *to have been* is one blurred syllable and a half.**

Listen again for the shape of it:

> /hiz **sed** təv bin **ɑːskt** tə liːv/

### 7B · Say

Say it slowly, then at speed. Then say this one three times, faster:

> *The claim is understood to have originated in a different department.*

### 7C · Make it mean something

Say these two:

> *He is said to* **BE** *fifty.* *(now)*
> *He is said to have* **BEEN** *fifty.* *(then — and he is older now)*

**One syllable, twenty years.** Which is which?

### 7D · Fluency 30 ⏺

Thirty seconds: **four passive reports, each with the weak forms properly weak.**
Three times, faster, keeping the two stresses.

## Part 8 · Writing ◆ **C · THE PERFORMANCE**

### A report of an unverified claim · 190 words


![A cutaway of a written report in four layers from the claim and its status to the checkable record and the harm.](figures/fig_b13_u07_p08_v02.png){width=6.6in}

*A report of an unverified claim, in section — Four layers, and the second one comes before any detail of the first.*


### 8A · Read the model

> It is said in the department that the appointment was rushed, and that the person appointed
> was asked to leave a previous post. `[the claim, reported, with no owner — because there is no
> owner]`
>
> None of which has been established. No complaint has been made, no date has been given, and
> no person has yet repeated it to me who heard it first-hand. `[the status, in four flat
> clauses, before anything else]`
>
> What is a matter of record is that the appointment panel had three members rather than the
> usual four, and that the fourth withdrew eight days before, in writing, citing workload.
> `[the checkable fact, which is smaller and duller than the rumour and is the only solid thing
> here]`
>
> Three weeks of this have cost one person their teaching allocation for the semester and have
> cost the department nothing, which is the asymmetry that makes rumours efficient.
> `[the harm, named, with the asymmetry stated]`

**Questions about the writer's choices:**

1. The status comes before the evidence. **Why not after?**
2. The checkable fact is "smaller and duller". **Why include it at all?**
3. The last paragraph names an asymmetry. **What would the report be without it?**

### 8B · The anti-model

> There have been some concerns raised about the recent appointment and a number of people have
> expressed disquiet. While nothing has been proven, it is felt that the matter should be looked
> into, and it is understood that steps are being taken. Further information will be provided
> in due course.

Correct English, and it repeats the claim while appearing not to. **Find the four structures
that have deleted a person**, and say what *it is felt* achieves that *I think* would not.

### 8C · Plan

> the claim, reported → the status, flat and early → what is a matter of record →
> the harm, and who carries it

### 8D · Write — 190 words
### 8E · Peer review — three criteria

> ☐ Is the status of the claim given before any detail of it?
> ☐ Is every checkable fact separated from every reported one?
> ☐ Is the harm named, with the person who carries it?

### 8F · Write it again · 8G · Publish

**Do not publish this one on the wall.** Hand it to one person and ask them one question: would
you be comfortable if the subject read it?

## Part 9 · The File ◆ **A · THE WORLD + C · THE PERFORMANCE**

# WORK FILE · The Safety Brief


![Four versions of a workplace safety brief compared for compliance, recall, behaviour and whether anybody afterwards reported anything.](figures/fig_b13_u07_p09_v05.png){width=6.6in}

*One safety brief, four deliveries — The same four minutes, and only one of them produces a report of a near miss.*


### 9A · Four minutes that nobody listens to 🔊 Track 7.4

The same safety brief, delivered four ways. **Listen.**

1. Which one do people remember afterwards, and why?
2. **Which one is legally complete and operationally useless?**
3. One of the four asks a question. What does that change?

### 9B · What a brief is actually for

> **compliance** — it was delivered, and there is a signature
> **recall** — people can repeat it tomorrow
> **behaviour** — people do the thing, under pressure, without thinking
> **reporting** — people tell you when something goes wrong

**Which of the four does your institution measure, and which does it need?**

### 9C · The fourth one is the hard one

A brief that produces **reporting** is a brief after which somebody will tell you about a near
miss. That requires people to believe nothing bad happens to the person who comes forward, and
no form has ever achieved that.

**What would convince you, in a new job, that it was safe to report something?**

### 9D · Your turn

In fours. **Twelve minutes.** Deliver a sixty-second safety brief for this room: exits, hazards,
what to do, who to tell. **Then test each other on it from memory, cold.**

### 9E · Transfer

**Find the safety notice nearest to where you are sitting.** When was it last changed, and can
anybody in the room say what it says?

![An infographic from week one to two years later showing how three versions spread, the note, the Stage 1 process and the outcome.](figures/fig_b13_u07_p10_v12.png){width=6.6in}

*Three weeks, six weeks, two years — Half the class sees the versions. Half sees the record. Rebuild it by speaking.*


### 10A · Relay

Half the class sees the three versions. Half sees the documentary record.
**Rebuild the three weeks by speaking.** Twelve minutes.

### 10B · Simplify

Explain to a new colleague what is known and what is not. **Five sentences only.**

> **Plan:** what is said → what is on record → what has been established →
> what has happened to the person → what you would advise them to do

### 10C · Compress

The review in Part 5B into **forty words**, keeping the last paragraph's point.

### 10D · Online

Write **one message** in a group chat where the rumour has just been repeated. You are not
defending anybody, you are asking one question, and you are not going to send a second message.

### 10E · Your language

Does your language have a reportative form — a verb ending or particle meaning *they say*?
**Say *he is said to have left* in both languages.** Which marks it on the verb, and which needs
a whole clause?

## Part 11 · The Decision ◆ **C · THE PERFORMANCE**

# Three weeks, three versions, no complaint

Something has been going round for three weeks. No complaint has been made. The person has
already lost a teaching allocation because a timetable was quietly rearranged. The head of
department has to do something or decide not to.

- **Investigate.** Open a Stage 1 concern, interview the people who have repeated it, and make
  it a matter of record. It becomes real, with a file, and a file does not close.
- **Do nothing formal.** Rumours die down. This one has no source and no complainant, and
  opening a process creates a document where there is currently only talk.
- **Go to the person.** Tell them exactly what is being said, in writing, today — before any
  process, so they are not the last to know and can decide for themselves what happens next.

### 11A · The evidence


![A line chart showing the number of people who had heard each of three versions of a rumour over three weeks, with first-hand hearers flat at one.](figures/fig_b13_u07_p11_v06.png){width=6.6in}

*How three versions spread, week by week — People who had heard each version, and people who had heard it first-hand.*


1. **The three versions and the record** from Part 5E — and the detail that is demonstrably
   false.
2. **The chart** — how the three versions spread, week by week, and how many people heard each
   one first-hand.
3. **The voice.** The person concerned, thirty seconds: *"I found out in week three from a
   student. Not a colleague — a student, who was embarrassed. I would have preferred almost any
   process to that, including one that found against me, because at least I would have been in
   the room."*

### 11B · Talk about it

In fours. Everybody speaks twice. Use the frame.

> *It is ______ that ______ , none of which ______ . What is a matter of record is ______ .*

**Words you can use:** *rumour · hearsay · allegation · corroboration · anonymity · due
process · harm · unverified · credible · substantiate*

### 11C · Decide

Write **three sentences**. Choose, say why, and name what it costs.

> I think the head of department should ______________________ ,
> because ______________________ .
> What this costs is ______________________ .

### 11D · Model decision

> Go to the person, today, in writing, before anything else. Every version of this problem that
> begins with an investigation ends with the subject learning about it from somebody who is
> enjoying it. Telling them first costs the department its control of the timing and gives the
> person the one thing nobody in a rumour ever has, which is the ability to ask a question of
> somebody who has to answer. The investigation may still follow. It should not come first.

### 11E · The Reveal

> **What happened.** She went to him on the Thursday with a one-page note listing the three
> versions in the words she had heard them. He read it, asked for an hour, and came back with a
> request: open the Stage 1 himself, as the person concerned, so that the file would say he had
> asked for it.
>
> The process took six weeks and found nothing, because there was nothing. One of the three
> versions was traced to a confusion with a different person of the same surname at a different
> university, which had been corrected in an email in week one — an email that nine people
> received and none of them forwarded.
>
> He stayed two more years and then left, for a job he had applied for before any of this.
> Asked afterwards what had helped, he named the note, and said the thing that still bothered
> him was the nine people, none of whom had done anything wrong, and all of whom had read the
> correction.

**Nobody lied, nobody acted maliciously, and a correction that nine people read reached nobody.
The whole thing cost one person a semester and the department a sentence in a file.**

> **Was it the right decision?** Say why — and say what should have happened in week one.

> **THE CLAIM** · rumour · hearsay · allegation · unverified · credible · circulate · go round · put about
> **THE CHECKING** · consensus · corroboration · substantiate · stand up · no evidence that · a matter of record · look into it · take it seriously
> **THE PEOPLE** · anonymity · due process · harm · come forward · hush up · die down
> **WHAT PEOPLE SAY** · it is said that · widely believed · there is no smoke without fire · somebody told somebody
> **AT ARM'S LENGTH** · reportedly · allegedly · apparently · supposedly · ostensibly · purportedly · seemingly · arguably · presumably
> **THE STRUCTURES** · understood · thought · is thought to · is said to · is believed to · is understood to · it is alleged that · according to sources · as far as we know · none of which has been established

## Part 0 · The Big Picture ◆ **A · THE WORLD**


![An administration building with three office doors, a pending tray, a flowchart on a noticeboard and a handover note under a keyboard.](figures/fig_b13_u08_p00_v01.png){width=6.6in}

*The corridor with three doors, week twelve — Twelve things to find. Four are a warning. Four are places a warning can stop.*


# What should you have done?

**By the end of this unit you can write a post-mortem of a decision that went wrong.**

| ◆ **A · THE WORLD** | A student who failed, and three warnings that each reached one person |
|---|---|
| ◆ **B · THE SYSTEM** | the hypothetical past: *should / could / would / ought to have* · 24 words for decisions, hindsight and audit · 21 criticism forms · *should've* /ʃʊdəv/ |
| ◆ **C · THE PERFORMANCE** | A post-mortem that finds a system fault, not a person to blame |

---

### 0A · Find it

**Find twelve things in this picture.** Four of them are a warning. Four are the places a
warning can stop.

> an inbox with 2,114 unread · a corridor with three office doors · a form half filled in ·
> a shared drive folder dated two years ago · a desk with a tray marked *pending* ·
> a noticeboard with a flowchart on it · a phone with a missed call ·
> a printed attendance list with four crosses in a row · a whiteboard with a name and a date ·
> a handover note under a keyboard · a meeting that four people are in and five were invited to ·
> an email marked *FYI*

### 0B · Guess it

**Three of the twelve are the same warning, in three different places.** Which, and which of the
three was anybody responsible for reading?

### 0C · Say it ⏺

**Record forty-five seconds.** *Describe something that went wrong around you where everybody
did their own job correctly.* You will record it again at the end of this unit.

## Part 1 · Vocabulary Lab 1 ◆ **B · THE SYSTEM**


![A network of three separate warnings each reaching one person, with the point where they would have been combined shown as an empty node.](figures/fig_b13_u08_p01_v08.png){width=6.6in}

*Three warnings, and the place none of them met — Six nodes. Every arrow is correct. The node in the middle does not exist.*


### 1A · Meet — the words of a post-mortem

> **decision** · **hindsight** · **audit** · **threshold** · **handover** · **oversight** ·
> **accountability** · **omission**

1. Which two of these are moments, and which two are states?
2. ***Oversight* means two opposite things.** Give both, and say how you tell them apart.
3. What is a *threshold*, and why does every automatic warning need one?
4. **Why is a *handover* the most dangerous five minutes in any process?**

> **An *omission* is not the same as a mistake.** A mistake is a thing done wrongly. An omission
> is a thing not done, and no log anywhere records it.

### 1B · Sort — the system, the person, or the aftermath?

> with hindsight · in retrospect · nobody owned it · the duty of care · warning ·
> fall through the cracks · flag it up · escalate · trigger · review

**Three columns. Two of them belong in more than one.** Say which and why.

### 1C · Meet — the phrase that does most of the work

> **c** **nobody owned it**

Not *nobody did it*. Everybody did a piece of it, correctly, and no single person was
responsible for the whole.

1. **Why is this harder to fix than somebody doing something wrong?**
2. Name one thing in your own life that nobody owns.

### 1D · Collocation Lab

| | |
|---|---|
| **flag it up** | ~ early · ~ to somebody |
| **with hindsight** | ~ it was obvious · ~ , we would have |
| **the duty of care** | owe ~ · breach ~ |
| **fall through the cracks** | ~ between two teams · let somebody ~ |

**Which two of these can only be said afterwards?**

### 1E · The fixed expressions

> **f** **Three people, three emails.** — the shape of the most common institutional failure
> there is, and it fits in four words.
> **f** **Somebody should have.** — a sentence with no subject, and that is the diagnosis as
> well as the complaint.

**Which one names the fault, and which one performs it?**

### 1F · Own it

The four phrasal verbs of a warning.

> **pass on** · **follow up** · **sign off** · **take up**

1. Which two end somebody's responsibility, and which two extend it?
2. **What exactly do you *sign off* — the work, or the risk?**
3. *Follow up* and *check*: what does *follow up* assume that *check* does not?
4. Write one sentence in which somebody **passes on** a warning and makes it weaker.

## Part 2 · Listening ◆ **A · THE WORLD**


![A coordinator and a review panel chair across a table, their speech shown as hypothetical past forms.](figures/fig_b13_u08_p02_v07.png){width=6.6in}

*The review meeting, week fourteen — One of them held the week-nine email. One of them is writing the finding.*


### 2A · The review meeting 🔊 Track 8.1

**Before you listen.** A second-year student has failed three units and withdrawn. Three
separate warnings were generated in the twelve weeks before. **Guess: how many people saw all
three?**

**Listen once. One question.** Who generated each warning, and who received it?

**Listen again. Complete the table.**

| | Warning 1 | Warning 2 | Warning 3 |
|---|---|---|---|
| week | | | |
| triggered by | | | |
| went to | | | |
| action taken | | | |

**Listen again. Choose the best answer.**

1. The three warnings went to **two people / one person / three people**.
2. The threshold for an automatic alert is **six / four / three absences**.
3. *It was not my responsibility* is said **and is wrong / and nobody answers / and is right**.
4. The handover happened in **week 2 / week 9 / week 6**.

**Noticing.** Four sentences from the recording.

1. *Somebody should have put the three together, and nobody did.*
2. *I could have asked, but there was nothing in front of me that said to.*
3. *With hindsight the handover ought to have included the open cases.*
4. *You needn't have carried that one — it was never yours.*

**All four judge a past action. Which one withdraws a criticism?**

### 2B · Decoding clinic 🔊 Track 8.2

Thirty seconds of the review. Four jobs.

1. How many *have* forms can you count?
2. In *should have put*, how is *have* pronounced?
3. Catch the two week numbers.
4. One speaker says *I could have* and means something different from *I should have*. What?

> Transcripts, page 157.

## Part 3 · Grammar Lab 1 ◆ **B · THE SYSTEM**

### Judging the past you cannot change

### 3A · Notice

Look at the four sentences in Part 2.

1. In sentence 1, did anybody put them together?
2. **In sentence 2, was the speaker able to ask?** Did they?
3. **Rewrite sentence 3 with *should*.** Does the meaning change?
4. Sentence 4 uses *needn't have*. **Did the person do it or not?**
5. Try *"Somebody should have put the three together, and nobody didn't."* **What is wrong?**

### 3B · See


![A vertical ladder of hypothetical past forms arranged by whether the action happened, with needn't have at the top and should have below.](figures/fig_b13_u08_p03_v09.png){width=6.6in}

*Did it happen, or not? — Five of these say it did not. One says it did, and it is the one everybody gets wrong.*


### 3C · Know

| Form | Means | Did it happen? |
|---|---|---|
| ***should have* + pp** | it was the right thing and it did not happen | no |
| ***ought to have* + pp** | the same, slightly more formal, often about duty | no |
| ***could have* + pp** | it was possible and it did not happen | no |
| ***would have* + pp** | it was the consequence of a condition that did not hold | no |
| ***might have* + pp** | it was possible, and we do not know | unknown |
| ***needn't have* + pp** | it happened, and it was unnecessary | **yes** |

> **The one that catches everybody.** *Needn't have done* means it was done. *Did not need to
> do* means it was not. *She needn't have come in* — she came. *She did not need to come in* —
> and probably stayed at home.

> ***could have* is not a criticism by itself.** *I could have asked* says it was possible.
> Whether it is an accusation depends entirely on what follows: *I could have asked, but nothing
> told me to* is a defence.

> **Use *otherwise* to carry the condition.** *The threshold should have been four. Otherwise
> nothing fires until it is too late.*

> **Why it matters.** A post-mortem written in *should have* is a list of accusations. One
> written in *could have* and *would have* is a description of a system, and only the second
> kind changes anything.

### 3D · Use — controlled

> Three separate warnings were generated. Each one went to one person, and each of those people
> did exactly what the procedure asked. Somebody (1) __________ have put the three together, and
> (2) __________ . The tutor (3) __________ have asked, but nothing in front of her said to.
> With hindsight the handover in week six (4) __________ to have included the open cases.
> The threshold (5) __________ have been four absences rather than six; (6) __________ nothing
> fires until the student has already missed a third of the semester. The registry
> (7) __________ have chased the second form at all — it had already been superseded, and
> chasing it cost two days that nobody had.

### 3E · Use — guided

Rewrite each one with the form in brackets.

1. It was the right thing to do and nobody did it. *(should have)*
2. It was possible and she did not do it. *(could have)*
3. She did it and it was unnecessary. *(needn't have)*
4. It was her duty and it did not happen. *(ought to have)*
5. If the threshold had been four, it would have fired in week three. *(would have)*

### 3F · Use — open

**In pairs.** Describe something that went wrong, in six sentences, three of which must use a
hypothetical past. **Your partner's job** is to say, after each one, whether it describes a
person or a system.

### 3G · Error autopsy


![Two panels comparing she needn't have come in with she did not need to come in.](figures/fig_b13_u08_p03_v11.png){width=6.6in}

*needn't have, and the Thursday it describes — needn't have, and the Thursday it describes*


**Read both versions. Then say whether the person came in or not.**

### 3H · Check — eight items

> 1. Somebody ______ have put the three together. *(right thing, did not happen)*
> 2. She ______ have asked, but nothing told her to. *(possible)*
> 3. The handover ______ to have included the open cases. *(duty)*
> 4. You ______ have carried that one — it was never yours. *(it happened, unnecessarily)*
> 5. If the threshold had been four, it ______ have fired in week three. *(consequence)*
> 6. ✗ *Somebody should have put them together, and nobody didn't.* → correct it.
> 7. The threshold should have been four; ______ , nothing fires in time. *(condition carried)*
> 8. It ______ well have been missed twice. *(possible, unknown)*

**0–4 →** Workbook pp. 34–37. **5–6 →** Part 6. **7–8 →** page 173.

## Part 4 · Speaking ◆ **C · THE PERFORMANCE**

### 4A · Fluency starter — person or system

One learner gives a *should have* sentence. The next rewrites it so that it describes a system
rather than a person. **The chain breaks when the system version still has a name in it.**

### 4B · The functional core — running a post-mortem


![Two panels compared: three dated warnings and three role descriptions, with the task that appears in neither marked.](figures/fig_b13_u08_p04_v04.png){width=6.6in}

*Three warnings, three job descriptions — Learner A has what fired. Learner B has what each role says.*


**Language Bank**

| | |
|---|---|
| **the sequence** | *In week three… · By week six… · Not until week nine…* |
| **what was possible** | *X could have…, but nothing in front of them said to.* |
| **the system fault** | *Nobody owned it.* · *The threshold was set at six.* |
| **the withdrawal** | *You needn't have…* · *That one was never yours.* |

**Information gap.** Learner A has the three warnings. Learner B has the three job descriptions.
**Find the task that appears in none of the three.**

### 4C · The performance — three minutes ⏺

Run **a post-mortem** on something that went wrong. **Three minutes.** You must include:

> the sequence, with dates · what each person could have done and why they did not ·
> the system fault, named without a person in it · one thing you are withdrawing

**Record it.**

### 4D · Observer

One person listens for **one thing**: did any sentence turn a system fault back into a person?

### 4E · Three criteria

> ☐ Is the sequence dated?
> ☐ Is the main fault stated without a name in it?
> ☐ Did I withdraw at least one criticism that does not survive the evidence?

## Part 5 · Reading ◆ **A · THE WORLD**

### 5A · Predict

An opinion column, titled **"Stop asking who."**
**What do you expect it to propose instead?** Two sentences.

### 5B · Read — two minutes and a quarter

---

**STOP ASKING WHO**
*opinion*

Every institutional failure I have read about in twenty years ends the same way: a report, a
name, and a sentence beginning *should have*. And every one of them produces the same two
results, which are that one person's career is damaged and the thing happens again within four
years.

I want to be careful here, because there is a version of what I am saying that is an excuse for
everybody, and that is not it. People are responsible for what they do. The question is whether
*who* is the most useful question to ask first, and I think the evidence is that it is not.

Take the case everybody in this sector knows. A student missed four consecutive classes in week
three. An automatic alert fired, because the threshold is four, and it went to the unit
coordinator. In week six the coordinator changed, as coordinators do, and the handover document
had a section for assessment deadlines and no section for open student cases. In week nine the
student's personal tutor received a separate flag, from a different system, and emailed the
coordinator — the new one — who replied the same day asking for context she did not have.

Three people. Three emails. Each of them did what their role described. The student withdrew in
week eleven.

The report named the second coordinator. With hindsight, it said, she ought to have asked a
follow-up question. She ought to have. She also ought to have, in the same week, prepared two
hundred and ten exam scripts and run a staff recruitment panel, and if we are listing the things
that a reasonable person ought to have done in week nine we are going to be here for some time.

What nobody asked was why a handover template that runs to four pages has no line on it for
open cases. That is a fixable sentence in a Word document. It does not require anybody to be
braver, or more diligent, or to have more hours in the day, and it would have worked in week
six without anybody noticing it had.

Ask *who* second. Ask it properly, and name people when they have earned it. But ask *what*
first, because *what* is the only one of the two questions that has an answer you can act on
next Tuesday.

---

### 5C · Comprehension — eight questions

1. What does the writer say every institutional failure report ends with?
2. **What are the "two results", and what is the time frame on the second?**
3. What is the version of the argument the writer is careful to reject?
4. **Describe the three warnings: when, from where, and to whom?**
5. What was missing from the handover document?
6. **What does the writer say about "she ought to have asked a follow-up question"?**
7. What does the writer say nobody asked, and why is it fixable?
8. What is the final recommendation, in the writer's own order?

### 5D · Vocabulary in context

Guess from the text. Then check.

> as coordinators do · a separate flag · context she did not have · earned it ·
> act on next Tuesday

### 5E · The counter-text


![Three automated warnings with their recipients and dates, beside an eleven-section handover template and an email reply asking for context.](figures/fig_b13_u08_p05_v05.png){width=6.6in}

*The three warnings and the handover template — Everything here was generated, delivered and filed correctly.*


**Read the three warnings and the handover template, and answer.**

1. What triggered each of the three warnings, and when?
2. **Which of the three reached somebody who had seen either of the others?**
3. What are the eleven sections of the handover template?
4. What is the attendance threshold, and what would four have changed?
5. One of the three emails has a reply. What does it ask for, and could it have been answered?

### 5F · Two texts

The column says the handover template has **no line for open cases**.
The template has **eleven sections** and runs to **four pages**.

**Is that an oversight or a design decision?** Three sentences.

## Part 6 · Vocabulary Lab 2 ◆ **B · THE SYSTEM**

### Criticising the past precisely

### 6A · Sort — how certain is each one?

Three columns: **certain · probable · possible**.

> clearly · should have · conceivably · foreseeably · could have · ought to have ·
> demonstrably · might well have

### 6B · Chunk completion

1. **______ have** put the three together *(right thing, undone)*
2. **______ have** asked, but nothing said to *(possible)*
3. **______ to have** included the open cases *(duty)*
4. **______ have** carried that one *(done, and unnecessary)*
5. **might ______ have** been missed twice *(possible, unknown)*
6. **in ______ case** , the threshold is the fault

### 6C · Two that sound like evidence

**clearly · demonstrably**

> *Clearly* is a claim about how obvious something is, and it is often used when nothing has
> been shown. *Demonstrably* says somebody has shown it. **Only one of them should survive a
> careful edit of a post-mortem.** Which, and why?

**Write two sentences about the column in Part 5, one with each.**

### 6D · Two that carry a consequence

> **c** **otherwise** · **c** **all the more so**

> *The threshold should have been four. Otherwise nothing fires in time.*
> *A handover is hard. With two hundred scripts to mark, all the more so.*

**Otherwise** carries the unstated condition. **All the more so** intensifies a point already
made. **Write one sentence with each, about week six.**

### 6E · The fixed expressions

> **f** **And nobody did.**

Three words, placed after a *should have*, and they turn an accusation into a finding.

> **Write the sentence before it, twice — once naming a person and once not.** Which one works?

### 6F · Contrast Clinic

Eight items. **Four are wrong. Correct them and say what the rule is.**

1. He should have asked, and he didn't.
2. She needn't have come in, but she did.
3. She needn't have come in — so she stayed at home.
4. He could have asked, and nothing told him to, so it is his fault.
5. He might well have been missed twice.
6. It ought to have included the open cases.
7. It ought have included the open cases.
8. He should asked.

## Part 7 · Pronunciation & Fluency Lab ◆ **B · THE SYSTEM**

### *should've*, and why it is not *should of*


![Four hypothetical past phrases marked for stress, showing have reduced to a single weak syllable.](figures/fig_b13_u08_p07_v10.png){width=6.6in}

*should've, could've, ought to've — One stressed syllable, then a schwa. This is why the wrong spelling exists.*


### 7A · Hear 🔊 Track 8.3

> *She should've asked.* /ʃʊdəv/
> **One weak syllable. It sounds exactly like *should of*, which is why that spelling exists
> and why it is wrong.**

Listen to the set:

> /ʃʊdəv/ · /kʊdəv/ · /wʊdəv/ · /ɔːtəv/ · /maɪtəv/

### 7B · Say

Say all five. Then say this one three times, faster:

> *Somebody should've put the three together, and nobody did.*

### 7C · Make it mean something

Say these two:

> *She needn't have* **COME** *in.* *(she came — and it was wasted)*
> *She didn't need to come in.* *(and she probably did not)*

**One structure, two Thursdays.** Which is which?

### 7D · Fluency 30 ⏺

Thirty seconds: **four hypothetical pasts, all with weak *have*.**
Three times, faster, keeping the schwa.

## Part 8 · Writing ◆ **C · THE PERFORMANCE**

### A post-mortem · 200 words


![A cutaway of a written post-mortem in four layers from the dated sequence to the system fault and the withdrawal.](figures/fig_b13_u08_p08_v02.png){width=6.6in}

*A post-mortem, in section — Four layers. There is no name in the third one, and that is the deliberate part.*


### 8A · Read the model

> In week three an automatic alert fired after four consecutive absences and went to the unit
> coordinator. In week six the coordinator changed. In week nine a second flag, from a different
> system, reached the new coordinator, who asked for context and received none.
> `[the sequence, dated, with no adjectives in it at all]`
>
> Each of the three people did what their role described. The second coordinator could have
> asked a follow-up question, and nothing in front of her indicated that one was outstanding.
> `[what was possible, and why it was not obvious — and this is where could have belongs]`
>
> The handover template has eleven sections and none of them is open student cases. Nobody owned
> the sequence, because no role description contains it. `[the system fault, stated twice, with
> no name in either sentence]`
>
> The first coordinator needn't have been named in the draft report. She had left the role
> eleven weeks before the withdrawal. `[the withdrawal, and it costs the writer something]`

**Questions about the writer's choices:**

1. The first paragraph has no adjectives. **Why not?**
2. *Could have* appears once and *should have* not at all. **What does that do?**
3. The last paragraph withdraws something. **What does a post-mortem without one look like?**

### 8B · The anti-model

> Unfortunately there was a breakdown in communication and the student fell through the cracks.
> Lessons have been learned and procedures will be reviewed to ensure that this does not happen
> again. All staff are reminded of the importance of following up on student welfare concerns.

Correct English, and nothing in it will stop this happening again. **Name the three things it
has turned into weather**, and say what *all staff are reminded* is a substitute for.

### 8C · Plan

> the sequence, dated and flat → what was possible, and why it was not obvious →
> the system fault, with no name in it → one withdrawal

### 8D · Write — 200 words
### 8E · Peer review — three criteria

> ☐ Is the sequence dated and free of adjectives?
> ☐ Is the main fault stated without a person in it?
> ☐ Is there a withdrawal, and does it cost the writer something?

### 8F · Write it again · 8G · Publish

Send it to the people named in it, before anybody else. **That is the test.**

## Part 9 · The File ◆ **A · THE WORLD + C · THE PERFORMANCE**

# STUDY FILE · The Literature Review


![Four students compared on how much they read, when they started writing and what their literature review did.](figures/fig_b13_u08_p09_v05.png){width=6.6in}

*Four literature reviews, one reading list — The one who read the most wrote the worst of the four.*


### 9A · Four people describe the same task 🔊 Track 8.4

Four students describe writing a literature review. **Listen.**

1. Which one read the most and wrote the worst one?
2. **Which one started writing first, and did that help?**
3. One of them describes a method that sounds lazy and is not. Which?

### 9B · What a literature review is not

> **not** a list of what everybody said
> **not** a chronology
> **not** a demonstration that you have read a lot
> **it is** a map of where the disagreements are, and where your question sits on it

**Which of the three *nots* is hardest to give up, and why?**

### 9C · The method that sounds lazy

Read five. **Then write the disagreement between them in one sentence.** Then read five more and
see whether the sentence survives. Most do not, and the ones that do are the structure of your
review.

**Try it with anything you are reading this week.**

### 9D · Your turn

In fours. **Twelve minutes.** Take any five sources you all know — news articles will do — and
write the one-sentence disagreement. **Compare the four sentences.** Which is a disagreement and
which is a topic?

### 9E · Transfer

**Find the literature review in any paper you have to read**, and mark the sentence where the
writer's own question first appears. How many pages in is it?

## Part 11 · The Decision ◆ **C · THE PERFORMANCE**

# Three warnings, three people, one student

A student withdrew in week eleven after failing three units. Three warnings were generated.
Each reached one person. Each of those people followed their role description. The review
panel has to write a finding.

- **Name the second coordinator.** She received the week-nine email, asked for context and did
  not chase it. It is the only point in twelve weeks where one person held enough to act.
- **Find no individual fault.** Three people, three roles, three correct actions. Record it as a
  process failure and recommend a template change.
- **Name the process and the gap.** No individual fault, and a specific, named omission: nobody
  owns the sequence, because no role description contains it, and that is a decision somebody
  made in 2016.

### 11A · The evidence


![A bar chart of students reaching two, three, four and six consecutive absences and the number in each group who later withdrew.](figures/fig_b13_u08_p11_v06.png){width=6.6in}

*Absence thresholds and what each one would catch — Students reaching each threshold last year, and how many of them withdrew.*


1. **The three warnings and the handover template** from Part 5E — and the reply that asks for
   context.
2. **The chart** — how many students hit each attendance threshold, and how many withdrew.
3. **The voice.** The second coordinator, thirty seconds: *"I am not going to say I did nothing
   wrong, because a student withdrew. What I will say is that I asked for context and the system
   that sent me the flag could have attached it, and in the eleven weeks since, nobody has asked
   me what the email actually said."*

### 11B · Talk about it

In fours. Everybody speaks twice. Use the frame.

> *______ should have ______ , and nobody did. ______ could have ______ , but ______ .*

**Words you can use:** *threshold · handover · escalate · oversight · accountability ·
omission · with hindsight · in retrospect · nobody owned it · the duty of care*

### 11C · Decide

Write **three sentences**. Choose, say why, and name what it costs.

> I think the panel should ______________________ ,
> because ______________________ .
> What this costs is ______________________ .

### 11D · Model decision

> Name the process and the gap, and date the gap. A finding of no individual fault reads as a
> cover unless it says precisely what was missing and when it went missing, and *nobody owned
> the sequence* is only useful if it is followed by *because the 2016 role review removed case
> continuity from the coordinator description and did not put it anywhere else*. That sentence
> has a year in it and a document behind it. It is also the only version of this finding that
> somebody can act on without waiting for anybody to be braver.

### 11E · The Reveal

> **What happened.** The panel found no individual fault and recommended a line be added to the
> handover template. The line was added in the following July: *Open student cases, with dates*.
>
> It fired for the first time in the second week of the new semester, when a coordinator
> changing mid-unit listed two open cases, one of which had been open since the previous
> October. Nobody had done anything wrong about that one either.
>
> The second coordinator was not named in the final report. She left the university eighteen
> months later and said in her exit interview that the thing which had stayed with her was not
> the review but the eleven weeks before it, during which four people asked her what had
> happened and none of them asked to see the email.

**One line in a Word document, added in July, found a case that had been open since October.
And the only person who was nearly named is the only person anybody remembers.**

> **Was it the right decision?** Say why — and say what the panel owed the second coordinator.

## Part 0 · The Big Picture ◆ **A · THE WORLD**


![A science building with a lab bench and a marked report, beside a canteen table with six people and four languages, and two signs saying the same thing differently.](figures/fig_b13_u09_p00_v01.png){width=6.6in}

*The science building and the canteen, one o'clock — Twelve things to find. Four of them are one register. Four are another.*


# Can you put that another way?

**By the end of this unit you can say the same thing three ways, for three audiences.**

| ◆ **A · THE WORLD** | A lab report that is factually perfect and has lost four marks for its English |
|---|---|
| ◆ **B · THE SYSTEM** | phrasal verbs: separable, inseparable, three-part · 24 words for register and identity · 21 particles and combinations · stress on the particle |
| ◆ **C · THE PERFORMANCE** | One idea, said three ways, with nothing lost in any of them |

**Find twelve things in this picture.** Four of them are one register. Four are another.

> a lab bench with a labelled rack · a marked report with four red brackets in the margin ·
> a style guide on a shelf · a whiteboard with a diagram and no words ·
> a poster for a research talk · a canteen table with six people and four languages ·
> a sign saying *Please do not leave glassware in the sink* ·
> a handwritten note saying *don't put glass in here* · a lanyard from a conference ·
> a laptop with a translation tab open · a door with office hours on it ·
> a shelf of dictionaries nobody has opened this year

**Two of the twelve say exactly the same thing.** Which, and which one works better where it is?

once to a twelve-year-old.* You will record it again at the end of this unit.

## Part 1 · Vocabulary Lab 1 ◆ **B · THE SYSTEM**


![A network from jargon, slang, idiom and register to who is excluded, with code-switching shown as the escape route.](figures/fig_b13_u09_p01_v08.png){width=6.6in}

*Four ways English can exclude somebody — Six nodes. Two of them are the same mechanism with different social standing.*


### 1A · Meet — the words of how we sound

> **register** · **idiom** · **colloquial** · **formal** · **jargon** · **slang** ·
> **accent** · **code-switch**

1. Which two of these are about a group rather than a situation?
2. **What is the difference between *jargon* and *slang*?** Both exclude people. How?
3. *Register* is a choice. **What is it a choice between?**
4. **Why is *code-switch* a skill rather than an inconsistency?**

> ***Formal* is not better.** It is one register of several, and using it where it does not
> belong is exactly the same kind of error as using slang in a thesis — it is just the one
> nobody is marked down for.

### 1B · Sort — the writing, the marking, or the speaker?

> native-like · the house style · fluency · penalty · marking · vocabulary ·
> plain English · mark down · in your own words

**Three columns. Two of them belong in more than one.** Say which and why.

### 1C · Meet — the phrase that does most of the work

> **c** **plain English**

Not simple English. English in which the structure of the sentence matches the structure of the
idea, so the reader does not have to hold anything while they wait.

1. **Rewrite this in plain English:** *It is not unlikely that a reduction in the frequency of
   sampling would not be without consequence for the reliability of the result.*
2. How many negatives did you remove?

### 1D · Collocation Lab

| | |
|---|---|
| **mark down** | ~ for grammar · be ~ed · ~ heavily |
| **get across** | ~ an idea · it did not ~ |
| **come across** | ~ as rude · how you ~ |
| **a turn of phrase** | an unusual ~ · a nice ~ |

**Which two of these are about the writer, and which two about the reader?**

### 1E · The fixed expressions

> **f** **It is perfectly clear.** — a sentence that settles an argument about meaning by
> refusing to have it.
> **f** **Unidiomatic is not a fault.** — the claim this unit is built on. **Do you agree?**

**Which one is a position and which one is a move?**

### 1F · Own it

The four phrasal verbs of making yourself understood.

> **get across** · **put across** · **come across** · **pick up on**

1. Three of them are about the message and one is about the listener. Which?
2. **What is the difference between *get across* and *put across*?**
3. *Come across* has two meanings. Give both.
4. Write one sentence in which somebody **picks up on** something that was not said.

*The marking office, Thursday — One of them took the four marks off. One of them has seen eleven of these.*

|---|---|---|---|
| what the report does well | | | |
| what the four marks are for | | | |
| what should change | | | |

## Part 3 · Grammar Lab 1 ◆ **B · THE SYSTEM**

### Phrasal verbs, and where the object can go

### 3A · Notice

Look at the four sentences in Part 2.

1. In sentence 1, can you say *put it up with*?
2. **In sentence 3, can you say *got across the idea*?** Can you say *got it across*?
3. **Put a pronoun object into each of the four.** Which ones force you to split the verb?
4. Sentence 4 has a three-part verb. **How many of the three can move?**
5. Try *"She got across it."* **What is wrong with it?**

### 3B · See


![A branching diagram of four phrasal verb types showing where an object noun and an object pronoun can go in each.](figures/fig_b13_u09_p03_v09.png){width=6.6in}

*Where the object is allowed to stand — One rule is absolute. The other three are about which group the verb belongs to.*


### 3C · Know

| Type | Example | Where the object goes |
|---|---|---|
| **separable** | *mark down · bring up · take on* | either side of the particle — **but a pronoun must split it** |
| **inseparable** | *get over · come across · look into* | always after the particle |
| **three-part** | *put up with · look forward to · run out of* | always after both particles |
| **no object at all** | *stand down · die down · look back* | nothing goes anywhere |

> **The pronoun rule is the only rule here that is absolute.** With a separable verb,
> *mark the student down* and *mark down the student* are both English. ***Mark down her* is
> not.** It must be *mark her down*.

> **Three-part verbs never split.** *Put up with it*, never *put it up with*. There are no
> exceptions, which makes them the easiest group of the four.

> **The particle carries the meaning, and often the stress.** *Look up* a word, *look into* a
> claim, *look over* a draft, *look after* a child. The verb is the same. The particle is the
> whole word.

> **Why it matters.** A phrasal verb is not informal English. *Put up with* is not slang; it is
> the ordinary way to say *tolerate*, and refusing them does not make writing more formal — it
> makes it foreign.

### 3D · Use — controlled

> She got the idea (1) __________ in every paragraph, which is what the criterion asks for, and
> she was marked (2) __________ four points for expression. The chair looked (3) __________ it
> and found eleven similar cases this year. "What I cannot get (4) __________ with," she said,
> "is penalising something nobody can name." She is not going to put (5) __________
> __________ it for another semester. She has brought it (6) __________ at three meetings and is
> running (7) __________ of ways to raise it politely.

### 3E · Use — guided

Put a pronoun object into each one. **Two of them must split.**

1. The marker marked down the student.
2. She got across the idea.
3. He could not put up with the criterion.
4. The chair looked into the eleven cases.
5. They brought up the question at a meeting.

### 3F · Use — open

**In pairs.** Describe a rule you think is unfair, in six sentences, four of which must use
phrasal verbs of three different types. **Your partner's job** is to try to split each one.

### 3G · Error autopsy


![Two panels comparing he could not put it up with and he could not put up with it.](figures/fig_b13_u09_p03_v11.png){width=6.6in}

*Splitting the one that never splits — Splitting the one that never splits*


**Read both versions. Then say which group of verbs the writer has applied the wrong rule to.**

### 3H · Check — eight items

> 1. She got the idea ______ . *(separable, noun object)*
> 2. She got ______ ______ . *(separable, pronoun object)*
> 3. He could not put ______ ______ it. *(three-part)*
> 4. ✗ *He could not put it up with.* → correct it.
> 5. The chair looked ______ it. *(inseparable)*
> 6. They brought ______ ______ at the meeting. *(separable, pronoun)*
> 7. We are running ______ ______ time. *(three-part)*
> 8. He is looking ______ ______ it. *(three-part, positive)*

**0–4 →** Workbook pp. 38–41. **5–6 →** Part 6. **7–8 →** page 195.
