## Part 4 · Speaking ◆ **C · THE PERFORMANCE**

### 4A · Fluency starter — three registers

One learner says a sentence. The next says it formally. The next says it to a child.
**The chain breaks when something is lost.**

### 4B · The functional core — one idea, three audiences


![Two panels compared: a technical sentence with a measured range and a popular version in which the range has quietly disappeared.](figures/fig_b13_u09_p04_v04.png){width=6.6in}

*The same finding, two registers — Learner A has the technical version. Learner B has the version for a newspaper.*


**Language Bank**

| | |
|---|---|
| **to a specialist** | *The sampling interval was reduced to…* |
| **to a colleague elsewhere** | *We took fewer readings, which means…* |
| **to a non-specialist** | *We checked less often. Here is what that costs.* |
| **the thing that must survive** | *…and the number is the same in all three.* |

**Information gap.** Learner A has the technical version. Learner B has the version for a
newspaper. **Find the one fact that has quietly changed between them.**

### 4C · The performance — three minutes ⏺

Explain **one idea three times**. **Three minutes.** You must include:

> a specialist version · a version for an educated non-specialist · a version for a
> twelve-year-old · and the same number, unchanged, in all three

**Record it.**

### 4D · Observer

One person listens for **one thing**: did anything become false on the way to being simpler?

### 4E · Three criteria

> ☐ Is the key fact identical in all three versions?
> ☐ Did the simplest version avoid becoming inaccurate?
> ☐ Did I change register without changing content?

## Part 5 · Reading ◆ **A · THE WORLD**

### 5A · Predict

A feature, titled **"The four marks."**
**What do you expect the marker to say when asked to explain?** Two sentences.

### 5B · Read — two minutes and a quarter

---

**THE FOUR MARKS**
*a feature*

The report is on soil respiration and it is, by the standards of a second-year unit, extremely
good. The method is reproducible. The two graphs are correctly labelled. The discussion
identifies a confound the student was not taught to look for. It was marked 84, and the sheet
says: *expression — 4*.

I asked to see what the four marks came off. There are eleven criteria on the rubric and none
of them mentions language. The marker, who has taught the unit for nine years and is one of the
best teachers in the faculty, said this: "It reads as though it was translated. It is not
wrong. I could not point at a sentence and tell you it was wrong. It just is not how we say it."

I asked for an example. She found one in twenty seconds, which tells you she had noticed it
properly: *The data permits us to affirm that the respiration rate augments with temperature.*

Every word in that sentence is an English word used with its dictionary meaning. *Permits*,
*affirm*, *augments*. A chemist in 1890 would have written something close to it. What makes it
read as foreign is that current scientific English prefers *allow*, *conclude* and *increase* —
a preference, not a rule, which nobody teaches and everybody enforces.

The student is from a Francophone education system in which *augmenter* is the unmarked word.
She has not made an error. She has made a different, defensible choice in a language whose
conventions she was never shown, and she has lost four marks for it in a unit whose rubric does
not mention language at all.

Here is what makes this hard rather than simple. The marker is not wrong that it matters. A
paper written like that will be read differently by reviewers, and pretending otherwise would
not help the student. The question is not whether to tell her. It is whether to tell her in a
comment or in a number, and whether the number is allowed to exist in a rubric that does not
mention it.

Eleven reports this year have lost marks under a criterion that is not on the sheet. Nobody has
been able to name it, and everybody can recognise it in twenty seconds.

---

### 5C · Comprehension — eight questions

1. What is the report about, and what three things does the writer praise?
2. **What was the mark, and what does the sheet say?**
3. How many criteria are on the rubric, and how many mention language?
4. **Quote the marker's explanation. What does she say she cannot do?**
5. What is the example sentence, and what is wrong with it?
6. **Why is the student's choice described as "defensible"?**
7. What does the writer say makes this "hard rather than simple"?
8. What is the final pair of numbers, and what do they show together?

### 5D · Vocabulary in context

Guess from the text. Then check.

> a confound · the unmarked word · a preference, not a rule · read differently by reviewers ·
> in a comment or in a number

### 5E · The counter-text


![A marking rubric with eleven criteria beside a marked report page with four margin brackets and a faculty style guide entry.](figures/fig_b13_u09_p05_v05.png){width=6.6in}

*The rubric and the marked page — Eleven criteria, none about language, and four brackets in a margin.*


**Read the rubric and the marked page, and answer.**

1. What are the eleven criteria, and what is each worth?
2. **Where on the rubric could four marks for expression legitimately come from?**
3. What do the four margin brackets mark, in each case?
4. What does the faculty style guide say about *augment*?
5. One of the four brackets marks something that *is* an error. Which?

### 5F · Two texts

The rubric has **eleven criteria** and none of them mentions language.
Eleven reports have **lost marks** for language this year.

**Is the marking wrong, or is the rubric?** Three sentences.

## Part 6 · Vocabulary Lab 2 ◆ **B · THE SYSTEM**

### What the particle is doing

### 6A · Sort — what does each particle add?

Four columns: **completely · continuing · away from · towards**.

> down · off · back · on · over · around · up · into · away · through · out

### 6B · Chunk completion

1. **put ______ ______** the noise for another year
2. **get ______ ______** saying nothing
3. **look ______ ______** the summer
4. **run ______ ______** time
5. **go ______ ______** the decision
6. **catch ______ ______** the reading

### 6C · Two that do the same job in different registers

**do away with · abolish**

> Identical in meaning. One of them is three words and ordinary; the other is one word and
> formal. **Neither is better.** The choice is about the reader, and in a report for a committee
> the one-word version is usually right — not because it is more correct but because it is
> shorter in a document people skim.

**Write two sentences about the rubric, one with each.**

### 6D · Two that are separable, and one trap

> **p** **take over** · **p** **bring up**

> *She took the unit over.* · *She took it over.* · *She took over the unit.*
> *He brought the question up.* · *He brought it up.* · *He brought up the question.*

**All three positions work with a noun. Only one works with a pronoun. Which, and why is that
the only rule you have to remember?**

### 6E · The fixed expression

> **f** **Say that again in writing.**

Four words that change what a claim costs. It is the move the unit chair makes at the end of
Part 2, and it is also what the student should have been told in week one.

> **Write the exchange around it.**

### 6F · Contrast Clinic

Eight items. **Four are wrong. Correct them and say what the rule is.**

1. He could not put it up with.
2. She marked her down.
3. He could not put up with it.
4. She got across it.
5. She got across the idea.
6. She marked down her.
7. We are looking it forward to.
8. We are looking forward to it.

## Part 7 · Pronunciation & Fluency Lab ◆ **B · THE SYSTEM**

### The particle takes the stress


![Four phrases marked for stress comparing phrasal verbs, where the particle is stressed, with verb plus preposition, where the verb is.](figures/fig_b13_u09_p07_v10.png){width=6.6in}

*The particle takes the stress — This is the opposite of what most learners do, and it is the fastest thing to fix.*


### 7A · Hear 🔊 Track 9.3

> *She marked it* **DOWN** *.*
> **In a phrasal verb the particle carries the stress, not the verb. This is the opposite of
> what most learners do.**

Compare with an ordinary verb plus preposition:

> *She* **LOOKED** *at it.* — here the verb is stressed and *at* is weak.

### 7B · Say

Say both. Then say this one three times, faster:

> *I could not put* **UP** *with it, so I brought it* **UP** *.*

### 7C · Make it mean something

Say these two:

> *She* **TOOK** *over.* *(from somebody — and the handover is the point)*
> *She took* **OVER** *.* *(entirely — and nobody else has a say now)*

**One verb, two takeovers.** Which is which?

### 7D · Fluency 30 ⏺

Thirty seconds: **four phrasal verbs, the stress on the particle each time.**
Three times, faster.

## Part 8 · Writing ◆ **C · THE PERFORMANCE**

### The same paragraph, three ways · 180 words total


![A cutaway of one finding written for a journal, a colleague and a child, with the measured limit kept in all three.](figures/fig_b13_u09_p08_v02.png){width=6.6in}

*One idea, three registers, in section — Four layers, and the same number is in all four.*


### 8A · Read the model

> **For the journal.** Respiration rate increased approximately linearly with soil temperature
> across the sampled range (8–24 °C), with a *Q*₁₀ of 2.1. `[the specialist version — and the
> number is here]`
>
> **For a colleague in another field.** Warmer soil breathes out more carbon dioxide, and over
> the range we measured it roughly doubles for every ten degrees. `[same claim, no notation, and
> the number survives as "doubles"]`
>
> **For a twelve-year-old.** Soil is full of things that are alive, and when it gets warmer they
> get busier and give off more gas. Ten degrees warmer, about twice as busy. `[same number, and
> nothing in it has become false]`
>
> **What did not change.** The range. Nothing above 24 °C was measured, and none of the three
> versions claims anything about it. `[the honest limit, which is the hardest thing to keep]`

**Questions about the writer's choices:**

1. The number appears in all three. **What would the third version be worth without it?**
2. The third version says "things that are alive". **Is that less accurate or less precise?**
3. The fourth paragraph states a limit. **Why is that the hardest part to keep simple?**

### 8B · The anti-model

> It is not unlikely that a reduction in the frequency of sampling would not be without
> consequence for the reliability of the result, and it may therefore be considered that a more
> frequent sampling regime might in certain circumstances be preferable.

Correct English, and it takes three readings. **Count the negatives and the hedges**, and say
how many words the sentence actually needs.

### 8C · Plan

> the specialist version, with the number → the educated non-specialist, same number →
> the twelve-year-old, same number → the limit, kept in all three

### 8D · Write — 180 words total
### 8E · Peer review — three criteria

> ☐ Is the key number identical in all three?
> ☐ Has anything become false on the way to being simpler?
> ☐ Does the limit survive into the simplest version?

### 8F · Write it again · 8G · Publish

Read the third version aloud to somebody outside your subject. **Watch where they stop you.**

## Part 9 · The File ◆ **A · THE WORLD + C · THE PERFORMANCE**

# CULTURE FILE · English as a Lingua Franca


![A transcript of a research meeting in English as a lingua franca, with four misunderstandings marked and their causes.](figures/fig_b13_u09_p09_v05.png){width=6.6in}

*Four minutes, six people, four first languages — Where the breakdowns were, and who repaired them.*


### 9A · Six people, four first languages 🔊 Track 9.4

A research meeting in which nobody is a first-language speaker of English. **Listen.**

1. How many misunderstandings are there in four minutes?
2. **Which one is repaired, and who repairs it?**
3. One speaker uses an idiom and it fails. What do they do next?

### 9B · What the research keeps finding

In meetings where English is everybody's second language, **idioms are the main source of
breakdown**, and the speakers who are most easily understood are not the ones closest to a
first-language model. They are the ones who **avoid idiom, signal structure out loud, and check**.

**Does that match your experience?**

### 9C · The uncomfortable part

If *native-like* is not the target, what is? **Intelligibility to the people in the room** is
one answer, and it has a problem: the room changes. A paper is read by people you will never
meet.

**Where do you personally draw the line?**

### 9D · Your turn

In fours. **Twelve minutes.** Discuss anything, with one rule: **no idioms**. Then: what did you
reach for and have to replace, and was the replacement worse?

### 9E · Transfer

**Count the idioms in the next thing you read for your subject.** How many would you have to
explain to somebody three years into English?

## Part 11 · The Decision ◆ **C · THE PERFORMANCE**

# Four marks, eleven reports, and a criterion that is not on the sheet

The science is correct. The English is unidiomatic and not wrong. Eleven reports have lost marks
this year under a criterion the rubric does not contain. The unit chair has to decide before
marks are released on Friday.

- **Return the marks.** The rubric does not mention language, so marks cannot be taken for it.
  Eleven students go up by between two and five points and nobody is told why.
- **Keep the marks, add the criterion.** Write *expression* into the rubric for next year, with
  a definition, and let this year stand. Honest about the future and not about the past.
- **Return the marks and write the comment.** Give the four back, and give every one of the
  eleven a written note naming exactly what was noticed and why it will matter to a reviewer.

### 11A · The evidence


![A bar chart of marks lost for expression grouped by first language, with the number of cases containing an actual error shown alongside.](figures/fig_b13_u09_p11_v06.png){width=6.6in}

*Marks lost for expression this year — Eleven reports, by the writer's first language and by what was actually marked.*


1. **The rubric and the marked page** from Part 5E — and the bracket that marks a real error.
2. **The chart** — marks lost for expression this year, by first language of the writer.
3. **The voice.** The student, thirty seconds: *"I would rather have the comment than the four
   marks. I am going to write in English for thirty years. Nobody has ever told me what the
   difference is between a sentence that is right and a sentence that is right and reads like
   somebody else's."*

### 11B · Talk about it

In fours. Everybody speaks twice. Use the frame.

> *She got ______ across. She was marked ______ for ______ , which ______ .*

**Words you can use:** *register · idiom · colloquial · jargon · code-switch · fluency ·
penalty · plain English · native-like · the house style*

### 11C · Decide

Write **three sentences**. Choose, say why, and name what it costs.

> I think the unit chair should ______________________ ,
> because ______________________ .
> What this costs is ______________________ .

### 11D · Model decision

> Return the marks and write the comment. The marks are indefensible — there is no criterion —
> and the observation is correct, which means the only honest move is to separate them: give
> back what cannot be justified and give the student what is actually useful, which was never
> the four points. The cost is that the markers will feel overruled on the one part of this
> where they were right, and the chair has to say so out loud when she tells them.

### 11E · The Reveal

> **What happened.** The marks went back. All eleven students received a comment, written by the
> original marker, naming the specific choices and what a reviewer would do with them. Eight of
> the eleven replied; four asked follow-up questions; one asked for a meeting and came with a
> list of nineteen words she had been using that she now wanted checked.
>
> *Expression* went onto the rubric for the following year, with a definition that took four
> drafts and ended up at thirty-one words. In its first year it was used eleven times, and
> every one of the eleven came with a comment, because the definition requires one.
>
> The sentence about soil respiration appeared, unchanged, in a paper two years later. One
> reviewer queried *augments*. The student had the comment, and changed it, and wrote in the
> response letter that it was a preference rather than an error, which the reviewer accepted.

**The four marks were never the useful thing. What the student wanted was the twenty seconds
it took the marker to find the sentence, written down.**

> **Was it the right decision?** Say why — and say what should happen in week one of next
> semester.

## Part 0 · The Big Picture ◆ **A · THE WORLD**


![A campus at dusk with a function room set out with forty chairs and a podium, beside a dark corridor, a logbook and a set of worn keys.](figures/fig_b13_u10_p00_v01.png){width=6.6in}

*Forty chairs, and a corridor at four in the morning — Twelve things to find. Four belong to the occasion. Four belong to the nine years before it.*


# Never have I been so…

**By the end of this unit you can deliver a three-minute formal address.**

| ◆ **A · THE WORLD** | A promotion that nobody asked whether anybody wanted |
|---|---|
| ◆ **B · THE SYSTEM** | inversion after negative adverbials · 24 words for rhetoric and occasion · 21 fronting forms · emphatic stress |
| ◆ **C · THE PERFORMANCE** | Three minutes, standing up, in front of people who know you |

### 0A · Find it

**Find twelve things in this picture.** Four of them belong to the occasion. Four belong to the
nine years before it.

> a room set out with forty chairs · a small podium with a microphone taped to it ·
> a printed programme with one name on it · a table with glasses and nothing else ·
> a card signed by thirty people · a corridor at four in the morning ·
> a set of keys on a ring, worn smooth · a logbook with nine years of entries ·
> a staff photograph from 2016 with one person at the edge ·
> a window showing a sky that is not dark · a lanyard being handed over ·
> a chair at the back, nearest the door

### 0B · Guess it

**Two of the twelve are nine years apart and show the same thing.** Which, and what has changed?

### 0C · Say it ⏺

**Record forty-five seconds.** *Describe a time you were given something you had not asked for.
Say what it was supposed to mean and what it actually meant.* You will record it again at the
end of this unit.

## Part 1 · Vocabulary Lab 1 ◆ **B · THE SYSTEM**


![A network from the occasion and the audience through the address and its cadence to the applause, with what is actually said shown as the one controllable node.](figures/fig_b13_u10_p01_v08.png){width=6.6in}

*What an occasion is made of — Six nodes. The one at the bottom is the only one the speaker controls.*


### 1A · Meet — the words of an occasion

> **address** · **audience** · **rhetoric** · **cadence** · **pause** · **podium** ·
> **applause** · **gravity**

1. Which two of these belong to the room rather than the speaker?
2. **What is *cadence*, and why can you hear it in a speech you cannot understand?**
3. *Rhetoric* has a neutral meaning and an insulting one. Give both.
4. **What exactly does *gravity* mean here, and can a short speech have it?**

> **An *address* is not a speech.** It is a speech given because an occasion requires one,
> which means the occasion is also in the room, and some of what is said is said to it rather
> than to the people.

### 1B · Sort — before, during, or after?

> acknowledgement · on this occasion · promotion · take the floor · occasion · a step up ·
> in all my years · tribute · a few words

**Three columns. Two of them belong in more than one.** Say which and why.

### 1C · Meet — the phrase that does most of the work

> **c** **a few words**

Never a few. It is a request for a short formal speech, and the person asking knows exactly how
long they mean and will not say.

1. **How long is *a few words*, in your experience?**
2. What is the difference between being asked for them in advance and being asked in the room?

### 1D · Collocation Lab

| | |
|---|---|
| **take the floor** | ~ for a moment · she took ~ |
| **on this occasion** | ~ , I would like to · not ~ |
| **in all my years** | ~ I have never · ~ here |
| **a step up** | ~ in pay · ~ , on paper |

**Which two of these can be said without any warmth at all?**

### 1E · The fixed expressions

> **f** **Never have I been so.** — the shape of a hundred toasts, and it only works if what
> follows is unexpected.
> **f** **I did not ask for this.** — eight syllables that can be gratitude, refusal or grief,
> and only the delivery decides.

**Say the second one three ways. How many did the room hear differently?**

### 1F · Own it

The four phrasal verbs of taking a job or not.

> **stand down** · **take on** · **turn down** · **rise to**

1. Which two are about leaving, and which two about accepting?
2. **What is the difference between *taking on* a role and *being given* one?**
3. *Rise to* what — the occasion, the challenge, or the bait?
4. Write one sentence in which somebody **turns down** something and is not believed.

**Before you listen.** Josie has worked nights for nine years. She is being moved to days, with
more money and a title. **Guess: has anybody asked her?**

| | The night job | The day job |
|---|---|---|
| hours | | |
| pay | | |
| people she works with | | |
| what she decides herself | | |

1. The move is **a restructure / a promotion / both**.
2. Josie was told **before / after / at the same time as** the announcement.

**Noticing.** Four sentences from the recording.

2. *Not until the email went out did anybody ask me.*
3. *No sooner had the restructure been signed off than my name was on it.*
4. *At no point was there a conversation.*

**All four begin with a negative and invert the verb. Which one would be unremarkable if you
put it back in order?**

Thirty seconds of Josie. Four jobs.

1. How many inverted sentences can you count?
2. In *never have I been so*, which word is loudest?

## Part 3 · Grammar Lab 1 ◆ **B · THE SYSTEM**

### Putting the negative first, and paying for it

### 3A · Notice

Look at the four sentences in Part 2.

1. In sentence 1, where would *I have* be in ordinary order?
2. **In sentence 2, what is the auxiliary, and where did it come from?**
3. **Rewrite sentence 3 in ordinary order.** How many words change position?
4. Sentence 4 inverts a *be* verb. **Is that the same operation?**
5. Try *"Never I have been so clear."* **What is missing?**

### 3B · See


![Two sentence diagrams comparing I have never been so clear with never have I been so clear, showing the auxiliary moving in front of the subject.](figures/fig_b13_u10_p03_v09.png){width=6.6in}

*The question operation, used for emphasis — Front the negative and the sentence inverts exactly as a question does.*


### 3C · Know

| Fronted element | Example | What moves |
|---|---|---|
| ***never · rarely · seldom*** | *Never have I been so sure.* | auxiliary before subject |
| ***hardly · scarcely · no sooner*** | *No sooner had it been signed than…* | and *than*, not *when* |
| ***not until · at no point*** | *Not until Friday did anybody ask.* | *do / did* appears if there is no auxiliary |
| ***little*** | *Little did we know.* | fixed, and almost always ironic |
| ***only then · only when*** | *Only then did she answer.* | the inversion comes after the whole clause |
| ***nor · neither*** | *Nor was there any warning.* | joins to a previous negative |

> **The operation is the question operation.** *I have never been* → *Have I been?* → *Never
> have I been*. If there is no auxiliary, English supplies *do*, exactly as in a question:
> *Nobody asked* → *Not until Friday did anybody ask*.

> ***no sooner* takes *than*, not *when*.** *No sooner had it been signed than my name was on
> it.* This is the single most common error with this structure.

> **Inversion is formal and it is emphatic, and it costs something.** Used twice in a speech it
> is powerful. Used six times it is a parody of a speech, and the audience will hear the
> structure instead of the content.

> **Why it matters.** This is the grammar of occasions: toasts, tributes, resignations,
> verdicts. You will not need it often and when you need it nothing else will do.

### 3D · Use — controlled

> (1) __________ have I been so clear about what I want and so unable to say it.
> (2) __________ until the email went out __________ anybody ask me. (3) __________ sooner had
> the restructure been signed off (4) __________ my name was on it, in a list, between two
> people I have never met. (5) __________ no point was there a conversation.
> (6) __________ did I imagine, nine years ago, that the thing I would have to defend would be
> the shift nobody else wants. And (7) __________ then, standing in a room with forty chairs in
> it, did I understand that the promotion was not for me.

### 3E · Use — guided

Invert each one. **Two of them need *do* or *did*.**

1. I have never been so sure.
2. Nobody asked until Friday.
3. It had no sooner been signed than my name was on it.
4. She answered only then.
5. We little knew what it would mean.

### 3F · Use — open

**In pairs.** Give a sixty-second tribute to something ordinary — a bus route, a kettle, a shift
pattern — using **exactly two** inversions. **Your partner's job** is to say whether a third
would have helped.

### 3G · Error autopsy


![Two panels comparing no sooner had it been signed when my name was on it with the same sentence using than.](figures/fig_b13_u10_p03_v11.png){width=6.6in}

*No sooner, and the conjunction it borrowed — No sooner, and the conjunction it borrowed*


**Read both versions. Then say which word the first one borrowed from a different structure.**

### 3H · Check — eight items

> 1. ______ have I been so sure. *(negative adverb)*
> 2. ______ until Friday ______ anybody ask. *(no auxiliary present)*
> 3. No sooner had it been signed ______ my name was on it. *(correct conjunction)*
> 4. ______ then did she answer. *(after a clause)*
> 5. ______ did we know. *(fixed, ironic)*
> 6. ✗ *Never I have been so clear.* → correct it.
> 7. ______ was there any warning. *(joining a previous negative)*
> 8. ______ no circumstances will I be moved. *(prepositional phrase)*

**0–4 →** Workbook pp. 42–45. **5–6 →** Part 6. **7–8 →** page 217.

## Part 4 · Speaking ◆ **C · THE PERFORMANCE**

### 4A · Fluency starter — front it

One learner gives an ordinary sentence with a negative in it. The next fronts the negative and
inverts. **The chain breaks on a missing auxiliary.**

### 4B · The functional core — a formal address


![Two panels compared: a written address with five inversions and the delivered version with two, and the sentences cut in the room.](figures/fig_b13_u10_p04_v04.png){width=6.6in}

*The speech as written, and the speech as given — Learner A has the draft. Learner B has the recording.*


**Language Bank**

| | |
|---|---|
| **the opening** | *On this occasion…* · *I have been asked to say a few words.* |
| **the acknowledgement** | *In all my years here…* · *Not once has anybody…* |
| **the turn** | *Never have I…* · *No sooner had… than…* · *Only then did I…* |
| **the landing** | one short sentence, in ordinary word order, and stop |

**Information gap.** Learner A has the speech as written. Learner B has what was actually said.
**Find the two sentences that were cut in the room, and say why.**

### 4C · The performance — three minutes ⏺

Deliver **a formal address**. **Three minutes, standing.** You must include:

> an opening that names the occasion · an acknowledgement of somebody specific ·
> exactly two inversions · a landing of one short sentence in ordinary order

**Record it.**

### 4D · Observer

One person listens for **one thing**: did the structure start to be more audible than the
content?

### 4E · Three criteria

> ☐ Did I use exactly two inversions, and were they in the right places?
> ☐ Is the acknowledgement about a specific person doing a specific thing?
> ☐ Is the last sentence short and in ordinary order?

## Part 5 · Reading ◆ **A · THE WORLD**

### 5A · Predict

An interview transcript, titled **"Nine years of nights."**
**What do you expect her to say she will miss?** Two sentences.

### 5B · Read — two minutes and a quarter

---

**NINE YEARS OF NIGHTS**
*interview transcript, lightly edited*

**Q: You have been offered a day role with a promotion. You have not accepted it.**

I have not turned it down either. I want to be careful about that, because the version going
round is that I am being difficult, and I am not being difficult. I have asked two questions and
neither has been answered.

**Q: What are the two questions?**

What happens to the building between eleven at night and six in the morning, and who does it.
That is one question really. The other is why nobody asked me before my name went on a list.

**Q: Tell me about the nights.**

There are nine of us across the week and I have done four of them for nine years. In that time
I have let three hundred and sixty people into buildings they were locked out of, most of them
at a point in the evening where they had decided the whole degree was a mistake. You do not
read about that in a restructure document. It is not a duty. It is what happens when the person
who has the keys is also the only person awake.

**Q: What will the day role involve?**

Supervision. A team of four. More money, which I am not going to pretend does not matter, and a
title. It is a step up. Everybody has said *a step up* to me eleven times in a fortnight and
nobody has said what is at the top of the step.

**Q: Would you have said yes if they had asked first?**

Probably. That is the part people find hard to believe. If somebody had come to me in March and
said we need you on days, here is why, here is what happens to the nights — I would almost
certainly have said yes, and I would have helped them work out the handover.

Not until the email went out did anybody ask me. By then I was in a list, and once you are in a
list you are either grateful or you are difficult. There is no third thing you are allowed to be.

**Q: Is there anything you want to say to the people organising the event?**

Move the chairs. Forty chairs in rows is a thing you do to an audience. We are nine people and a
kettle. Nobody who works nights wants to be looked at.

---

### 5C · Comprehension — eight questions

1. What has she not done, and what is she careful to distinguish it from?
2. **What are her two questions, and why does she say they are "really one"?**
3. How many night staff are there, and how many nights does she work?
4. **What is the three hundred and sixty, and why does she say it is "not a duty"?**
5. What does the day role involve? Give four things.
6. **What has been said eleven times in a fortnight, and what has not been said?**
7. Would she have said yes, and under what condition?
8. What does she ask the organisers to do, and what is her reason?

### 5D · Vocabulary in context

Guess from the text. Then check.

> the version going round · a restructure document · at the top of the step ·
> once you are in a list · a thing you do to an audience

### 5E · The counter-text


![A restructure paper paragraph on night cover beside a week of night log entries sorted by category, with one line appearing in both.](figures/fig_b13_u10_p05_v05.png){width=6.6in}

*The restructure paper and the night log — One week of entries, set against the paragraph that describes the job.*


**Read the restructure paper and the night log, and answer.**

1. What does the restructure paper say about night cover?
2. **How many entries are in the night log for one week, and what are they?**
3. What is the proposed night establishment after the change?
4. What does the paper say about consultation, and with whom?
5. One line of the log appears in the restructure paper, reworded. Which?

### 5F · Two texts

The paper says night cover will be **maintained through a revised rota**.
The log shows **two hundred and nine entries in one week**, of which most are not security.

**Does the paper describe the job?** Three sentences.

## Part 6 · Vocabulary Lab 2 ◆ **B · THE SYSTEM**

### What can go first, and what has to follow it

### 6A · Sort — which take *do / did*?

Three columns: **needs *do / did* · uses an existing auxiliary · uses *be***.

> scarcely · no sooner · nor · little · only · neither · never · hardly · rarely ·
> nowhere · seldom

### 6B · Chunk completion

1. **not ______** the email went out
2. **at ______ point** was there a conversation
3. **under no ______** will I be moved
4. **only ______** did I understand it
5. **no sooner ______** it been signed
6. **little did we ______**

### 6C · Two that are not negative at all

**only · not until**

> Neither contains a negative word, and both invert. *Only then did she answer.* *Not until
> Friday did anybody ask.* **What they share is that they restrict**, and restriction is what
> triggers the inversion, not negation.

**Write two sentences about the interview in Part 5, one with each.**

### 6D · Three phrasal verbs for a career

> **p** **set out** · **p** **look back** · **p** **rise to**

> *When I set out, nine years ago…* · *Looking back, the nights were the job.* ·
> *She rose to it, and nobody noticed that she had.*

**Write one sentence with each, in a tribute to somebody you know.**

### 6E · The fixed expressions

> **f** **Not for one moment.** — a whole-sentence denial, and it inverts whatever follows it.
> **c** **not once** — *Not once has anybody asked.*

> **Use both in the same short paragraph.**

### 6F · Contrast Clinic

Eight items. **Four are wrong. Correct them and say what the rule is.**

1. Only then she answered.
2. Nor was there any warning.
3. Not until Friday anybody asked.
4. No sooner had it been signed when my name was on it.
5. Never I have been so sure.
6. Not until Friday did anybody ask.
7. Never have I been so sure.
8. No sooner had it been signed than my name was on it.

## Part 7 · Pronunciation & Fluency Lab ◆ **B · THE SYSTEM**

### Emphatic stress, and the pause before it


![Three pitch contours comparing never have I been so clear with and without a pause after the fronted word.](figures/fig_b13_u10_p07_v10.png){width=6.6in}

*The fronted word, and the pause after it — Three deliveries. The grammar is identical in all three.*


### 7A · Hear 🔊 Track 10.3

> **NEVER** | *have I been so clear.*
> **The fronted word takes the stress, and then there is a pause, and the pause is the whole
> effect.**

Now without the pause:

> *Never have I been so clear.*
> **Grammatically identical. It has become a sentence rather than a moment.**

### 7B · Say

Say both. Then say this one three times, with the pause in a different place each time:

> *No sooner had the restructure been signed off than my name was on it.*

### 7C · Make it mean something

Say these two:

> *I did not ask for* **THIS** *.* *(I asked for something else)*
> *I did not* **ASK** *for this.* *(it was given to me, and I had no part in it)*

**One sentence, two complaints.** Which is which?

### 7D · Fluency 30 ⏺

Thirty seconds: **two inversions, each with a pause after the fronted element, and nothing else
inverted.** Three times. **Count how many you want to add, and do not.**

## Part 8 · Writing ◆ **C · THE PERFORMANCE**

### An address · 200 words


![A cutaway of a formal address in four layers from the opening and the acknowledgement to the turn and the landing.](figures/fig_b13_u10_p08_v02.png){width=6.6in}

*A formal address, in section — Four layers. There are exactly two inversions and both are in the middle two.*


### 8A · Read the model

> I have been asked to say a few words, and I have been told that a few words means four
> minutes, which is the first honest thing anybody has said to me about this event.
> `[the opening — it names the occasion and does one small true thing]`
>
> In all my years here, the part of this job I would defend is not on any document. Between
> eleven and six, three hundred and sixty people have been let back into buildings they were
> locked out of. Not once has that appeared in a restructure paper.
> `[the acknowledgement, with a number — and the first inversion]`
>
> No sooner had the paper been signed than my name was on a list, and I want to say clearly that
> I would probably have said yes. `[the turn, the second inversion, and it concedes]`
>
> Move the chairs. We are nine people and a kettle. `[the landing — ordinary word order, two
> short sentences, and it is an instruction]`

**Questions about the writer's choices:**

1. The opening does "one small true thing". **Why does an address need one?**
2. There are exactly two inversions. **Where would a third have gone, and what would it cost?**
3. The last two sentences are ordinary. **Why not end on an inversion?**

### 8B · The anti-model

> Never before have I been so honoured, and seldom have I felt such gratitude. Not only has this
> institution given me a career, but rarely does one find such colleagues. Little did I imagine,
> when I started, that never would I wish to be anywhere else.

Grammatically perfect. **Count the inversions**, and say at which one you stopped listening to
the content.

### 8C · Plan

> the opening, naming the occasion and doing one true thing → the acknowledgement, with a number
> → the turn, with the second inversion → the landing, ordinary and short

### 8D · Write — 200 words
### 8E · Peer review — three criteria

> ☐ Exactly two inversions?
> ☐ Is there a number in the acknowledgement?
> ☐ Is the last sentence in ordinary word order?

### 8F · Write it again · 8G · Publish

**Stand up and deliver it.** That is the only version of this task that counts.

## Part 9 · The File ◆ **A · THE WORLD + C · THE PERFORMANCE**

# WORK FILE · The Meeting


![A record of a fifty-minute meeting with four agenda items annotated as decide, announce, consult or be seen to consult.](figures/fig_b13_u10_p09_v05.png){width=6.6in}

*Fifty minutes, four decisions, one of them real — A restructure meeting, with what each item actually was.*


### 9A · Fifty minutes, four decisions 🔊 Track 10.4

A restructure meeting. **Listen.**

1. How many of the four decisions were made in the room?
2. **Which one was already made, and how can you tell?**
3. One person speaks for eleven seconds and changes an outcome. What do they say?

### 9B · What a meeting is for

> **to decide** — and the decision is not made before it
> **to announce** — and the decision was
> **to consult** — and the decision can still move
> **to be seen to consult** — and it cannot

**Which of the four is hardest to tell apart from which, and who can tell?**

### 9C · The question that reveals it

> *"What would have to be true for this to change?"*

If there is an answer, it is a consultation. If the answer is a pause and then a restatement of
the benefits, it is not.

**Where would you have asked it in the meeting in 9A?**

### 9D · Your turn

In fours. **Twelve minutes.** One person chairs a meeting about something real on your campus.
**Everybody else must work out, without asking directly, which of the four kinds it is.**

### 9E · Transfer

**In the next meeting you attend, write down at the start which of the four you think it is.**
Check at the end. Were you right?

Half the class sees the restructure paper. Half sees the night log.

## Part 11 · The Decision ◆ **C · THE PERFORMANCE**

# A promotion nobody asked about

Josie has worked four nights a week for nine years. She has been named in a restructure paper as
moving to a day supervisory role, with more money and a title. The paper is signed. The event is
booked for Friday. She has asked two questions and neither has been answered.

- **Take it.** More money, a team, and the night rota will be covered by somebody. Nine years is
  a long time on nights and nobody stays for ever.
- **Turn it down.** Stay on nights, decline the title, and accept that she will be the person
  who said no to a promotion, which is a sentence that follows people.
- **Answer the two questions first.** Refuse to decide until somebody puts in writing what
  happens to the building between eleven and six, and who does it.

### 11A · The evidence


![A bar chart of one week of night log entries by category, split into those in the job description and those not.](figures/fig_b13_u10_p11_v06.png){width=6.6in}

*What the night log actually records — One week, 209 entries, sorted by whether anybody is paid to do it.*


1. **The restructure paper and the night log** from Part 5E — and the line that appears in both.
2. **The chart** — what the night log actually records, by category, across one week.
3. **The voice.** Josie, thirty seconds: *"Three hundred and sixty people. I am not saying that
   to be thanked for it. I am saying it because it is going to stop happening in March and
   nobody has written it down anywhere, and in two years somebody will ask why the complaints
   went up and there will be no document to find."*

### 11B · Talk about it

In fours. Everybody speaks twice. Use the frame.

> *Never ______ . Not until ______ did ______ . Only then ______ .*

**Words you can use:** *address · audience · occasion · tribute · acknowledgement · promotion ·
gravity · a step up · take the floor · in all my years*

### 11C · Decide

Write **three sentences**. Choose, say why, and name what it costs.

> I think Josie should ______________________ ,
> because ______________________ .
> What this costs is ______________________ .

### 11D · Model decision

> Answer the two questions first, in writing, and then take it. The questions are not a
> negotiating position and they are not reluctance; they are the only part of nine years that
> cannot be handed over in a conversation, and once she has moved to days nobody will ever ask
> them again. The cost is a fortnight of being called difficult by people who have not read the
> log, and a fortnight is cheap for a document that will still exist in two years.

### 11E · The Reveal

> **What happened.** She asked for the two answers in writing. It took eleven days. The written
> answer said night cover would be provided by a contracted service from March, which was true,
> and did not mention lockouts, because lockouts are not in the contract.
>
> She took the job. In her four minutes on the Friday she read out the number — three hundred
> and sixty — and said that from March there would be a telephone number instead, and that the
> telephone number was fine, and that somebody should write down what it did not do. The room
> was quiet and then clapped for slightly too long.
>
> Lockout callouts went from an average of seven a week to nineteen in the first semester under
> the contract, because a contracted callout has to be logged and a person with keys does not.
> The nineteen is the first number anybody has ever had. It is in a report now, and the report
> exists because of four minutes in a room with forty chairs in it.

**She was right that it would stop happening and wrong about why it mattered. The thing that
survived was not the service. It was the number.**

> **Was it the right decision?** Say why — and say what should be in the contract.

**Three columns, from memory:** fronted elements needing *do / did* · those using an existing
auxiliary · those using *be*.

### 12C · Can-do, with evidence

> ☐ I can deliver a three-minute formal address. **Evidence:** 4C, 8D
> ☐ I can invert correctly after negative and restrictive adverbials. **Evidence:** 3H, 6F

### 12E · Glossary — forty-five items, as a map

> **THE OCCASION** · address · audience · occasion · podium · applause · gravity · tribute · acknowledgement
> **THE SPEAKING** · rhetoric · cadence · pause · take the floor · a few words · on this occasion · in all my years
> **THE JOB** · promotion · a step up · the night shift · stand down · take on · turn down · rise to · set out · look back
> **WHAT PEOPLE SAY** · never have I been so · I did not ask for this · not for one moment
> **FRONTING** · never · rarely · seldom · little · hardly · scarcely · nowhere · nor · neither · only · no sooner

# Answer Key
